ii 
 
 
 
PREFACE 
 
 I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on 
Health and Family Welfare, have been authorized by the Committee to present the Report on 
its behalf, hereby present this 173rd Report of the Committee on the Demands for Grants for 
the year 2026-27 (Demand No. 47) of the Department of Health Research, Ministry of Health 
and Family Welfare. 
 
2.  The Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committees are entrusted with the 
constitutional role of ensuring accountability of executive to the Parliament.  In this regard, 
Rule 270 (a) of Rules & Procedure and Conduct & Business in the Council of States (Rajya 
Sabha) empowered the Department-related Standing Committees to consider the Demands for 
Grants of the related Departments/Ministries with the objective of ensuring financial 
accountability of the executive and report thereon. In exercise of its constitutional mandate, 
the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health & Family Welfare 
scrutinised the Demands for Grants for the year 2026-27 (Demand No. 47) of the Department 
of Health Research, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. The Committee, in its meeting, 
held on 23rd February, 2026 heard the Secretary, Department of Health Research and other 
Officers on Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Department. 
 
3.  The Committee while making its observations/recommendations has mainly relied 
upon the following documents:– 
(i) Detailed Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Department of Health Research; 
(ii) Annual Report of the Department for the year 2025-26; 
(iii) Detailed Explanatory Note on Demands for Grants (2026-27) of the Department 
of Health Research; 
(iv) Output-Outcome framework 2026-27 for scheme of the Department of Health 
Research; 
(v) Projection of outlays for the schemes by the Department during the Financial 
Year 2026-27; 
(vi) Written replies furnished by the Department to the Questionnaires sent to them 
by the Secretariat; and 
(vii) Presentation made by the Secretary (Health Research) and other officers 
concerned, etc. 
 
4. The Committee, in its meeting held on 18th March, 2026, considered the draft report 
and adopted the same. 
 
5. For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the 
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.

1 
 
CHAPTER I 
 
INTRODUCTION 
 
 
Department of Health Research (DHR) 
 
1.1 The history of the Department of Health Research (DHR) traces back to 1911, when 
the Indian Research Fund Association (IRFA) was established to sponsor and coordinate 
medical research across the country (IRFA was later renamed the Indian Council of Medical 
Research in 1949).  In its present form, the DHR was created as a separate department under 
the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare on September 17, 2007, through an amendment to 
the Government of India (Allocation of Business) Rules,1961. The department was formally 
launched on October 5, 2007, by the Minister for Science & Technology and Earth Sciences 
during a ceremony presided over by the Minister for Health & Family Welfare. However, the 
DHR became fully operational only in November, 2008, following the appointment of its 
first Secretary, who also served as the Director General of the ICMR. 
 
1.2 Vision and Mission of DHR 
 
1.2.1 Vision of DHR: to bring modern health technologies to the people through research 
and innovations related to diagnosis, treatment methods and vaccines for prevention; to 
translate them into products and processes and, in synergy with concerned organizations, 
introduce these innovations into public health system. 
 
1.2.2 Mission of DHR:  
1. Promotion and co-ordination of basic, applied and clinical research including clinical 
trials and operational research in areas related to medical, health, biomedical and 
medical profession and education through development of infrastructure, manpower 
and skills in cutting edge areas and management of related information thereto. 
2. Promote and provide guidance on research governance issues, including ethical issues 
in medical and health research. 
3. Inter-sectoral coordination and promotion of public- private – partnership in medical, 
biomedical and health research related areas. 
4. Advanced training in research areas concerning medicine and health, including grant 
of fellowships for such training in India and abroad. 
5. International co-operation in medical and health research, including work related to 
international conference in related areas in India and abroad. 
6. Technical support for dealing with epidemics and natural calamities. 
7. Investigation of outbreaks due to new and exotic agents and development of tools for 
prevention. 
8. Facilitating scientific societies and associations, charitable and religious endowments 
in health research areas. 

2 
 
9. Coordination between organization and institutes under the Central and State 
Governments in areas related to the subjects entrusted to the Department and for the 
promotion of special studies in medicine and health. 
10. Administering and monitoring of Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR). 
11. Health Technology Assessment 
 
 
1.2.3 The Rules/Regulations/Guidelines where the Department has a nodal/administrative 
role:- 
 
1. Clinical Trial Registry- Registration of all regulatory clinical trials is mandatory as 
per the drug licensing authority in India i.e. the Drugs Controller General (India) 
(DCGI). In addition, several journal editors, ethics committees and medical colleges 
have made it mandatory to register clinical studies in the CTRI. 
 
2. Bioethics -Chapter IV of New Drug Clinical Trial Rules 2019 under Drugs and 
Cosmetic Act 1940, came into effect from 14th September 2019, according to rule 15 
of NDCT 2019, Any institution or organisation which intends to conduct biomedical 
and health research shall be required to have an Ethics Committee to review and 
oversee the conduct of such research as detailed in National Ethical Guidelines for 
Biomedical and Health Research Involving Human Participants. 
 
3. ART/Surrogacy Registry- It acts as Central Database of all the ART Clinics, Banks 
and Surrogacy Clinics in the country through which the details of all the clinics and 
banks of the country including nature and types of services provided by them, 
outcome of the services and other relevant information shall be obtained on regular 
basis. The data generated from the National Registry shall be utilized by the National 
Board for making policies, guidelines and shall help in identifying new research areas 
and conducting research in the area of assisted reproduction, surrogacy and other 
related fields in the country. 
 
1.2.4 The Committee enquired whether any post-legislative impact assessment of the 
Rules/Regulations/Guidelines for which the Department has a nodal/administrative role has 
been carried out. DHR informed that no specific post-legislative impact assessment has been 
c 
DHR established in 2007 Promoting, coordinating and translating research into action for improving the health of population.         e       * 1,5, 6 & 7 are largely implemented through ICMR 11 Health Technology Assessment 

3 
 
undertaken. However, DHR added that in view of large no. of cases in the country, holistic 
view is being taken for amending the Acts. 
 
1.2.5 The Committee understands that DHR has nodal/administrative role regarding 
various Rules/Regulations/Guidelines, including the Acts like Assisted Reproductive 
Technology (Regulatory) Act 2021 and Surrogacy (Regulatory)Act 2021.The 
Committee also notes that DHR has not undertaken any post-legislative impact 
assessment of the Acts/Rules/Regulations/Guidelines, as on date. But, the Department, 
taking into consideration the large no. of cases in the country, a holistic view is being 
taken for amending the Acts. The Committee lauds the view of the Department and 
recommends that necessary steps maybe taken towards specific post-legislative impact 
assessment and bring in appropriate amendments to the 
Acts/Rules/Regulations/Guidelines keeping in mind the requirements of the changing 
time on account of scientific, technological  and medical advancements. 
 
1.3 Opportunities, Strength and  Challenges of DHR as an organization 
 
1.3.1 According to the Department, the SWOT analysis of the functioning of DHR along 
with its opportunities and strength to achieve the vision, mission and goals of the department 
in the stipulated time frame are as mentioned below. 
1.  Strengths- 
a. Strong Institutional Framework: DHR is a core department under the Ministry 
of Health & Family Welfare with a clear mandate to advance health research, 
innovation, diagnostics, vaccines, and evidence-based guidelines. 
b. Nationally Recognized Schemes & Networks: Established schemes such as 
Model Rural Health Research Units, DIAMONDS, HTAIn, grant‑in‑aid 
programs for innovative health research. 
 
c. Nationwide infrastructure: Backed by 28 institutes of ICMR and network of 
MRUs/MRHRUs, VRDLs, etc across the country. 
2.  Weaknesses-  
a. Lack of effective mechanism for partnership with industry and private sector- 
owing to which many research leads do not get translated into products for 
public use 
 
b. Low budget-This is inadequate (at 0.02% of GDP) compared to the health 
research budget in developed countries and in the context  ofcountry’s needs 
 
c. Limited Human Resource Capacity: Need for more skilled researchers, faculty 
training, and sustained HRD efforts, as highlighted by the need for specialized 
skill‑building schemes. 
 
 
 
 

4 
 
3.  Opportunities –  
With its 5-years strategic action plan, DHR prioritizing affordable health 
technologies, India can reduce import dependency in diagnostics, vaccines, other 
medical technologies and products. 
a. Through its strong One Health institutional framework, coordination 
mechanism andexpertise as well as effective biosecurity infrastructure of NIV 
Labs and VRDLs, DHR can play an important role in Pandemic Preparedness. 
DHRcan leverage, strengthen and integrate existing R&D and manufacturing 
capabilities of the country to bolster India’s pandemic preparedness and 
response against disease “X”.  
 
b. The proposed Section 8 company of DHR- MIDAC will provide 
comprehensive support for converting research leads/ innovationsrelated to 
national health priorities into approved products through public-private 
partnerships (PPP)mode. 
c. The development of evidence-based guidelines by DHR will be helpful in 
enhancing healthcare quality, optimizing treatment strategies, and ensuring the 
efficient use of resources. It will also bring in the uniformity and scientific 
rigor in clinical practice. 
d. The capacities of DIAMOnDSCentres can be enhanced to conduct genome 
sequencing for cancer diagnosis through biomarker analysis, contributing to 
precision medicine. 
e. The expansion of HTAIn through a separate scheme would catalyse the aim of 
maximizing health in the population, reducing out of pocket expenditure 
(OOP) and reducing inequity. 
 
f. Growing Global Recognition and Collaboration: As a national research hub 
with rapidly growing capabilities, DHR is well‑placed to deepen South–South 
collaborations partnerships among developing nations in Asia, Africa, and by 
sharing affordable and scalable health technologies aligned with the Action 
Plan’s emphasis on indigenous solutions. 
 
4. Threats 
a. Emerging Health Challenges: Rapid evolution of AMR, NCDs, 
climate‑related illnesses, and new pathogens pose threats if research 
advancements lag. 
 
b. Ad-hoc staff: A significant proportion of the research workforce consists of 
project‑based staff, employed on short‑term contracts. This result in high 
turnover, which disrupts research continuity, weakens institutional memory, 
and increases costs due to repeated recruitment and retraining. 
 
1.3.2 The Department also informed about key concern areas related to its functioning and  
the strategy can be adopted to overcome those problems. Those beingAd‑hoc/Project-Based  
Staffing Challenges: A considerable segment of the research workforce is engaged on 
short‑term, project‑linked appointments, leading to instability. This heavy reliance on 
temporary staff results in:  

5 
 
 
(i) Frequent churn and loss of trained personnel 
(ii)    Disruptions in ongoing research  
(iii)  Higher administrative and training overheads. This structural dependence on 
short-term roles mirrors wider global public‑health staffing issues, where 
integrating temporary workers into permanent institutional frameworks remains 
difficult and adds operational burdens. 
1.3.3 The Committee finds that DHR considers Ad‑hoc /Project-Based Staffing to be 
one of its biggest challenges. According to the Department this results in high turnover, 
which disrupts research continuity, weakens institutional memory, and increases costs 
due to repeated recruitment and retraining. The Committee endorses the view of DHR 
that the contractual staff, either project-based or as part of Secretariat weakens 
institutional memory due to frequent change and loss of trained personnel causing  
disruption in ongoing research, besides having  higher administrative training and skill 
development overhead. It is of the considered opinion of the Committee that permanent 
work force is an asset to any organization, especially, in offices like DHR and ICMR 
where institutional memory and research continuity is a requirement for a long term 
result oriented research. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Department 
shall continue its efforts to acquire permanent workforce both for DHR Secretariat as 
well as scientific research activities.  
1.4 Human Resource Management of DHR  
 
1.4.1 Taking note of the submission of DHR regarding Ad‑hoc /Project-Based staffing, the 
Committee sought to know if there was any approved plan or timeline to fill the posts under 
the Department permanent basis. In regard, the Committee also wanted to know the different 
modes of recruitment to fill up posts that were vacant and scientists engaged on contractual/ 
consultant/ ad-hoc basis in DHR and its subordinate organisations/schemes.

distributed among three divisions namely- DHR Secretariat, HTAin Attached Office and 
Ethics Committee. The distribution and vacancy position of these posts is as follows:

1.4.4 The Committee also enquired about the efforts undertaken by DHR to fill up the posts 
during the last 5 years. The reply of DHR is as under: 
 
The Department has been making regular efforts for filling up the posts of Scientist 
C, D and E in DHR Secretariat on Deputation (ISTC) basis and has advertised the posts five 
times over the period of last five years since 2021. The details regarding action taken by 
DHR to fill the posts from 2021 till date is as follows: 
 
(i) Vacancy circular for posts of Scientist C, D and E (2 post each) was issued for the 
first time in October 2021. After receiving applications for Scientists C, D and E, the 
proposal for appointment to the post of Scientist C, D & E in DHR on deputation 
(Including Short Term Contract) basis was submitted to the Appointments branch, UPSC 
under the single window system on 04.05.2022. Vide their letter no.3/40(6)/2022-ADT-1 
dated 23.08.2022, UPSC recommended Dr. M. Kalaivani, Senior Scientific Officer, Indian 
Pharmacopoeia Commission, Ghaziabad for appointment to the post Scientist D in DHR 
on Deputation (including short term contract) basis. Offer of appointment was issued to 
the recommended candidate, however, the recommended candidate did not join and all 
posts of Scientists remained vacant. 
 
(ii)  The vacancy were advertised for second time by vacancy circular dated 
16.02.2023. The last date for submission of application for the above referred circular was 
18.04.2023 which was extended further for 60 days i.e. to 17.06.2023. 
Accordingly, circular dated 17.04.2023 extending the date of submission to 17.06.2023 
was issued. The last date was once again extended for a second time upto 17.07.2023. In 
the meanwhile, DoPT issued the notification dated 13.07.2023 wherein post upto level 

7 
 
13A to be filled by Deputation or Deputation (ISTC) were exempted from UPSC 
consultation. Further, these posts were revived for second time as per revival of the 
post order of Department of Expenditure dated 15.02.2024. The applications received for 
the post were shortlisted and personal talk held with the eligible candidates on 27.12.2023. 
The Selection Committee recommended 02 candidates for the post of Scientist C and 01 
candidate for the post of Scientist D. These selected candidates joined DHR in March 
2024, however, incumbent for the post of Scientist D resigned in June 2024 due to 
personal reasons and both posts of Scientist D & E once again remained vacant. 
 
(iii) Upon repatriation of Scientist D in June 2024, vacancy circular dated 23.08.2024 
was issued for the post of Scientist D & E, with closing date of application being 24 
October 2024. However, as only 1 application was received through proper channel, the 
last date for submission of application was extended by 1 month till 25 November 2024. 
In November 2024, the last date was once again extended till 09 January 2025. 2 
applicants were eligible for post of Scientist E and 1 for Scientist D. However, only one 
applicant joined the post of Scientist E. 
 
(iv) Vacancy for post of Scientist D & E was advertised for fourth time vide circular 
dated 14.08.2025 with last date of application as 30.09.2025. The last date was further 
extended twice to 31.10.2025 and again to 15.11.2025. However, only one eligible 
applicant was found during this period. Upon interview by the Selection Committee, the 
applicant was recommended for selection as Scientist D and joined DHR on 16.01.2026. 
 
(v) Vacancy for post of Scientist C, D and E (1 post each) is currently being 
advertised for fifth time w.e.f. 03.01.2026. The last date for applying for the posts was 
02.02.2026.  
 
1.4.5 Further, it has also been submitted that for HTAIn, Department of Expenditure had 
approved 12 Scientific posts (Scientist-F-2, Scientist-E-2, Scientist-D-4, Scientist-C-4). The 
posts were advertised as per the recruitment rules on deputation basis on the 25thJanuary 
2025. A total of 24 applications were received (Scientist-11, scientist-D-6 and Scientist-E-7). 
After screening by a committee, no candidate was found to be eligible. In continuation, 
recruitment rules have been re-drafted for Direct Recruitment also. The recruitment rules will 
be uploaded in UPSC portal for approval. The Recruitment Rules for the post of Scientists in 
the Ethics committee attached office are yet to be formed. These posts are currently vacant. 
 
1.4.6 When the Committee wanted to know when the last Cadre Review of scientific posts 
in DHR was conducted/scheduled and whether the issue of vacancies and creation of a 
permanent scientific cadre be specifically addressed in it, DHR submitted that despite 
advertising the posts of Scientists multiple times, the posts have remained vacant as less 
number of eligible applicants have applied for these posts. Accordingly, a committee has 
been formed in DHR to review the Recruitment Rules of Scientists in November 2025. The 
review of the RRs by the committee is under process. 
1.4.7 Based on the submission of the Department, the Committee feels that 
contractual (or project-based) appointments are the new norm. The Committee is not in 
agreement with such ad-hoc or contractual arrangement. The Committee reckons the 

8 
 
practical point of view that for time-bound specific scheme contractual appointments 
may contain operational merits and should have cost effectiveness. However, the 
Committee expresses concern to observe that the contractual scientists are now 
outnumbering the permanent/deputation staff. The Committee is in favour of 
permanent staff in DHR secretariat and permanent scientist for continuity in research 
activities.   
1.4.8 In the view of the Committee, a permanent scientific workforce in research 
offers significant merits for an entity like DHR. Wherein institutional knowledge, 
sustained progress in mission-oriented, complex, long-term research are of critical 
importance in general. Further, job security encourages researchers to pursue novel 
and high-impact ideas which should curtail migration of brighter minds to abroad due 
to lack of stability and career progression. The Committee acknowledges that 
permanent positions involve upfront costs and less flexibility for the Government but in 
research, where breakthroughs often require patience and accumulated wisdom, the 
long-term benefits of sustained scientific advancement cannot be overlooked. Hence, the 
Committee recommends a sustainable approach on the issue like hybrid model of 
appointments including permanent and deputation with less dependency on contractual 
scientists. The service conditions for contractual appointees can be based on the New 
Labour Codes which may include fixed term contracts, social security network and 
performance-based conversion to permanent posting after a fixed period on the lines of 
CSIR model. 
1.4.9 The Committee appreciates the efforts taken by the Department to fill up the 
various post of scientist but is disappointed to perceive the reasons for the posts to 
remain vacant like difficulty in attracting eligible and qualified candidates, lack of 
interest on the part selected candidate to join, etc. Meanwhile, the Committee 
recognises the pro active approach adopted by the Department in addressing the issue 
and revisiting and reviewing the Recruitment Rules of HTAIn to include direct 
recruitment as well. Accordingly, the Committee supports and recommends the revision 
of recruitment rules of HTAIn Attached Office to include direct recruitment keeping in 
account the importance of assessment of AI technologies/tools by HTAIn.  
 
1.4.10 Considering the relevance of Ethics Committee in the era of emerging AI 
technologies and digital health platforms in the public health system, the Committee 
feels that while formulating the Recruitment Rules for the post of Scientists in the 
Ethics Committee, the Department should opt for direct recruitment on the lines of 
HTAInAttached Office. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Recruitment 
Rules for the post of Scientists in the Ethics Committee needs to be formulated 
including direct recruitment, at the earliest, and these posts are to be populated as soon 
as possible.The Committee also recommends that time to time updation of the 
Recruitment Rules should be a conscious effort to contemporise the conditions of 
service for development of a permanent scientific workforce in Health Research sector 
of the country. 
 

9 
 
1.4.11 Subsequently, the Committee sought to know about any recent shift in paradigm and 
philosophy of working of the Department and to the extent the same is in consonance with 
global trend of health research. In response submitted as under:- 
 
1)   The paradigm shift in philosophy of working - 
 
a. Reassignment of roles: -Roles of different bodies under DHR including ICMR 
have been explicitly differentiated according to comparative advantage. DHR has 
retained stewardship functions—research infrastructure, human resource 
development and governance frameworks. Centre for Guidelines has been set up for 
Evidence synthesis, and Health Technology Assessment in India (HTAIn) attached 
office is established for cost effectiveness studies. ICMR has been aligned primarily 
to generation of research evidence through intramural and extramural mechanisms. 
 
b. From problem description to solution orientation: - The most visible doctrinal 
shift is the inversion of the research mix. Translational research now accounts for 
~70% of the portfolio compared with ~30% earlier.  
 
c. From centralized science to distributed capacity:- Extramural funding has 
expanded from 26% (2017) to 64% (2024) of the ICMR budget. Dedicated 
governance through an Additional DG (Extramural) and mission programmes such 
as the National Health Research Programme have made multi-centric, multi-state 
implementation the norm rather than the exception. 
 
d. From investigator autonomy to priority alignment: - Competitive intramural 
grants and structured review processes have shifted emphasis from individual 
curiosity-driven proposals toward institutional and national priorities. 
 
2) Instruments that operationalize the shift- The philosophical change is backed by   
machinery. 
a. Mission-mode research:- The National Health Research Programme organizes 
large, multi-disciplinary projects across priority areas—child health, cancers, 
anemia, mental health, AMR, vector-borne diseases, tuberculosis, maternal and 
elderly health, emergency care, and health systems. The design spans discovery to 
delivery. 
b. Innovation pathway support:- Through partnership with Central Drugs 
Standard Control Organization, the MedTechMitra platform provides regulatory 
navigation, mentoring, and evaluation. Quantitatively, hundreds of innovators have 
engaged; technologies have moved into test licensing, clinical evaluation, and HTA 
referral. 
c. Patent facilitation, infrastructure sharing (I-RISE), collaborating centres of 
excellence, and device fabrication capacity (e.g., mPRAGATI at Indian Institute of 
Technology Delhi) collectively reduce barriers between lab insight and market entry. 
d. Evidence architecture:- Creation of a Centre for Evidence-Based Guidelines, 
nationwide technical hubs, systematic reviews, and meta-analyses embeds 
methodological rigor into national recommendations. 

10 
 
e. Health Technology Assessment;-HTAIn’s formal positioning within DHR, 
expansion of resource hubs and centres, and completion of dozens of studies create 
an economic and outcomes-based filter for adoption. 
f. Human capital pipeline:- The HRD consolidation, fellowship expansion, 
women scientist awards, Young Medical Faculty PhD programme, and Faculty of 
Medical Research under Academy of Scientific and Innovative Research represent 
structural investment in physician-scientists. 
g. Pandemic preparedness & One Health:- Under DHR leadership, integrated 
surveillance, BSL-3 networking, mock drills, genomic concepts such as I-COG, and 
vaccine/diagnostic pipelines show a move toward anticipatory capability. 
h. Data as infrastructure:- The national repository introduces standardized, 
privacy-screened, reusable datasets to accelerate secondary research. 
i. Financial efficiency:- Utilization of 99.75% of allocation in 2024-25 indicates 
high absorptive and execution capacity. 
3)  Consonance with global trends in health research 
The described reforms to widely recognized directions in contemporary international 
practice: 
(i) emphasis on translation and impact, 
(ii) mission-driven consortia, 
(iii) regulatory and commercialization facilitation, 
(iv) HTA-anchored reimbursement logic, 
(v) One Health security frameworks, 
(vi) data platforms enabling reproducibility, and 
(vii) cultivation of clinician-scientists. 
 
Further, DHR also stated that it largely operates in line with Management by Objectives 
(MBO) through its schemes, guidelines, and attached offices to translate mission goals into 
programs with defined outputs (MRU/MRHRU labs, HRD schemes, grant-in aid, HTAIn, 
evidence‑based guidelines), which is characteristic of objective‑driven management in 
healthcare administration. 
1.4.12 The Committee is appreciative of the working ecosystem of DHR. Reassignment 
of roles, problem description to solution orientation, centralized science to distributed 
capacity are effective management tactics. Further, the Committee finds that operation 
mechanism for these tactics like mission-mode research, patent facilitation, Health 
Technology Assessment, human capital pipeline, data as infrastructure are bringing in 
expected benefits at ground level. The Committee also notes that the Department 
operates through the concept of Management by Objectives (MBO). This management 
approach is based on setting specific and measurable goals and, according to the 
Committee, is an effective requirement in healthcare administration. 
1.5 Health Research Challenges In India       
 
1.5.1 Health Research in India has covered many milestones in the last few decades. Still 
challenges are in abundance, primary being dearth in skilled work force, followed by the 
need to translate research findings into evidence-based practices; development and safe usage 
of emerging technologies, like AI; analyzing the various ethical component of research; etc.  

11 
 
 
1.5.2 As part of understanding these challenges, the Committee sought the details of the 
efforts made by DHR to ensure that research findings are consistently translated into 
evidence-based practices and implemented across all states, addressing the current policy-to-
practice gap. In reply, the Department submitted that Health Technology Assessment in India 
(HTAIn) has been established to systematically collect and analyse the evidence on cost, 
clinical effectiveness and safety of health interventions. By collaborating closely with State 
Health Departments, HTAIn facilitates capacity building, identifies regional priorities, and 
aids evidence-based decision-making by evaluating both clinical efficacy and cost-
effectiveness. HTAIn has provided strategic recommendations for a diverse range of 
community health interventions and diagnostic technologies like: 
 
i. Chronic Disease Screening: Screening of Type-2 diabetes, hypertension, and oral 
cancer. 
ii. Infectious Disease Management: Screening of Hepatitis B and C. 
iii. Indigenous Diagnostic Innovations: Locally developed Point of Care (PoC) test 
kits for Hemophilia A, Von Willebrand Disease (VWD), and Sickle Cell Disease. 
iv. Community-Based Technologies: 
a) Neonatal Care: Portable automated ABR devices for newborn hearing 
screenings. 
b) Respiratory Health: Handheld X-ray devices and AI-driven interpretation 
tools for the detection of Tuberculosis in field settings. 
 
1.5.3 Apart from HTAIn, DHR also has a Centre for Evidence Based Guidelines to 
promote consistency and methodological rigor in evaluating evidence for national health 
guidelines. Further, DHR plays a crucial role in mainstreaming implementation research to 
facilitate the promotion and implementation of research within its partner institutions which 
involves capacity building, co-production and community engagement, structured evaluation 
and monitoring and dissemination to policymakers. District-level tools such as risk 
stratification systems and dashboards are being tested in partnership with State Governments 
to improve outcomes. DHR has supported direct programme evidence generation, including 
development of operational guidelines for IV iron use in pregnancy, review and refinement 
of Anaemia Mukt Bharat (AMB) strategies, and updating treatment algorithms for anaemia 
in pregnant women and children. Under AMB multi-state PRAKASH study, ASHAs and 
frontline workers have also been involved in routine programme settings to generate 
actionable evidence, and the findings are systematically shared with states and M/o HFW 
through structured feedback mechanisms, enabling timely programme strengthening and 
closing the policy-to-practice gap. Further, the multi-state NECCTAR trial generates 
implementation-ready evidence by strengthening the nutritional quality of take-home rations 
alongside structured behaviour change interventions within existing government platforms, 
enabling States to directly adopt or adapt findings. Overall, DHR’s approach ensures that 
research outputs directly inform programme design, operational guidelines, and scale-up 
decisions, thereby, systematically bridging the evidence-to-implementation gap. 
 
1.5.4. According to DHR, there are several examples of research findings from MRHRUs 
that contributed to addressing the current policy to practice gap. These are as follows:  
 

12 
 
1. MRHRU Dahanu: Implemented a comprehensive programme in Dahanu Block 
covering prevention, early management, and surveillance of snakebite. Research contributed 
to WHO recognition of snakebite as a notifiable disease. 
 
2. MRHRU Chandragiri: Conducted a state-wide assessment of better diet program 
that focused on children from deprived settings in Andhra Pradesh Social Welfare 
Residential Educational Institutions Society. The findings have resulted in an informed 
policy revision in food package distribution practices.  
 
3. MRHRU Puducherry: Implemented a school-based dengue reduction programme 
combining education, awareness, and community participation. This has been recognized as 
a scalable inter-sectoral model for vector-borne disease prevention.  
 
4. MRHRU Tirunelveli: Developed an Android Applications for early stroke 
identification and adverse pregnancy outcomes by using Bateman’s Obstetric Comorbidity 
Index.  
 
5. MRHRU Tigiria: Developed the Demography Integrated Surveillance for Health 
Assessment (DISHA). This has facilitated real-time monitoring of dengue and chikungunya 
trends at the community level. 
 
1.5.5 The Committee recognises the work done by DHR through HTAIn and MRHRU 
towards systematic collection of data and analysis of evidence thereby addressing the 
current ‘policy to practice’ gap. Having said that, the Committee feels more can be 
done to track whether research findings are actually incorporated into 
national/state/district health policy/plans. The Committee observes the need and 
existence of programmes addressing malnutrition, infectious diseases, and maternal 
mortality point to this. Taking this into account, the Committee recommends that DHR 
should create an “Evidence-to-Action Dashboard” that maps research outputs against 
national health priorities. Further, every ministry/department involved in the 
implementation of a particular programme/scheme should submit an action-taken 
report on relevant ICMR/DHR studies within six months of its publication, with public 
disclosure and Parliamentary review. The Committee is of the considered view that 
such measures would bridge the critical translation gap and ensure that India’s robust 
health research ecosystem directly strengthens evidence-based community interventions 
and delivers measurable public health gains. The Committee is of the firm conviction 
that research must ultimately translate into affordable medicines and accessible 
therapies for citizens. 
 
1.5.6 Another concern area of Health Research is development of AI technologies/ tools 
and integration of digital health platforms into the existing public health system. One of the 
crucial steps in this direction is setting up ICMR-National Institute for Research in Digital 
Health and Data Science (NIRDHDS) and entrusting it with the task of developing India-
specific AI-based solutions for strengthening the healthcare system with an overarching goal 
of achieving Universal Health Coverage. The Committee wanted DHR to share a few 
flagship AI-based solutions or pilots that NIRDHDS has already deployed or is piloting in 
public health facilities. In response, the Department a few ongoing scientific works for 
development or piloting AI-based solutions are the: 

13 
 
 
(i) Eye-Platter Dataset, which is a comprehensive, multimodal dataset that has been 
developed to facilitate the evaluation of ocular diseases though the collaborative 
research initiative led by IIT Jodhpur and Director, ICMR-NIRDHDS, in partnership 
with PGIMER Chandigarh, AIIMS Guwahati and Sri Sankaradeva Nethralaya.  
 
(ii) Another ongoing project that focuses on developing an AI-based system for the early 
diagnosis of chronic kidney disease (CKD) through retinal images, with the aim of 
improving healthcare access and delivery, particularly in underserved and remote 
areas. 
 
(iii) Another ongoing project, PrAimaan, aims to create a national platform for the 
continuous evaluation, benchmarking, and adaptation of health AI models in India, 
embedding ongoing validation and contextual relevance into AI governance.  
 
(iv) A national-level open-access databank hosting over 2500 annotated medical images 
from 35 institutions across 26 states Indian annotated Medical-image Repository for 
Imaging and Telepathology (i-AMRIT) has been developed.  
 
(v) A GGIS-based digital platform mapping over 600 government diagnostic laboratories 
in Delhi, Diagnostic Services in Healthcare: Availability & Mapping (DISHA) portal 
has been launched. It aims to enhance access, policy planning, and rationalisation of 
diagnostic services aligned with the National Essential Diagnostics List (NEDL). 
 
(vi) An AI-based tool for diagnosis of oral and breast cancer has been developed using 
YOLO-NAS, UNet-ResNet34, and ResNet50. 
 
(vii) Multicentric study in seven states of Northeast India Bioimpedance-Based 
Diagnostic Innovation has piloted a novel, portable bioimpedance device for 
classification of oral lesions among tobacco users.  
 
(viii) A study that is under planning is the validation of the Roshni AI system to assess its 
diagnostic accuracy, impact on cataract screening and treatment uptake, usability for 
ASHAs, and real-world performance, and by comparing community-based results 
with federated AI testing conducted at IIT Kanpur. 
 
1.5.7 According to DHR, research targets of ICMR-NIRDHDS include development of 
standard guidelines for data curation, anonymisation, and sharing for unlocking health data 
for research, and the development of standard guidelines for scientific evaluation of AI 
solutions at each stage of their lifecycle in health to enable adoption and scale up of safe and 
responsible solutions.  
 
1.5.8 Further, the Department also informed the Committee about other initiatives from 
NIRDHDS that are as under: 
 
Establishment of Centres of Collaborative Research (CCR): 
 
1.5.9 As a part of the intramural initiative of ICMR, the institute has proposed an initial 
establishment of three CCRs which is under review. Through these centres, ICMR-
NIRDHDS has proposed to collaborate with premium institutions and centres across the 
country for focused research in the following areas: 
 

14 
 
 The CCR with IIT Madras, IISc Bengaluru, and ISB Hyderabad aims to 
establish a first-of-its-kind programme generating HTA-informed evidence for a 
Responsible AI framework in Indian healthcare. It will evaluate AI's real-world 
effectiveness, safety, equity, fairness, explainability, and governance, adapting 
conventional methods for evolving AI systems across diverse settings. The CCR will 
carry out health systems research, implementation research, and economic evaluation, 
and will complement this by examining operational workflows, organisational 
readiness, behavioural responses, and resource implications that determine real-world 
effectiveness, value, and sustainability at scale. 
 
 The CCR with C-DAC, ECHO, and Intelehealth will work to strengthen 
access, continuity, and quality of care for priority non-communicable diseases, 
specifically diabetes and hypertension, by leveraging India’s existing national digital 
health infrastructure. The work will examine how eSanjeevani, ABHA, ABDM, and 
the National NCD Portal can be more effectively linked to support longitudinal NCD 
care pathways rather than episodic consultations.  
 
1.5.10 The Institute will lead rigorous clinical, implementation, and health systems research 
to assess feasibility, safety, clinical relevance, and governance considerations. The overall 
objective is to generate actionable evidence to inform national policy and program design on 
how telemedicine and digital public infrastructure can be leveraged to close persistent access 
gaps in NCD care within the public health system. 
 
 The CCR with IIT Ropar, AIIMS Delhi, and PGIMER Chandigarh will have 
the centre functioning as a data-integrated ecosystem, focusing initially on athletics 
(running and javelin), swimming, and fencing, while remaining scalable to other 
Olympic disciplines. Core components include sports cardiology, biomechanics and 
orthopaedics, precision nutrition, and artificial intelligence-enabled analytics. A 
central deliverable will be a National Digital Athlete Performance Dashboard, 
enabling longitudinal tracking of cardiovascular load, biomechanical efficiency, 
injury risk, training response, and recovery metrics. By integrating multidisciplinary 
clinical data with real-time performance analytics, the Centre seeks to systematically 
reduce performance variance, improve peak readiness, and translate India’s 
expanding sports infrastructure into measurable international medal outcomes. 
 
Establishment of a dedicated Unit for Evaluation of AI-driven solutions in healthcare: 
 
1.5.11 The proposed Unit aims to collaborate closely with key stakeholders, including a 
network of healthcare institutions across India (such as TMH and National Cancer Grid 
(NCG), AIIMS Delhi and other centres, PGIMER Chandigarh, etc.), network of technical 
partners (such as IITs, IISc Bengaluru, IIIT Delhi, etc.), development partners, and line 
ministries at Centre and State levels (IndiaAI Mission, NHA, State health Authorities/ 
Missions, etc.). Through this comprehensive network, ICMR-NIRDHDS will engage in 
systematic planning and conduct of technical, clinical, and economic evaluations of AI 
solutions in health through research studies. The institute is also in the process of establishing 
a Clinical Trials Unit to conduct trials of digital health solutions and advance methodological 
research in this emerging field. This Unit will complement the proposed Evaluation Unit by 
strengthening our institutional capacity for high-quality evaluations of digital and AI-enabled 
health technologies. 
 
Intramural projects:  
 
1.5.12 ICMR-NIRDHDS has submitted eight proposals under the intramural grant call of the 
ICMR which pertain to development and evaluation of India-specific AI-driven solutions and 

15 
 
strengthening of research data infrastructure for enabling AI in the health research ecosystem 
in the country. Additionally, the institute has submitted a proposal under the ICMR-
Intramural MedTech Development Grant-2025, and it has been selected for the grant.  
 
1.5.13 In this connection, the Department also brought to the notice of the Committee the 
main challenges in AI and Digital Health Integration which are as follows: 
 
(i) Data Quality, Privacy, and Security: AI models require vast amounts of high-quality, 
diverse, and representative data for training and validation. 
(ii) Regulatory and Ethical Concerns: The rapidly evolving nature of AI technology 
outpaces traditional regulatory frameworks for medical devices. 
(iii) Interoperability and Integration: A lack of standardised data protocols hinders 
effective data exchange between different systems and providers. 
(iv) Clinical Validation and Reliability: Rigorous, real-world validation through 
prospective studies and randomised controlled trials (RCTs) is needed to demonstrate 
a tool's clinical and economic utility and ensure it is safe and reliable for diverse 
populations. 
(v) Workforce Skills and Acceptance: There is a significant shortage of healthcare 
professionals with expertise in AI and data science. 
 
1.5.14 The Committee is pleased to note the work in progress towards the development 
of India-specific AI-based solutions for strengthening the healthcare system in the 
country. Having said that, the Committee feels that emerging technologies such as AI 
diagnostic tools and digital health platforms in India remain in their early stages due to 
issues like poor data management, algorithmic bias, ethical challenges, and limited 
internet access in semi-urban, rural, hilly and tribal areas. Alongwith these factors, the 
Committee also hold the view that callous electronic health records maintenance and 
cybersecurity vulnerabilities compromise trust and safety towards AI tools & digital 
health platforms. Accordingly, the Committee opine that there should be data driven 
healthcare planning with strict privacy safeguards. For the success of these data driven 
healthcare policy, the country also needs technology empowered scientist, doctors, 
support staff and patients consolidating Protective Research Equilibrium (PRE). 
 
1.5.15 As steps to make AI tools & digital health platforms more reliable the 
Committee recommends that DHR should make pre and post-market HTA mandatory 
for all AI diagnostics seeking to integrate with public health system. DHR must update 
and enforce Ethics Guidelines for Application of Artificial Intelligence in Biomedical 
Research and Healthcare, 2023 by ICMR with compulsory bias audits, diverse dataset 
requirements, and ‘Human-in-the-Loop’ features for every government funded or 
publicly integrated AI tool. Health data/record being a sensitive subject, the Committee 
recommends for more region-diseases specific studies by DHR/ICMR on health data 
bias, maintenance and usage. 
 
1.5.16 The Committee notices that inspite of Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission 
(ABDM), internet in rural/tribal areas is still unreliable. Another concern in rural area 
is digital literacy gaps and inadequate training for frontline workers like Accredited 
Social Health Activist (ASHAs) and Auxiliary Nurse Midwife (ANM).  This results in 
urban-centric development and continued exclusion of millions in the rural belts from 

16 
 
benefits of tools like AI-powered chest X-ray analysis or clinical decision support 
systems like eSanjeevani. To address these, the Committee recommends that DHR 
should develop offline-capable indigenous AI solutions and launch a nationwide AI 
training programme designed for last-mile integration beginning with the aspirational 
districts for frontline workers (ASHAs, ANMs) through Model Rural Health Research 
Units. 
 
1.5.17 On the sensitive issue of ethical concerns related to Health Research, DHR further 
stated to the Committee that NIRDHDS coordinates with IndiaAI Mission (MeitY), 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) and Anusandhan National Research Foundation 
(ANRF) to avoid duplication and ensure interoperability of datasets and AI models. 
NIRDHDS functions as a technical integrator and standards steward, aligning with IndiaAI 
on AI safety and evaluation, with ABDM/NHA on interoperable health data pipelines, and 
with national research funding frameworks, thereby minimising duplication, enabling reuse 
of public datasets, and ensuring interoperable, trustworthy AI development for health in 
India.  
 
1.5.18 The Committee further queried on the safeguard measures built into NIRDHDS 
projects to prevent re-identification of health & research data, especially when datasets are 
shared with private partners. In reply, DHR submitted that as of now, data has not been 
shared by NIRDHDS with any private partner. However, the institute, as per its mandate, has 
been taking up research projects to develop standard guidelines and frameworks for data 
curation, anonymisation, and sharing to enable safe and ethical use of data. The institute has 
constituted a technical working group on data curation, sharing &governance. The group is 
working on the preparation of standard guidelines and frameworks for data curation, 
anonymisation, and sharing pipelines. The work on the federated benchmarking platform for 
testing SI models in health with IIT Kanpur and NHA is a step towards creating a safe 
environment for model testing without giving data access to the model developers. Similarly, 
another federated model training platform is under development by iSpirit where developers 
can train their models on datasets without being able to see or retrieve data. Under the 
guidance of DG, ICMR, the institute along with ICMR headquarters is facilitating the 
planning and testing of the platform. As part of infrastructure, a federated model where data 
is secure and accessible is being built. The institute has been targeting Anusandhan National 
Research Foundation (ANRF) support to bridge the gap between policy design and technical 
execution. The institute has been aligning central and state entities through standardised 
docking guidelines for data management. 
 
1.5.19 It was also submitted that ICMR-NIRDHDS has initiated the work for development 
of an India-specific AI risk-classification framework for health through a dedicated “Risk, 
Monitoring & Evaluation” working group. This initiative is unique in that it proposes a risk-
tiered model linked to clinical use and potential patient harm, rather than technology type 
alone. The framework will establish lifecycle-based approval and oversight mechanisms and 
mandate standards for data quality, transparency, and bias mitigation for high-risk health AI 
systems. 
 

17 
 
1.5.20 According to DHR, apart from NIRDHDS it also woksin collaboration with the 
National Health Authority (NHA) and ICMR, is addressing theethicalchallenges in the 
Health Research through initiatives like: 
 
a) Establishing Regulatory and Ethical Frameworks: DHR adheres to existing national 
digital and data protection frameworks, including the ICMR's ethical guidance for 
biomedical AI, the IT Act 2000, and the Digital Personal Data Protection Act.  
 
b) Developing an Interoperable Digital Ecosystem: Through the Ayushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM), the government is building a national digital health 
infrastructure that uses a unique Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA) ID for 
citizens to link and access their digital health records across various facilities. 
 
1.5.21 The Committee realises that AI is revolutionizing health sector as in every other 
field. However, like any technology, it comes with both advantages and challenges. In 
the opinion of the Committee, besides the considerable efforts on the part of DHR and 
its institutions, strengthening oversight and the capacity of Ethics Committees is one of 
the measures needed to ensure consistent ethical review standards of emerging Al 
technology. Foremost, the Committee recommends thorough monitoring by DHR of 
registration/ re-registration of Ethics Committees across India with the National Ethics 
Committee Registry for Biomedical and Health Research (NECRBHR) and enforce 
penalties for non-compliant institutions. The Committee is of the opinion that the 
country lacks appropriate number of accredited and functional Ethics Committees in 
comparison to the volume of regulatory and academic trials, resulting in delayed 
approvals and increased risk of ethical lapses. 
 
1.5.22 Since there is an involvement of various Ministries/Bodies like DHR, MeitY, 
ICMR, National Health Authority (NHA), Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), 
Anusandhan National Research Foundation (ANRF), etc, the Committee recommends a 
dedicated AI Surveillance & Ethics Monitoring Cell with inter-ministerial 
representation and mandated annual Action Taken Report submission to an 
Appropriate Authority for real time performance tracking and periodic ethical audits 
and to create safety benchmarks & roadmaps for AI integration for health sector as a 
whole.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  

18 
 
CHAPTER – II   
ANALYSIS OF BUDGETARY ALLOCATIONS 
 
Funding pattern of DHR 
 
2.1 National Health Policy 2017 has recommended 2.5 % of GDP on health by 2025. 
According to Economic Survey 2025-26, India’s Total Public Health Expenditure (Centre + 
States) is at 1.8 % of GDP in 2025-26. The Budgetary Allocation of DHR for 2026-27 is 
Rs.4, 821 crore, which is an increase of Rs. 893.57 crore compared to 2025-26. In spite of the 
hike, budget allocation to DHR is mere 4.52 % of the Total Health Budget of the country and 
0.02% of GDP of India as the table shown below.

2.3 The Committee finds that the public expenditure on health research has been 
maintained at a modest 0.02% of GDP and has contributed to the slow progress in the area of 
Health Research in the country. In this regard, the Committee wished to know from DHR the 
measures adopted by them to bridge the significant funding gap to support cutting-edge 
research and infrastructure development across the country.     
 
2.4 In response, the Department submitted that it has employed a multi-pronged approach 
to bridge the health research funding gap, primarily through fostering public-private 
partnerships, and implementing targeted grant-in-aid schemes for research and infrastructure 
development. The key initiatives are as under: 
 
i. Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs): DHR is actively promoting PPP models to 
augment infrastructure and expertise, encouraging private equity investments into 
underfunded segments of the healthcare system. 
ii. Inter-Sectoral Convergence Scheme: DHR provides grant-in-aid to encourage 
research partnerships among different government agencies, NGOs, and industry 
to translate existing health knowledge into deliverable products and processes. 

19 
 
iii. During the 15th Finance Commission (FC) period, an allocation of Rs 324 crore 
was made for the VRDL Scheme. For the 16th FC period, the DHR has proposed 
Rs 980.91 crore, which includes an expanded scope covering syndromic 
surveillance activities, recurring support to Infectious Disease Research and 
Diagnostic Laboratories (IRDLs), and the establishment of new VRDLs, among 
other initiatives. If the proposed allocation is approved, there would be sufficient 
funding to support cutting-edge research and infrastructure development under the 
VRDL Scheme, effectively bridging the current funding gap. 
iv. Multidisciplinary Research Units (MRUs): DHR continues to establish and fund 
MRUs in government medical colleges and research institutions across the 
country. These units aim to strengthen health research infrastructure, build 
capacity, and focus research on national priorities like non-communicable 
diseases (NCDs). 
v. Model Rural Health Research Units (MRHRUs): To bridge the urban-rural 
research gap, MRHRUs are being established in the vicinity of 
Primary/Community Health Centres. These units focus on transferring technology 
to the rural level and improving health services for the rural population. 
vi. Human Resource Development (HRD) Scheme: This initiative aims to create a 
pool of talented health research personnel through fellowships, scholarships, and 
specialized training for medical faculty, mid-career scientists, and students in 
priority areas such as clinical trials, genomics, and stem cell research. 
 
2.5 Further, DHR-ICMR 2024-29 Action Plan directed research toward critical national 
health challenges: promoting indigenous and affordable health technologies, providing 
solutions for difficult health problems like anaemia, antimicrobial resistance (AMR), and 
child malnutrition, advancing digital health solutions and technology-driven surveillance, 
ensuring research evidence is translated into practical action and policy.  
 
2.6 The Committee also sought to know the specific steps DHR has been taking to ensure 
better utilization of allocated capital funds to develop tangible healthcare assets and research 
facilities. In reply, the Department stated the following: 
 
i. Improved Financial Discipline: DHR has emphasised adhering to financial 
discipline and administrative due diligence as outlined in GFRs. 
ii. DHR ensures that key projects and programmes are sufficiently funded based on 
the initial budgetary allocations and plans to approach the Ministry of Finance for 
additional funds at the Revised Estimate (RE) stage and through supplementary 
grants, if necessary, depending on the progress of ongoing missions, R&D 
projects, and facility build-up. 
iii. The Department is actively involved in building new infrastructure and 
establishing state-of-the-art equipment. This includes the establishment of a 
network of Viral Research and Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) for managing 
epidemics and pandemics, development of Multi-Disciplinary Research Units 
(MRUs) in government medical colleges and Model Rural Health Research Units 
(MRHRUs) in rural areas. 
iv. The Government of India conceptualized and introduced an umbrella scheme for 
development of Infrastructure for health research since 2013-14.  It has 02 sub-

20 
 
schemes namely (i) establishing Multidisciplinary Research Units (MRUs) in 
Medical Colleges/Research Institutes, and (ii) establishing Model Rural Health 
Research Units (MRHRUs) in rural areas. While MRUs are established in a 
Medical College, MRHRUs are standalone buildings in vicinity of a Community 
Health Centre/Primary Health Centre.  Both the sub-schemes promote creation of 
tangible health research assets. Currently there are 123 MRUs and 39 MRHRUs 
across the country. 
v. To ensure effective utilisation of capital support provided under the Viral Research 
and Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDL) Scheme, DHR has adopted measures such as 
mandatory assessment of laboratory space, phased release of funds, and 
infrastructure readiness, periodic monitoring of progress, and physical site visits. 
vi. An inter-ministerial committee has been constituted to monitor the implementation 
of action plans. 
vii. Dedicated project monitoring units to track progress and swiftly resolve 
administrative hurdles in scheme implementation. 
viii. Reforms in government budgeting, such as focusing on outcomes through an 
improved outcome budget document, are being implemented. 
 
2.7 The Committee observes that since Government is spending only 0.02% of GDP 
for Health research for the last 5 years, a large proportion of existing schemes would 
naturally suffer from implementation gaps, poor last-mile delivery, and suboptimal 
progress in semi-urban, rural, hilly and tribal areas. Despite the funds being hard to 
come, DHR has always ensured best utilization of the allocated funds and the 
Committee is appreciative of the efforts of DHR. Having said that, the Committee 
believes that the shortfall in Health Research Funding is not only a budgetary issue but 
reflects weak evidence on the returns of existing schemes/programmes as well, both 
physical and financial. DHR should be able to persuade the Ministry of Finance in pre-
budget consultations, that every additional fund allocated, yields promotion of 
indigenous affordable health technologies and digital health solutions which is 
translated into practical health policies and actions, all of these ultimately translating to 
saving of public funds. Accordingly, the Committee recommends a dedicated cell in 
DHR to bring forth periodic reports on both the physical and financial aspects of public 
health spending on bio-medical research. The reports should give emphasis on the 
outcome of schemes/programmes and utility of innovations and technology emanating 
from bio medical research for practical use on the ground level. 
 
2.8 To improve the fund utilization of DHR, the Committee recommends measures 
like teaming of seasoned scientists with early-career researchers in 
programmes/schemes to bridge knowledge gaps and variant insights in the funding 
landscaping, real-time tracking of projects outcomes involved in assets creation via 
GIS-enabled portal, developing scorecards and linking the same to annual performance 
appraisals of institutes/individuals concerned, introduction of automatic forfeiture and 
reallocation of unutilised capital grants after a certain period as well as blacklisting of 
defaulting institutes for future projects. For the Committee, the success of any research 
endeavour is inextricably linked to adequate funding. By addressing the challenges 
inherent in the funding process through these recommended methods, DHR should be 
able to strengthen their position which may catalyse the Ministry of Finance to dedicate 

21 
 
more funds for the Department for the stated strategic purpose based on actual 
outcome intending to make the healthcare more accessible and affordable. 
 
2.9 Regarding the issue of only less than 25% of the health and education cess collections 
being transferred to the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN) and its 
consequences on DHR-funded schemes, the Department replied that its schemes are not 
directly funded by the PMSSN. But the Department expressed its concern that its 
schemes/programmes are part of the larger health ecosystem that experiences increased 
financial pressure when the dedicated cess funds are not fully utilised as intended. This can 
lead to greater competition for limited Gross Budgetary Support (GBS) funds, potentially 
hindering the DHR's ability to secure increased funding for expanding its research initiatives 
and human resource development programmes. 
 
2.10 The Committee understands that currently DHR schemes are not benefitting 
from by the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN). However, the 
Committee is of the firm opinion that the DHR schemes are in no way less important in 
the endeavours to attain inter- alia the targets of National Health Policy, 2017, which is 
one of the salient features of PMSSN. Hence, the Committee recommends that DHR 
should submit a formal proposal to the Ministry of Finance for ring-fencing an 
appropriate percentage of the health component of cess from PMSSN for its schemes/ 
programmes. The Committee feels that such a proposal would hold weightage since 
PMSSN is the primarily a non-lapsable reserve fund which has been conceived to cover 
broader aims like National Health Policy 2017, Emergency & Disaster preparedness 
and response during health emergencies, etc. These aims of PMSSN directly impinge 
implementation of evidence-based interventions in urban/rural/tribal areas and 
translation of research outcomes into policy and practice. 
 
2.11 Regarding 70% of the research funding of DHR being urban centric, the Department 
submitted measures taken by them to mitigate this bias. According to DHR,ICMR provides 
financial support to medical colleges, universities and research institutions across the 
country. These institutions are generally located in Tier I, II and III cities; however, the 
geographical location of an institution does not limit the population coverage of research 
activities.  Available data indicates that nearly 60% of ongoing research projects are funded 
in institutions situated in Tier II and Tier III cities. Furthermore, many studies conducted by 
institutions located in urban areas involve extensive field components covering both rural and 
underserved populations. The National Health Research Projects portfolio is designed to 
ensure representation across geographical regions, including urban, rural and remote 
communities. 
 
2.12 In addition, several ICMR institutes and regional centres specifically undertake 
research addressing the health needs of tribal and other underserved populations. These 
include the ICMR-National Institute of Research in Tribal Health, ICMR-Centre for 
Research Management and Control of Hemoglobinopathies, ICMR-Vector Control Research 
Centre, ICMR-National Institute for Implementation Research on Non-Communicable 
Diseases, ICMR-Regional Medical Research Centre Sri Vijaya Puram and ICMR-Regional 
Medical Research Centre Bhubaneswar. These institutes generate evidence on disease 
burden, nutrition, maternal and child health, genetic disorders and health-system 

22 
 
strengthening among tribal populations, including Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups 
(PVTGs).Moreover, DHR implements an umbrella scheme for development of infrastructure 
for health research.  It has two sub-schemes namely (i) establishing Multidisciplinary 
Research Units (MRUs) in Medical Colleges/Research Institutes, and (ii) establishing Model 
Rural Health Research Units (MRHRUs). The MRUs are predominantly located in urban 
areas and MRHRUs are located in rural areas. The allocation of funds for the two sub-
schemes is Rs. 288.11 Cr and Rs. 192.36 Cr, respectively, for the 15th Finance Commission 
Period, which amounts to 60% and 40% allocation to the two sub schemes, respectively. 
MRHRU supports research projects through funding. The 36 MRHRUs are supporting 
research activities primarily in rural settings. They aim to build research infrastructure in 
rural India, focusing on health issues relevant to local communities and bridging the gap 
between researchers and rural health systems. 
 
 2.13 The Committee considers the submission of the Department that as far as 
research programmes/schemes are concerned, even though institutions are generally 
located in Tier I, II and III cities, the population coverage of research activities and 
field components includes both rural and underserved populations. Also, the 
submission made by the Department of 60% and 40% fund allocation to MRUs and 
MRHRUs respectively, under Development of Infrastructure for Health Research 
during the 15thFinance Commission Period suggests appropriately earmarked funding 
pattern for both urban and rural belts. Still the Committee feels that lack of proper 
monitoring mechanism when research is conducted in rural areas leads to 
infrastructure and human resource gaps at the time of its implementation. To address 
this, DHR can launch a real-time portal linked to all MRHRUs and Ayushman Bharat 
Health & Wellness Centres (AB-HWCs) and accessible to the Centre and States. 
Moreover, to bring in more transparency in fund allocation, the Committee 
recommends mapping of funding patterns and to make available in public domain, 
institute-wise, State-wise, and rural/urban break-up data of sanctioned funds. Further, 
an independent annual equity audit by CAG should be institutionalised, with findings 
presented to the Parliament. 
 
2.14 The Committee is of the considered view that when research funding remains 
concentrated in urban-based institutions and research centres it may result in health 
challenges innate to rural and tribal areas such as vector-borne diseases, malnutrition, 
maternal mortality and sickle cell anaemia, not getting its needed share of funding. 
More research institutions in the tribal and aspirational districts will ensure the area 
specific research gets needed attention and no region is left behind in healthcare 
preparedness. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that DHR should notify a 
policy mandating that at least 40% of all extramural research grants be awarded to 
projects led by or implemented in rural/tribal institutions. These projects can also be 
partnered by State Medical Colleges and Model Rural Health Research Units 
(MRHRUs).  
 
2.15 As perceived by the Committee, another downside for rural and tribal areas is 
that their medical colleges and research units lack trained manpower, equipment, and 
administrative support, making them less competitive for DHR grants thereby 
reinforcing the urban funding dominance. In this regard, the Committee recommends 

23 
 
that DHR should provide extra weightage in grant evaluation for proposals from these 
institutions and special incentives like hardship allowance, fast-track promotion for 
scientists opting for rural postings to aggressively handhold them. 
 
2.2 Projected Demands and Actual Allocations of DHR 
 
2.2.1 The Department of Health Research has been allocated as BE 2026-27 Rs. 
4821.21crores against the Projected Demand of Rs. 5523.88 crores. Details of the Projected 
Demand, BE, RE and Actual Expenditure pertaining to DHR for the financial years, 2024-25, 
2025-26 and 2026-27 has been illustrated below: - 
 
(In Crores)

2.2.2 The total allocation of DHR of 2026-2027 of Rs 4821.21crore has increased by 
42.4% from Actuals of 2024-2025 of Rs 3383.93 crore and around 23% from BE of Rs 
3900.69 crore and RE of Rs 3927.64crore2025-2026. The Committee wished to know from 
the Department, the major factors that have led to this. In response, DHR submitted that the 
expenditure on DHR schemes of Human Resource and Capacity Development, 
Establishment of Infrastructure (MRU/MRHRU) was minimal in 2021-22 and 2022-23 
owing to Covid-19 situation as the resources of both DHR as well as its grantee institutions 
were pre-occupied with tackling of the pandemic and there was restricted activity owing to 
lockdowns, etc. The expenditure continued to be low in the third year (2023-24) also being 
the recovery year and minimum carry forwarding of liabilities (2nd/3rd year grants) of 
previous years. The normal activity of the Schemes could be resumed in the last two years 
and the expenditure, accordingly, picked up. 
 
2.2.3 The major factor that has led to year-on-year growth in ICMR budget is more 
investments in research activities at Intra-Mural and Extra-Mural projects. Post pandemic the 
focus is to promote research in health priorities and to develop affordable, innovative, 
indigenous health technologies and products. To achieve this ICMR has prioritise new 
research initiatives like- First in the World, Development of Frontier Technologies, 
MedTechMitra, Patent Mitra, Centre for Advanced Research (CAR), National Health 
Research Priority Projects (NHRP) etc. Further, marginal increase has been in salary 
component due to inflation adjustments. 
 
2.2.4 Thereafter, the Committee wanted to know the specific Heads under which this 
increased fund of Rs. 893.57 crore of the BE of DHR for 2026-27 has been allocated and the 
details of re-calibration of such schemes in terms of their physical and financial targets for 
the ensuing year. For which, DHR submitted that ICMR budget has been enhanced by 850 Cr 
Rupees. This additional amount will be utilized in the following manner-

A. There is an enhancement of Rs 12.765 cr in VRDL scheme. This is incremental 
enhancement taking into consideration that the scheme of Development of Tools /Support 
to Prevent Outbreaks & Epidemics,” which is proposed to be discontinued from the 
XVIEFC cycle, with its core components being integrated into the VRDL Scheme.  
 
B.  In the HRD scheme, it is stated that the total budget for FY 2025-26 was Rs. 93.00 
crore and for FY 2026-27 the budget (RE) has been provisioned as Rs. 105.00 crore. The 
increase of budget by Rs. 12.00 crore over previous year is a normal increase and has been 
planned toutilize as per EFC plan. The comparative rise in physical target is reflected in-
100 additional fellowships.  
 
C. PM ABHIM Scheme- In the 16thFC period, new research infrastructure activities 
have been proposed-
