repository for authenticated viral strains is proposed to accelerate the development of 
indigenous diagnostics and vaccines during future public health emergencies 
 

25 
 
2.2.5 Further, the Department was asked about any reappropriation of funds they are 
planning related to schemes under it in the last FY or for this FY.The details for the same as 
provided by DHR is as under:- 
 
1. Development of Tools/Support to Prevent Outbreaks of Epidemics (CS) 
Under the scheme “Development of Tools / Support to Prevent Outbreaks & 
Epidemics”, DHR had sought an additional provision of ₹1.50 crore as Revised 
Estimates (RE) for the year 2025–26 to support ongoing research, diagnostic 
development and outbreak preparedness activities. However, an amount of ₹18 
lakh was approved, hence RE is 3.67 crore. 
 
2.  Human Resource and Development Scheme (HRD): 
It is stated that during FY 2024-25, no reappropriation of funds related to HRD 
scheme have been done. However, for FY 2025-26 so far, DHR-HRD is able to 
utilize the allocated funds and if there is any saving in the department under 
different schemes, the same will be utilized to the extent of shortfall. 
 
2.2.6 Based on the figures of Projected Demand, BE, RE and Actual Expenditure 
pertaining to DHR for the financial years, 2024-25, 2025-26 and 2026-27, the 
Committee observes that the gap between the funds projected by DHR and the budget 
allocated by the Ministry of Finance has narrowed from Rs 1069.34 crore in 2024-25 to 
Rs. 702.67 during 2026-27. The Committee views such trend as a positive move and 
hopes that the funds get translated into measurable outcomes like affordable treatment, 
strong disease surveillance and world class research infrastructure. The Committee also 
observes that the utilisation of funds by Department which remained below par owing 
to various emergent and unprecedented constraints initially has picked up in the post-
pandemic period.  
 
2.2.7 The Committee observes that the enhanced BE for 2026-27 of Rs 4,821.21 crore 
(increase of 42.2 % over the Actuals of 2024-25 and 23% over RE of 2025-26) is 
overwhelmingly research-driven (Rs. 850 crore to ICMR, including Rs. 475 crore 
extramural, Rs 150 crore Intramural and Rs. 200 crore GIA-Capital funds), with 
targeted scaling of new initiatives, MedTechMitra, First-in-the-World, Centre for 
Advanced Research (CAR), National Health Research Priority Projects (NHRP), 
NIRMAN, National Virus Resource Facility (NVRF), etc.) and HRD fellowships. The 
Committee expects DHR to devise proactive steps to efficiently utilise the increased 
funding received for the ensuing year. Ensuring the assessment of capacity to utilise 
including institutional infrastructure, financial compliance, project progress roadmap, 
etc,this should be ensured before funds are released. Such measures would be especially 
pivotal in case of new Schemes like MedTechMitra, Patent Mitra, etc. The key concerns 
areas of utilisation leading to under absorption of resources that needs to be identified 
and sorted out. The Committee also strongly favours and recommends the creation of 
an institutional mechanism to monitor progress of GIA and PM-ABHIM projects, 
which have consistently witnessed project delays in the past. Realtime Outcome 
Dashboards would be a great tool for the purpose of monitoring the schemes. This step 
may be in addition to the time proven, periodic utilisation review mechanisms that may 
be in place for expected results. A dedicated monitoring mechanism would help to 
convert the ₹200+ crore GIA-Capital spike into tangible assets within the financial year, 
avoiding RE-stage surrenders and supporting long-term research capacity. 

26 
 
 
2.2.8 The Committee further observes that to make optimal utilisation of the funds 
allocated, the Department may explore channelising money from schemes making 
sluggish progress, despite best efforts, to schemes making better headway. The 
Committee recommends such move can be made when required with the due 
consultation and approval of the Ministry of Finance, wherever required. 
 
2.2.9 The Committee takes into account the submission of DHR about the proposed 
new research infrastructure activities under PM-ABHIM Scheme for the 16th Finance 
Commission period viz. NIRMAN, NVRF, National Grade Data Centre and Disaster 
Recovery Infrastructure and establishment of Infectious disease Research & Diagnostic 
Labs. All are critical initiatives that can act as stepping stones in the nation’s progress 
towards self-reliance in the health sector in combating its challenges.  
 
2.2.10 With regard to the capital expenditure (Rs 1.81crore) remaining negligible at less than 
0.04% of total allocation despite the overall increase in total allocation of DHR for 2026- 
2027, the DHR informed that the comparatively low capital expenditure of the Department 
amounting to approximately ₹1.75–1.81 crore in 2026–27 (less than 0.04% of the total 
allocation of ₹4821.21 crore), may appear disproportionate in isolation. However, it does not 
indicate any policy preference for revenue or grant-based spending at the cost of 
infrastructure development. DHR is primarily a policy, coordination and funding department 
under the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare and does not maintain independent 
laboratory campuses or buildings. Accordingly, its direct capital outlay is limited to essential 
administrative requirements and is expected to be suitably adjusted at the Revised Estimates 
(RE) stage and in subsequent years as required. The creation and strengthening of physical 
research infrastructure is undertaken through the Indian Council of Medical Research 
(ICMR) and national health research missions, and therefore the bulk of capital formation is 
reflected under those programme heads rather than under the DHR administrative budget, 
including GIA (Capital) of ₹200 crore earmarked for the relocation and establishment of NIV 
Pune at Pashan, development of the Institute of Digital Health in Delhi NCR, infrastructure 
expansion at ICMR Headquarters along with ongoing construction across 28 institutes, 
establishment of a Data Centre at ICMR Headquarters, and procurement of essential 
equipment for 28 ICMR institutes and centres. 
2.2.11 Substantial investments are, in fact, being made towards strengthening national 
research capacity. Under the Human Resource and Capacity Development (HRCD) Umbrella 
Scheme of ICMR, an allocation of ₹105 crore for 2026–27 supports development of a trained 
health research workforce through Short-Term Studentships, medical thesis support, PhD and 
post-doctoral fellowships, women scientist schemes, training workshops and funding of 
ongoing research projects, with several hundred to more than a thousand fellowships being 
supported during the year. In addition, ₹24 crore has been provided for inter-sectoral 
convergence and research grants to promote development of new technologies and affordable 
healthcare solutions, and ₹21 crore for the DIAMONDS programme supporting 25 hubs and 
establishing new centres, along with international cooperation activities. Ongoing grant-in-
aid projects with national institutions such as CSIR laboratories, ICAR institutes and state 
medical colleges cover drug discovery, cancer therapeutics, pulmonary fibrosis, probiotics, 
chronic kidney disease biomarkers and treatment of kala-azar, thereby strengthening 
translational and collaborative research. 

27 
 
2.2.12 Further, significant infrastructure development is being undertaken under the PM-
Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) with an allocation of ₹221.32 
crore, including completion of zonal National Institute of Virology facilities, establishment 
of a BSL-3 laboratory at NITVAR Pune, creation of Infectious Disease Research and 
Diagnostic Laboratories, a Health Research Data Centre, the National Virus Resource 
Facility at NIV Pune and the National Initiative for Reliable Medical Device Testing and 
Assessment Network (NIRMAN).These initiatives substantially enhance national biosecurity 
preparedness, diagnostics, medical device evaluation and research capability. 
2.2.13 Therefore, the present budgetary pattern reflects a structured division of 
responsibilities: DHR focuses on policy guidance, funding support and human resource 
development, while ICMR and national missions undertake infrastructure creation and 
laboratory expansion. The approach emphasizes development of skilled human resources and 
a distributed national research network rather than centralised administrative infrastructure. 
In the long term, this model is expected to strengthen health research capacity, accelerate 
translational research, improve disease surveillance and pandemic preparedness and promote 
indigenous innovation. Accordingly, the low capital expenditure under DHR should be 
viewed as a matter of budgetary classification within an integrated national health research 
architecture and not as an indication of inadequate investment in research infrastructure. 
 
2.2.14 With respect to the matter of capital investment in health research, the 
Committee reckon from the submission of DHR that while DHR is primarily a policy, 
coordination and funding entity and do not maintain independent laboratory 
infrastructure. Physical research infrastructure is undertaken through ICMR and 
national health research missions which reflect the bulk of capital formation. PM-
ABHIM also involves significant infrastructure developments. Hence, the present 
budgetary pattern reflects a division of responsibilities between DHR and ICMR on 
these lines. The Committee makes the observation that DHR and ICMR shoulder a very 
significant component of national advancement that would contribute towards the 
Viksit Bharat vision of the Government and should take all measures to bring out the 
expected outcomes.

2.3.6 The Committee notes that DHR, apart from its existing schemes/programmes, 
has inter alia submitted proposed new schemes, like, Integrating Evidence-based 
Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme⁠ and Policy and Strategic 
Alliance for Nurturing Medical Innovations and Translational Research Acceleration 
(SANMITRA) for the 16th Finance Commission period (2026-27 to 2030-31). The 
financial statistics of 15th Finance Commission period in respect of Department of 
Health Research shows that all through the period, the Department has never been 
allocated their Projected Demand. The Committee’s inference on the aspect is that the 
components of the schemes are getting compromised. The Committee, therefore, would 
expect that the Department is more realistic in their physical & financial proposals for 
the 16th Finance Commission period. The Committee recommends that the Department, 
on their part should adopt broad measures like concentrating on high-impact areas and 
aligning them with the national priorities, linking resources to measurable components, 
resorting to modern public financial management, turning to performance-linked 
incentives to retain skilled scientist/researches, etc. Thereby, the Department must 
enhance the performance of their schemes/programmes and be more assertive of their 
financial requirement.

According to the Department, although health research carries high social and economic impact, its allocation remains relatively modest within the broader science and technology funding landscape. Based on such assessment, a number of policy directions may be considered to strengthen and enhance the Department’s overall allocation in a structured manner. 2.4.4 First, health research may be recognised as a strategic national priority comparable to sectors such as space, atomic energy and industrial research. Given the demonstrated role ofbiomedical research in pandemic preparedness, disease surveillance, diagnostics, and indigenous technology development, it would be appropriate to adopt a missionfunding approach with a multiincremental budgeting. According to hough health research carries high social and economic impact, its allocation remains relatively modest within the broader science and technology funding landscape. Based on such assessment, a number of policy directions may be considered to enhance the Department’s overall allocation in a structured manner. First, health research may be recognised as a strategic national priority comparable to sectors such as space, atomic energy and industrial research. Given the demonstrated role of biomedical research in pandemic preparedness, disease surveillance, diagnostics, and indigenous technology development, it would be appropriate to adopt a mission-mode year assured funding framework rather than annual ntal budgeting. This would allow stable planning of clinical research networks, 
allocation at BE stage  4446.00 4795.97 4895.89 5385.00 5224.27 5636.46 5746.51 6323.41 6657.78 6765.62 

31 
 
biorepositories, cohort studies and translational research programmes that require long 
gestation periods. 
2.4.5 Second, the allocation mechanism may be strengthened through outcome-linked 
budgeting. DHR programmes such as diagnostic development, vaccine support, health-
technology assessment and public-health research directly influence affordability of care, 
reduction in disease burden and savings in public health expenditure. Systematic 
documentation of economic returns—such as cost savings to national health programmes, 
import substitution of medical technologies and support to indigenous manufacturing—can 
justify higher public investment in health research relative to traditional laboratory-based 
research sectors. 
2.4.6 Third, enhanced inter-ministerial convergence may be institutionalised. Creation of 
joint national missions for diagnostics, medical devices and clinical trials infrastructure may 
further strengthen this approach. DHR has already undertaken such initiatives. For example, 
Ayush - ICMR Centre for Integrative Health Research has been established as a joint 
initiative between the Ministry of Ayush and ICMR to generate robust scientific evidence on 
integrative and traditional medicine approaches through collaborative research, clinical 
studies, and policy guidance. Similarly, Med-Tech Mitra is a joint initiative by ICMR and 
Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) launched in December 2023. Over 
400 innovators, start-ups, and industry partners have been engaged through this platform 
helping them overcome challenges in the process of developing regulation compliant 
products, their clinical validation, and scaling-up.  
2.4.7 Fourth, a dedicated capital funding window for national health research infrastructure 
may be considered. While laboratory infrastructure is currently created through ICMR and 
national health missions, establishment of permanent national centres for clinical trials, 
regulatory science, health data analytics and evaluation of medical technologies would 
improve long-term institutional capacity and justify enhanced allocations. This may be 
complemented by public-private partnerships, especially for device testing facilities, bio-
banks and advanced laboratories. 
2.4.8 DHR’s HTAIn should be viewed as a prerequisite for efficient rollout of AB-PMJAY, 
National Health Mission, and national health programmes, and not as a discretionary research 
scheme. Evidence generation through it is a very small fraction of what it guides. Therefore, 
a fixed share of major centrally sponsored health schemes can be routed to HTAIn for 
evidence generation and budget impact analyses. 
2.4.9 Finally, strengthening translation and commercialization pathways may improve 
budget justification. Facilitating technology transfer, start-up incubation, and integration with 
the domestic pharmaceutical and medical device industry would demonstrate tangible 
economic benefits from health research investment. Evidence of improved programme 
efficiency, indigenous innovation, and reduced healthcare costs can support a case for 
progressively increasing the Department’s share within the national R&D expenditure. 
2.4.10 Already, DHR has proposed for Medical Innovations Development Acceleration 
Council (MIDAC) for steering translational research partnerships and technology 
development with focus on priority health conditions for the nation. It will support industry 
collaboration to bridge critical gaps in product development, assist researchers in validation 
and evaluation products and also manage royalty and equity for ICMR.  

32 
 
 
2.4.11  The Committee takes into account DHR's budgetary allocations for research 
activities vis-a-vis other key ministries and departments involved in research, such as 
the Department of Science and Technology (DST), Department of Biotechnology 
(DBT), Department of Space (DoS), Department of Atomic Energy (DAE), Ministry of 
Education and Ministry of AYUSH in the Union Budget 2026-27, is the lowest to DHR. 
Comparison of the similarly structured Ministry of Health & Family Welfare and 
Ministry of Education is more thought provoking, as both cater to infrastructural 
development as well as research. Even, Ministry of Education is spending 9% of its 
total allocation for research activities while Ministry of Health & Family Welfare 
shares only 4.8% with DHR. Having said that, the Committee observes that out of the 
total Government of India R&D outlay (all scientific ministries combined) for the FY 
2026–27 at ₹63,381 crore, DHR is allocated to the tune of Rs. 4,821.21 crore which is 
approximately 7.6%. When compared to 2017-18, the BE for the DHR/ICMR has more 
than tripled in 2026-27.However, the allocation over the years has been chequered. 
Whereas the growth in BE for CSIR/DSIR for the corresponding period has been 1.5 
times but their increase has been linear. 
 
2.4.12 Overall, the Committee feels that the DHR's share of budgetary allocations for 
research activities can be considered only as modest compared to other key ministries 
and departments involved in the field. Funding for the sake of enhanced budget 
allocation is not proper without relevant outcome. To improve/enhance its total 
allocation DHR has adopted many policy directions like recognition of health research 
as a strategic national priority comparable to sectors such as space, atomic energy and 
industrial research,  strengthening of allocation mechanism through outcome-linked 
budgeting, institutionalisation of enhanced inter-ministerial convergence, 
conceptualisation of a dedicated capital funding window for national health research 
infrastructure complemented by public-private partnerships, viewing of schemes like 
HTAIn as a prerequisite for efficient rollout of AB-PMJAY, National Health Mission, 
and national health programmes and not as a discretionary research scheme, etc. Also, 
DHR/ICMR should consider raising its own resources by tying up with major private 
players in the industry. The Committee is of the considered view that these policy 
directions of DHR can accelerate the pace of activities in the field of medical research in 
the country that would ultimately lead to global standard of healthcare. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that DHR should strive for Medical Innovations 
Development Acceleration Council (MIDAC) for steering translational research 
partnerships and technology development and equity for ICMR. 
 
Establishment Expenditure of the Centre 
 
2.5 Secretariat 
 
2.5.1 The Head- Secretariat is for establishment related expenditure on the Secretariat of 
the Department. The Budget Estimate for 2026-27 at Rs 51.80 crore shows a 10.8% increase 
over Revised Estimatefor2025-26 at ₹46.73 crore—the highest year-on-year jump. Further, 
the allocation under this Head has grown by 21.4% from Actual Expenditure (AE) 2024-25 
at Rs 42.67 crore to Budget Allocation (BE) of 2026-27 at Rs 51.80 crore.  

33 
 
 
2.5.2 On enquiry about the surges, DHR informed that the revenue expenditure object 
heads of Allowances, Office Expenses, Rent Rates and Taxes and Professional Services and 
capital expenditure heads of Information, Computer, Telecommunications (ICT) equipment 
and Furniture & Fixtures are the major contributors towards the 21.4% increase from AE 
2024-25 to BE 2026-27. The details of the increase are as under:  
 
1. Allowances - To cater to the increase in Dearness Allowance, House Rent 
Allowance and Transport Allowance from 2024-25 to 2026-27. 
2. Office Expenses - The meetings and conferences organised by the Department 
have increased both in numbers and magnitude. Further, there has been increase in 
number of outsourced support staff like DEO, MTS, housekeeping and security. 
3. Rent Rates and Taxes - The increase is due to increment in the rate of rent. 
4. Professional Services - In the SFC proposal for continuation of DHR schemes, an 
increase has been proposed in the number of employees in the PMIUs and their 
remuneration. Further, the Department as also hired Consultants and IT 
Professionals through NICSI. 
5. Information, Computer, Telecommunications (ICT) equipment – All the ICT 
devices which have exceeded or nearing its usable life are to be replaced in 
compliance with cyber security guidelines. 
6. Furniture & Fixtures – The office is likely to shift to a new location during the next 
FY which would require procurement of furniture. New furniture is also required 
to cater to the increasing staff strength of the Department. 
 
2.5.3 The Committee also sought an explanation for the Capital Expenditure for Secretariat 
being raised to ₹1.75 crore Budget Allocation (BE) 2026-27 from (RE) ₹1.36 crore in 2025-
26 along with the components under which the increase has been granted and the expected 
outcome, thereof.  
 
2.5.4 The reply of the Department was that the raise in Capital Expenditure from RE 2025-
26 to BE 2026-27 is attributable to increase in following object heads:

34

FY 2025-26 to the lessor, Indian Red Cross Society. 
 
2.5.6 The Committee takes cognizance of the reasons submitted by the Department on 
the increase of its allocation under the Head- Secretariat under various stages of 
allocation during 2025-26 and BE 2026-27. During the previous FY, DHR had funds 
under the Head kept aside for filling up vacant posts in the Department and acquiring 
an office premise of its own. The Department clarified that very little progress has been 
made on both the issues. The Committee has recommended for the filling up of the 
vacant posts and acquiring office premise of its own for the Department during the 
examination Demands for Grants, 2025-26, as well. It is discouraging to notice that the 
one of the premier Departments in a field as vital as Health Research of the nation is 
deprived of two basic infrastructural requirements, i.e., physical infrastructure and 
human resources. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Department puts 
forth a better plea based on practical solutions to rectify its HR issues and get itself a 
permanent official premise and avoid recurring expenditure on rent. 
 
2.6 Health Technology Assessment (HTA)  
 
2.6.1 From the year 2024-25, in Demand No 47 of Department of Health Research, within 
Establishment Expenditure of the Centre. a sub head ‘Health Technology Assessment’ has 
been included. There is also a similar component with the nomenclature ‘governance issues, 
including Health Technology Assessment’ under the Scheme Human Resource and Capacity 
Development. The Committee wanted to know reason for the reflection of ‘Health 
Technology Assessment’, separately, under two heads. The Department explained that it had 
initiated a pilot programme on HTA in April 2017 for evaluation of appropriateness and cost 
effectiveness of available and new health technologies in the country as part of “Research 
Governance” mandate of the Department. In 2021, it was approved as the Sub scheme of an 
umbrella scheme of Human Resource and Capacity Development and scheme had a budget 
line of 3103. Thereafter, in 2023, Health Technology Assessment India was approved as an 
Attached Office under Department of Health Research and 15 regular scientific and 
administrative posts approved by Department of Expenditure. A separate Budget line for the 
attached office under establishment expenditure was approved from 1st April 2024 with a 
budget of 17.85 cr.  
 
2.6.2 According to DHR, although HTA was part of Human Resource and Capacity 
Development Scheme in 15th Finance Commission cycle, given its distinctive mandate and 
requirement of sufficient technical and financial support, concept note of a separate scheme 
‘Integrating Evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme⁠ 
and Policy’ has been submitted to Department of Expenditure. With a proposed outlay of Rs. 
379 Cr (2026-27 to 2030-31), it will be implemented by HTA Attached office.  
 
2.6.3 The Committee takes into account the explanation furnished by the Department 
regarding reflection of HTA under two different heads in their Demand. HTA began as 
a pilot programme in April 2017 for evaluation of appropriateness and cost 

35 
 
effectiveness of available and new health technologies in the country and was later 
approved as an Attached Office of DHR in 2023. A separate budget line was given in 
2024. Now, given its distinctive mandate and requirement of sufficient technical and 
financial support, concept note of a separate scheme ‘Integrating Evidence-based 
Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme⁠ and Policy’ has been 
submitted to Department of Expenditure. With a proposed outlay of Rs. 379 Cr (2026-
27 to 2030-31), the scheme will be implemented by HTA Attached office. The 
Committee is in agreement with DHR and perceives HTA as a very important aspect of 
curating an effective ecosystem for ensuring quality and cost effectiveness of medical 
technology available the country through evidence-based mechanisms and recommends 
that DHR should effectively pursue approvals for the new scheme from the Ministry of 
Finance during the 16th FC Cycle. The Committee hopes that the accounting matter of 
reflecting HTA under two heads in the Demand No 47 of DHR will also be taken care of 
as HTA is now removed from the HRD scheme and the new scheme, namely,  
Integrating Evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in 
Programme⁠ and Policy to the implemented by the Attached Office is approved.   
 
2.6.4 As submitted by DHR, HTA is now removed from the HRD scheme and is proposed 
as a new scheme ‘Integrating Evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment 
in Programme⁠ and Policy’. A Concept Note for the same is pending with Department of 
Expenditure. The Budget over lay of the HTA attached office for the last 2 years is given 
below and thestatement indicating projected demand of the Department and BE allocated for

Further, as part of the oral submission related to the examination of Demands for Grant 2026-
27 of DHR, the Secretary also apprised the Committee on the merits of HTA that itmakes 
technologies available at lower cost to the population of India. He cited examples that 27 
medical devices were supported last year, projects were initiated for developing 28 devices 
and 52 diagnostics; cost for 11 technologies has been cut from half to 1/12th. 
 
2.6.11 The Committee observes that as of February 2026, no new separate HTA 
Scheme has been publicly notified. As on date, the new HTA Scheme titled ‘Integrating 
Evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme and 
Policy’ – 2026-27 to 2030-31 is awaiting final approval of Department of Expenditure. 
Whereas the old scheme HTAIn is continuing only as a sub-scheme under the umbrella 
Human Resource & Capacity Development programme of the 15thFinance Commission 
cycle. In this scenario, the Committee is apprehensive that this change over and absence 
of a dedicated, adequately funded HTA scheme risks funding gaps, stalled HTA studies, 
etc. The Committee, over the years, has repeatedly recommended for time-bound 
planning and better coordination while seeking approval to avoid administrative delays. 
In tune with this, keeping in mind the relevance of having HTA, the Committee 
recommends that the Department should approach the Department of Expenditure to 
expedite its approval and finalise a standalone National Health Technology Assessment 
Scheme considering the growing need for evidence-based prioritisation under national 
programmes like, Ayushman Bharat PM-JAY, AB-HWCs, etc. 
 
2.6.12. The Committee is of the firm opinion that the Department should revisit the 
proposed outlay of Rs. 379 Cr, if a standalone National HTA scheme is to be considered 

40 
 
in place of a mere expansion of the existing HTAIn sub-scheme for 2026–31. Taking 
into consideration the trend of allocation, the Committee notices that the Department 
hardly gets its Projected Demand for a scheme because even for the FY 2026-27, the 
proposed demand of Rs. 56.63 Cr was downsized to Rs. 25.00 Cr. Percentage of 
Variation of BE 2026-27 as compared to Projected Demand was 55.85%. The 
Committee, therefore recommends the Department to be more realistic and reasonable 
while making projections in consonance with requirement and in convincing the 
Department of Expenditure of their fund requirement with due justification. 
 
2.6.13 The Committee appreciates that the targets set under Health Technology 
Assessment for the FY 2025-26 have exceeded the mark except for the fact that no of 
policy briefs have been utilized by user agencies. The Annual Report -2025-26 of DHR 
shows that HTAIn has completed 136 studies. But the need of the hour is also that these 
studies are consumed by user agencies in the form of guidelines, uptake of technology in 
program and for procurement of devices/kits/technologies. Only then India’s goal of 
Atmanirbhar Bharat in health technology can be achieved taking the nation towards 
efficient, equitable and sustainable healthcare. To ensure that the new HTA scheme 
provides affordability, access and cost saving and  maximum value for every rupee, the 
Committee, therefore, recommends mandatory provisions of i) time-bound integration 
of every HTA recommendation into appropriate national health programmes and 
essential medicine/device lists within 6 months, (ii) periodic capacity-building for 
officials and procurement officers (iii)  introduction of a performance-linked funding 
mechanism tied to number of HTA studies translated into policy within 12 months. 
According to the Committee, scientific evaluation of drugs, devices and treatment 
protocols should be the guide for health policy decisions to ensure optimal utilization of 
public resources. 
 
2.6.14 The Committee desires that more Research Hubs & Centres be established 
under the new HTA Scheme, particularly in unserved /underserved regions such as 
Jammu & Kashmir, Ladakh, the North-Eastern Region (NER) and in the Islands. 
These States/UTshave region-specific health challenges like, high-altitude diseases, 
tribal health issues, difficult terrain, which are poorly represented in current HTA 
studies. At present, HTAIn operates with only 8 Regional Hubs and 20 Resource 
Centres, the majority concentrated in southern states. Now, the proposal is to establish 
another 50 centres/hubs.The Committee observes that establishment of physical hubs is 
just an initial step. Without appropriate infrastructure and trained manpower, 
evidence generation for underserved areas is not possible. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that each proposed new centres/hubs should have a core team of HTA 
professionals including epidemiologists, health economists, and clinicians. Also, these 
remote hubs will need sustained annual recurring expenditure for manpower, training, 
data systems, etc. Hence, the Committee strongly recommends that these facts are 
appropriately taken care of in the new schema of HTA.

3.1.1 An amount to the tune of Rs. 4,821 crore has been earmarked for the Department of 
Health Research for the FY2026-27. As compared to last year, the allocation of DHR has 
witnessed an increase of Rs. 893.57 crore. The Committee took cognisance that out of the 
increase of Rs. 893.57 crore, almost Rs. 850.00 crore is reflected against ICMR. The other 
components under DHR get an additional allocation of Rs 43.57 crore. While appraising the 
Committee with details of its allocation and utilization for the FY 2026-27, Secretary, DHR 
agreed that after deduction of Rs. 850.00 crore reflected against ICMR, the remaining is a 
small increase for other parts. He also assured that the Department is trying to get it 
increased. During the 16th Finance Commission, the Department have re-sent higher amounts 
based on requirement of work of the schemes and were hopeful that DHR will get some 
increase, or substantial increase, in the amount after the EFCs are done for the DHR schemes.   
 
3.1.2 As part of the examination of Demands of Grants 2026-27of DHR, the Committee 
has sought from the Department since the 16th Finance Committee Report has been tabled in 
the Parliament to provide the details of the recommendations pertaining to the schemes, 
programmes, funding, etc, of DHR. In reply, it was informed that no specific 
recommendation pertaining to DHR is stated in 16th FC report. 
 
3.1.3 The Committee, as part of the examination of Demands of Grants 2026-27, has 
appraised the Committee on the importance of the schemes of DHR and the fast 
progress of the schemes. The Committee feels that the meagre increase in the allocation 
for the schemes, apart from ICMR, is not enough considering the projects lined up 

42 
 
under these schemes by the Department. Further, while analysing the progress of the 
schemes under DHR, the Committee notes that certain schemes have been merged like 
Development of Tools/support to Prevent Outbreaks of Epidemics have been merged 
with VRDLs scheme and for other schemes like MRUs & MRHRUs draft EFC Notes 
seeking extension of the scheme for 16th FC period is awaiting approvals. According to 
DHR, the Department have re-sent higher amounts based on requirement of work of 
the schemes would be considered after the EFCs are done for the DHR schemes. 
Considering all these the Committee wants the DHR to fervently pursue the approval 
for their schemes and secure adequate funding so as to realise all the original objectives 
of the schemes to the best extent possible. 
 
Central Sector Schemes/Projects 
 
3.2 A. Establishment of Network of Laboratories for Managing Epidemics and 
National Calamities- Viral Research & Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) 
3.2.1 VRDL Scheme is implemented to strengthen laboratory-supported disease 
surveillance, outbreak investigation, and pandemic preparedness across the country. This is a 
Central Sector (CS) Scheme approved by the Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC) on 20th 
March, 2013. Against the target of 169 VRDLs in 15th Finance Commission, achievement of 
the scheme so far is a total of 167 VRDLs. The 167 VRDLs, comprises of 11 Regional level, 
27 State level and 129 Medical college level VRDLs, established till 23.1.2026 covering 27 
states and 6 UTs for creating requisite infrastructure in the Medical colleges/Research 
Institutes/Military hospitals etc. for timely diagnosis of existing and emerging viruses. A 
target of 02 more new VRDLs are proposed to be set up during the remaining period of the 
financial year. No has been finalized/ approved for 2026-27. More than 5 million samples 
have been tested and 2623 outbreaks have been investigated across the country so far. In 
addition, more than 60 million samples have been tested for COVID-19 by VRDL network. 
VRDLs were also involved in diagnosis of recent outbreaks of emerging/re-emerging viral 
diseases: Nipah outbreak (2018, 2019, 2021, 2023, 2025), Zika outbreak (2018, 2021), 
CCHF outbreak (2019, 2024, 2025), SARS-CoV-2 (2020), Measles outbreak (2022), Mumps 
(2024), Varicella Zoster virus (2024), Monkeypox (2024) and Avian influenza (205). 
3.2.2 VRDL serves as systematic sentinel surveillance platform in India for early detection of 
emerging/ re-emerging infectious diseases and there have been 2230 suspected outbreaks/ 
disease cluster reported, including high risk pathogens viz., MPoX, Nipah, Zika, CCHF, 
Avian influenza etc. So far 52 lakhs samples have been tested. A total of 1612 publications 
made and 23,591 personnel trained. DHR informed the Committee that 51 out of 165 VRDLs 
have established Next Generation Sequencing facilities.

3.2.3 The total allocation the Scheme dipped 6.5% from AE 2024-2025 for ₹60.99 crore to 
both BE at ₹57.24 crore and RE at ₹57.24 crore for 2025-2026. The Committee wanted to 
know the reason for reduction in the allocation during 2025-26, In response, the Department 
stated that the total approved cost of the Scheme during the 15th Finance Commission period, 
as approved by the Standing Finance Committee (SFC), is ₹324.60 crore. During the initial 
phase of implementation, i.e., 2021–22 and 2022–23, an expenditure of ₹90.77 crore and 
₹84.22 crore, respectively, was incurred. This phase coincided with the COVID-19 
pandemic, during which enhanced budgetary support was provided to meet unprecedented 
public health exigencies, resulting in higher allocations during those years. With the 
subsiding of the pandemic, major one-time capital investments have largely been completed, 
and the Scheme is presently focused on sustaining ongoing activities. Accordingly, the 
allocation for 2025–26 has been rationalised in line with the reduced balance available under 
the approved SFC outlay and the assessed programme requirements. 
3.2.4 For the 22.3% increase of BE 2026-2027 at ₹70.00 crore over 2025-2026 BE, DHR 
submitted that such incremental enhancement is done taking into consideration that the 
scheme of “Development of Tools / Support to Prevent Outbreaks & Epidemics,” which is 
proposed to be discontinued from the 16thExpenditure Finance Cycle, with its core 
components being integrated into the VRDL Scheme. The 48 VRDLs supported in PM-
ABHIM during 15th FC period will be supported through VRDL scheme during 16th FC 
period. 
3.2.5 For 2026–27, the Department informed that it had initially proposed a substantially 
higher Budget Estimate (BE) of over ₹180 crore, taking into account the expanded scope and 
scale of activities envisaged under the VRDL and Infectious Diseases Research & Diagnostic 
Laboratories (IRDL) networks. This expansion proposes inclusion of new components such 
as enhanced surveillance activities, support to IRDLs, NABL accreditation and External 
Quality Assessment (EQA), and contingency support for outbreak management. Many of 
these activities were earlier supported under the scheme “Development of Tools / Support to 
Prevent Outbreaks & Epidemics,” with its core components being integrated into the VRDL 
Scheme to ensure continuity and optimal utilisation of resources. 

45 
 
3.2.6 However, against this proposal, the BE for 2026–27 has been fixed at ₹70.00 crore, as 
the proposal for expansion of the Scheme is currently under examination by the Expenditure 
Finance Committee. The approved BE thus reflects a calibrated and conservative allocation 
aimed at sustaining essential ongoing activities with zero growth and no improvements. Any 
further enhancement in allocation, including to address emerging public health threats such 
as new viral strains, outbreaks, or national health emergencies requiring expanded laboratory 
capacity, will be considered after completion of the EFC appraisal and receipt of requisite 
approvals 
3.2.7 The Committee sought to understand the reason for no capital expenditure being 
earmarked for the Head considering that the Head is for setting up of a nationwide network 
of laboratories for managing epidemics and national calamities. In reply, the Committee has 
been informed that capital expenditure is proposed and released under the Scheme only when 
new VRDLs are sanctioned, covering infrastructure development and procurement of 
essential laboratory equipment. Thereafter, the Scheme provides recurring support for 
operational expenses of these laboratories. Further, during the 15th Finance Commission 
period, VRDLs that have completed five years of recurring support under the DHR Scheme 
are being transitioned to the PM–Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-
ABHIM) for long-term sustainability. Accordingly, 48 VRDLs are presently being supported 
under PM-ABHIM.Further, for the 16th Finance Commission period, it is proposed in the 
EFC that the recurring expenditure of IRDLs established under PM-ABHIM will be 
supported through the VRDL Scheme to ensure continuity and optimal utilization of the 
national laboratory network. 
 
3.2.8 The Committee has been given to understand that the 22.3% increase of BE 
2026-2027 at ₹70.00 crore over 2025-2026 BE of the scheme has been earmarked taking 
into consideration that the scheme of “Development of Tools / Support to Prevent 
Outbreaks & Epidemics,” is proposed to be discontinued from the 16thExpenditure 
Finance Cycle and its core components are to be integrated into the VRDL Scheme. 
Also, certain numbers of VRDLs are to be upgraded to IRDLs by including new 
components such as enhanced surveillance activities, support to IRDLs, NABL 
accreditation and External Quality Assessment (EQA), and contingency support for 
outbreak management. The funding pattern of the scheme suggests that the allocation 
over the past three years have been stagnant, but the utilization has been 100%. There 
has been a moderate increase of BE 2026-2027 at ₹70.00 crore against the projected 
demand of ₹180.00 crore thereby the percentage of variation of BE 2026-27 as 
compared to projected demand is 61.11 %. The Committee is of the opinion that it is 
disappointing, especially considering the significance of the scheme and some vital 
components are being merged with the scheme. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that with added responsibilities and structure of the scheme, the 
Department should make efforts to secure appropriate fund allocation under the 16th 
Finance Commission (2026-31) emphasizing the importance of investment in virology 
labs, genome surveillance and diagnostics to strengthen preparedness against future 
epidemics. 
 
 

46 
 
3.2.9 The Committee, further, observes the fact that capital expenditure is ‘nil’ under 
the scheme because capex is proposed and released under the Scheme only when new 
VRDLs are sanctioned. During the 15th Finance Commission period, VRDLs that have 
completed five years of recurring support under the DHR Scheme and for the 16th 
Finance Commission period, it is proposed in the Expenditure Finance Committee that 
the recurring expenditure of IRDLs established under PM-ABHIM will be supported 
through the VRDL Scheme to ensure continuity and optimal utilization of the national 
laboratory network. Accordingly, DHR apprised the Committee that no proposals are 
there in the pipeline for new labs under the scheme. The Committee observes that the 
map of VRDL labs shows gaps in the geographical spread of State level and regional 
level labs. India is a country with more than a billion population, extreme topographic 
and climatic diversity accompanied by rising zoonotic and climate-amplified threats 
like, Nipah, Dengue surges, Avian influenza, etc which require that the gaps of regional 
and State level labs across the country should be abridged. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that VRDLs should be upgraded to IRDLs and these factors may be given 
due consideration and even upgradation across the country should be undertaken. 
 
3.2.10 The Committee takes note of the achievements of VRDL scheme like Integrating 
of VRDLN portal with Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP) which has 
enhanced the public health surveillance and the launching of the Quarterly VRDL 
Bulletin at the National VRDL Conclave 2025 has provided a transparent platform for 
real-time data on viral infection trends. The Committee is of the view that the viral data 
base of VRDLs must serve as systematic sentinel surveillance platform in India for 
early detection of emerging/ re-emerging infectious diseases in the ensuing year, 
keeping into consideration the veiled threat of high risk pathogens viz., MPoX, Nipah, 
Zika, CCHF, Avian influenza etc.The Committee observes that such steps would ensure 
early-warning and containment of outbreaks.

3.3.3 The Department submitted that allocation under the Head nearly doubled in BE 2026-
2027 at Rs 171.35 crore in comparison to AE 2024-2025 at Rs 80.00 cr, due to additional 
items of expenditure proposed in BE 2026-27 in respect of MRU and MRHRU sub schemes

3.3.4 The Performance Evaluation Committee (PEC) constituted vide order dated 19th 
December, 2022 by DHR had made recommendations, that will help India in achieving 
higher standard in Health Research Infrastructure. Some important recommendations in this 
regard are- 
1.Creation of Pan-India network of National Institutes of Health Research 
2. Establishment of following new institutes: 
- National Institute of Ageing and Healthy Longevity 
- National Institute for NCD Basic Science Research  
- National Institute for NCD Clinical Research 
3. Multidisciplinary Research Units (MRUs) should be renamed as Multidisciplinary 
Medical Research Centres (MMRCs) and extended to eligible private medical 
institutions with proven research potential. 
4. Model Rural Health Research Units (MRHRUs) should be renamed as Population-
based Health Research Centres (PHRCs) and also established in urban areas to 
focus on urban health systems and population dynamics. 
5. Virus Research and Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) should be transformed into 
Infectious Disease Research and Diagnostic Laboratories (IRDLs) with enhanced 
capabilities for diagnosis, surveillance, and infectious disease research. 
3.3.5 In this regard, DHR has undertaken various research infrastructure activities in PM 
ABHIM . Four zonal National Institutes of Virology (NIVs) at Jammu, Dibrugarh, Jabalpur 
and Bengaluru and National Institute of One Health (NIOH) Nagpur are being constructed by 
ICMR. Construction of Satellite Centre of National Institute of Translational Virology and 
AIDS Research (ICMR-NITVAR) Pune has also been undertaken. Two mobile Biosafety 
Level 3 (BSL-3) vans have already been procured and deployed at National Institute of 
Virology (NIV) Pune and Regional Medical Research Centre (RMRC) Gorakhpur, two more 
are also being procured. Besides, 25 Viral Research & Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) 

48 
 
have been inducted for upgradation to Infectious disease Research and Diagnostic 
Laboratories (IRDLs), and 05 VRDLs have been inducted for upgradation to Biosafety Level 
3 (BSL-3) laboratories. 5 MRUs have been set up in private medical institutions and the 
recommendations of PEC pertaining to MRU and MRHRU have already been included in 
EFC Memo for 16th Finance Commission period. The other recommendations of PEC shall 
also be taken up by ICMR in coming year. 
 
3.3.6 The Committee is pleased to note that the Development of Infrastructure for 
Promotion of Health Research is one umbrella scheme which has been granted the 
Projected Demand for the FY 2026-27 of Rs 171.35 crore as BE 2026-27. Even though 
the BE 2026-27 of Rs 171.35 crore is lesser but the RE 2025-26, it is still a good start. In 
the opinion of the Committee, accurate assessment of gaps in financial resource with 
justified projections is the reason for the Department securing the Projected Demands. 
Hence, it feels that the Department has earned the Projected Demands for the scheme 
as the utilization over the years has been close to 100%. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends the Department to continue with the judicious utilisation of enhanced 
funds and build confidence in its capacity to deploy and absorb resources effectively for 
nationwide research infrastructure. 
 
3.3.7 The Committee, further observes a thrust towards the funding of MRUs at Rs 
100.27 crores in comparison to that of MRHRUs at Rs 71.08 crores for the FY 2026-
27.The Committee over the time emphasised on adequate attention towards both rural 
health research and multi-disciplinary research in medical colleges. But, when more 
thrust is being given to MRUs, it by default leads to rural areas being left out as these 
areas lack in government medical colleges compared to urban areas. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends more or at least equal thrust for both areas and to ensure apt 
push is given under MRUs to government medical colleges in rural areas. This would be 
in alignment with National Health Policy goals for more research in underserved areas. 
 
3.3.8 The Committee, keeping in mind the increasing life span of the Indian 
population, has highlighted the arising need for attention towards the problems related 
to ageing and geriatrics. In this regard, it is curious to know about the 
recommendations of the Performance Evaluation Committee (PEC) constituted vide 
order dated 19th December, 2022 by DHR on the establishment of a new institute- 
National Institute of Ageing and Healthy Longevity. National Centre for Ageing, 
AIIMS, is one of the government institutions which are currently dedicated for the 
cause. The Committee wants to be appraised on the progress made on this 
recommendation of the PEC. The Committee recommends that proactive steps shall be 
taken for the establishment of the National Institute of Ageing and Healthy Longevity 
with approved traditional and integrative medical practices. Also, DHR should 
collaborate with AIIMS to create protocol of treatment to address the problems related 
to ageing and geriatrics. That is so because AIIMS has elaborate infrastructure and 
treatment experience for ageing and geriatrics. 
 
 

49 
 
 
i. Multi-Disciplinary Research Units (MRUs) 
3.3.9 Originally conceptualized during the 12th Plan Period (2012–17) under the umbrella 
scheme Development of Infrastructure for Promotion of Health Research, the MRU initiative 
aims to bridge longstanding gaps in research capacity, laboratory infrastructure, and 
scientific output across medical institutions in India. Following successful implementation 
during previous plan periods—including an interim extension aligned with the 14th Finance 
Commission, the continuation of the scheme for the 15th Finance Commission cycle (2021–
22 to 2025–26) was approved. As of now, 123 MRUs including 5 MRUs in Private Medical 
Colleges have been established across various States and Union Territories. These units 
primarily focus on Non-Communicable Diseases (NCDs), while also addressing emerging 
public health challenges through clinical trials, multi-centre research, and capacity-building 
initiatives. The key objectives of MRUs are:- 
i. To strengthen the research environment within medical colleges by establishing 
dedicated, state-of-the-art MRUs. 
ii. To bridge infrastructural gaps that hinder the conduct of quality health research by 
creating well-equipped research facilities. 
iii. To build research capacity and human resources in the field of health research 
through training, mentorship, and hands-on project involvement. 
iv. To ensure equitable geographical distribution of health research infrastructure by 
establishing MRUs across underserved and underrepresented regions. 
v. To contribute to improved public health outcomes through the generation of 
evidence-based diagnostic methods, processes, and scientific knowledge. 
vi. To undertake clinical trials in accordance with the Allocation of Business Rules of the 
Government of India. 
vii To promote multi-centric research projects involving MRUs, medical colleges, and 
research institutions for addressing national and regional health priorities. 
3.3.10 Monitoring of MRUs is carried out at both the institutional and central level to ensure 
effective functioning, accountability, and alignment with the objectives of the sub-scheme. 
The Local Research Advisory Committee (LRAC)considers and approves research proposals. 
The Executive Committee ensures broad participation of all Departments in submitting 
research proposals to the MRU. At the central level, DHR administers and monitors the 
progress of MRUs through a structured framework, supported by the Programme Monitoring 
and Implementation Unit (PMIU) comprising of Project Manager, Finance Manager, 
Scientists, and other support staff.  
3.3.11 During the 15th FC period, 30 MRUs @ 06 MRUs per year were required to be 
established. A number of 12 MRUs were established during 2021-22. The status regarding 
establishment of MRUs during the last 04 years is as follows: 
Targets Achievements

2023-24 (06) Target already achieved

Assurance)

• 2723 Research Projects undertaken/initiated and 1526 publications issued in MRUs 
during 15thFinance Commission period up to 31.12.2025. 
 
3.3.12 The MRU sub scheme is presently approved up to 15th FC 2025-26. The proposal for 
extension of the MRU sub scheme is presently under consideration. The targets for the next 
03 years will get finalized after the proposal for extension of the sub scheme into 16th FC 
period get approved by the Competent Authority.  
 
3.3.13 The Committee sought to know about the financial or human resources issues that 
hampers the smooth functioning of various MRUs. According to DHR, a Third-Party 
Evaluation of the performance of MRUs has pointed out the following key financial or 
human resources issues that are relevant for smooth functioning of the MRUs:- 
1. To increase Consumables/Contingencies/Training (CCT) grant and shift to 
performance/requirement-based funding beyond uniform allocations in 
deserving cases. 
2. To ensure continued functionality and optimal utilization of infrastructure, 
financial support may be extended for civil repairs/maintenance of equipment in 
MRUs. 
3. To review and strengthened the human resource structure at DHR. 
4. To take following initiative with regard to human resources for smooth 
functioning of MRUs: 


51 
 
a) Organize periodic workshops for awareness, orientation and capacity 
building (both online and offline) for all stakeholders. 
b) Conduct capacity-building programs in research methods, data 
management, laboratory operations, etc. 
c) Develop and disseminate communication and advocacy materials through 
official DHR social media platforms and other outreach channels. 
The above-mentioned issues have been incorporated in the draft EFC Note prepared for 
seeking extension of the scheme for 16th FC period. 
3.3.14  The Committee notes that the MRU scheme has achieved the annual targets 
throughout the 15th Financial Commission period. The projected demand of Rs 100.27 
crores for the FY 2026-27 has been allocated with one of the targets being establishment 
of new MRUs. In this regard, the Committee observes that MRUs should be more 
evenly distributed across the country. The Committee finds from the map submitted by 
DHR on MRUs across India that there are gaps in areas like western and central India. 
The Committee recommends that the Department should consider these areas for the 
establishment of new MRUs. Further, as a significant portion of funds are allocated to 
extramural projects and MRUs, proper and timely feedback on their utilization and 
outcomes are to be ensured through a well defined Utilization Report System. 
 
3.3.15  One of the ways to improve the performance of MRUs according to a Third-
Party Evaluation is going beyond uniform allocations and shifting to 
performance/requirement-based funding in deserving cases. The Committee feels that 
the contention may contain merit but, in its opinion, to ensure even ground, the varying 
constraints under which different beneficiary institutions operate has to be 
appropriately factored in while deciding upon the parameters to assess the 
performance. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Department create a 
dedicated MRU monitoring cell under DHR with real-time tracking for both physical 
and financial progress for evaluating performance/requirement-based funding beyond 
uniform allocations. 
 
ii. Model Rural Health Research Units (MRHRUs) 
3.3.16 The Central Sector Umbrella Scheme for Development of Infrastructure for Health 
Research in 2013–14, comprises two sub-schemes: (i) Multidisciplinary Research Units 
(MRUs) in medical colleges and research institutes; and (ii) Model Rural Health Research 
Units (MRHRUs) in rural areas under ICMR responsible institutes. At present, 39 MRHRUs 
have been sanctioned across various States and Union Territories. The Scheme aims to: 
i.   To create infrastructure for transfer of technology to the rural levels for improving 
quality of health services to rural population. 
ii.  To ensure an interface between the New Technology Developers (Researchers in 
Medical institutions; State or Centre), Health Systems Operators (Centre or State 
Health Services) and the Beneficiaries (Communities in rural areas) and  
iii.  To ensure geographical spread of health research infrastructure in the country. 
3.3.17  Once the proposals for setting up of MRHRU are received by State Governments, 

based on proposal received, DHR designates a responsible ICMR institute to coordinate with the State Government concerned for finalisation of the proposal. Proposals are examined by a Technical Evaluation Committee (TEC) comprising of senior subject experts who furnish their recommendations to DHR. Final approval is accorded by an Approval Committee chaired by Secretary, DHR. Upon approval, a Tripartite Agreement is signed between DHR, the State Government, and the ICMR Responsible institute.3.3.18 A Programme Monitoring & Implementation Unit (PMIU) at DHR oversees overall administration and monitoring, centrally.at MRHRU level considers and approves research proposals. period gets approved by the Competent Authority. The Committee sought to know about the 
financial or human resources issues that hamper the smooth functioning of various 
MRHRUs. According to DHR, a Third-Party Evaluation of the performance of MRHRUs has 
pointed out the following key financial or human resources issues that are relevant for 
smooth functioning of the MRHRUs: 
1. To introduce flexibility in equipment and Consumables/Contingencies/Training 
(CCT) funding norms based on specific operational needs of MRHRUs. 
2. To ensure continued functionality and optimal utilization of infrastructure, 
financial support may be extended for Civil repairs/maintenance of equipment in 
MRHRUs. 
3. To review and strengthen the human resource structure at DHR for monitoring 
and guidance purposes. 
4. To provide security personal to safeguard infrastructure and operational assets.

for seeking extension of the scheme for 16th FC period. 
 
3.3.21  The Committee has been given to understand that increase in cost of 
construction of MRHRUs buildings under Civil Work is one of the components under 
the MRHRUs scheme for which allocation of Rs 71.08 crores for the FY 2026-27 has 
been made. Further, the Committee observes that as per report of a Third-Party 
Evaluation on the performance of MRHRUs, one of the areas of concern is ensuring 
continued functionality and optimal utilization of infrastructure, financial support for 
civil repairs/maintenance of equipment in MRHRUs. The Committee observes that the 
ICMR responsible institution is assigned a MRHRU project to coordinate with the State 
Government concerned and that institution oversees civil works and infrastructure 
development, procurement and installation of equipment, etc, of the project. In this 
regard, the Committee recommends that the ICMR be responsible to undertake a 
comprehensive annual audit of the MRHRUs project assigned to them, including actual 
expenditure versus sanctioned grants and submit the same to be made part of DHR’s 
annual report.  
3.3.22  Another area of concern is the human resource structure for MRHRUs. The 
prerequisite of MRHRUs are adequate staff strength with posts including clinicians, 
epidemiologists, bio-statisticians, technicians, etc., as lack of human resources would 
directly affect research outputs of MRHRUs. The Committee has been recommending 
hardship allowance and fast-track promotion for rural postings to attract more human 
resource. The Committee is reiterating its earlier recommendation for hardship 
allowance, fast-track promotion and other appropriate measures for rural postings. 
Adequate human resource is essential to strengthen research institutions in tribal and 
aspirational districts. This would ensure balanced regional growth and take care that 
will no region left behind in healthcare preparedness. 
 
 

54 
 
3.4 C. Development of tools/support to prevent outbreaks of epidemics 
 
3.4.1 The scheme Development of tools/support to prevent outbreaks of epidemics operated 
as a separate central sector scheme with a small budget to the tune of Rs.50.71 cr. for five 
years, which was launched in 2015-16 to support outbreak response &management and 
contingent needs during public health emergencies. This scheme is complementary to the 
VRDL scheme i.e. Setting up and strengthening of nation-wide network of laboratories for 
managing epidemics and national calamities” which runs concurrent in emergent situation. 
The scheme provides extra support to the VRDLs in times of outbreaks. Now, in the 
proposed VRDL EFC, this scheme will be merged into the VRDL scheme. This merger will 
streamline operations, optimize resources, and strengthen the VRDL network’s capacity for 
comprehensive laboratory-supported surveillance and timely outbreak response

(a) Human Resource Development (HRD) for Health Research 
Outlay (BE 2026-27): Rs. 105 Crore  
 
3.5.2 The Human Resource Development Scheme for Health Research is intended to create 
a pool of talented health research personnel in the country by upgrading skills of faculty of 
Medical Colleges/Institutes, mid - career Scientists, medical students, etc., by specialized 
training in priority areas of health research in leading national and international institutions. 
The trainees are encouraged to develop and take up research projects for addressing critical 
national and local health problems.  
 
The year-wise data (2021-22 to 2025-26) on Health Research Scheme is as under: 
 
3.5.3 There has already been over 85% utilization of Rs 93 Cr. allocated in HRD scheme in 
2025-26 till 5.2.2026. The DHR, has prepared an action plan to ensure at least 95% 
utilization of allocated funds under the HRD Scheme for 2026–27 and beyond. The key 
measures on Action Plan inter alia include: 
 
i. Timely Calls for Applications will be launched on the DHR website, supplemented by a 
structured social media campaign to ensure wide dissemination and participation. 
ii. Enhanced Technical Evaluation Capacity: The number of Technical Evaluation 
Committee (TEC) meetings, including component-specific TECs, will be increased to 
evaluate a larger number of proposals and minimize delays in processing. In order to 
incentivize professors from leading medical colleges and scientists of prominent 
research institutions, proposal for allowing provisioning of sitting fees to such TEC 
members will be sent to Ministry of Finance. 
iii. Monitoring and Mid-Course Corrections: Periodic monitoring of fund utilization and 
scheme progress will be undertaken, with mid-course corrections introduced wherever 
necessary to ensure timely completion and effective use of resources. 
iv. Capacity Building and Outreach: Special emphasis will be placed on capacity building 
workshops and outreach programmes to encourage participation from diverse

and achieve the targeted 95% utilization of funds under the HRD Scheme by 2026–27. 
 
3.5.4 The relatively low expenditure in the initial years of the Scheme was primarily due to 
the impact of the Covid-19 pandemic and foreign training programmes were suspended 
during this period. Medical colleges, being part of the frontline healthcare system, were 
extensively involved in Covid-19 management, which further contributed to the reduced 
activity under the Scheme during the pandemic years.. The reasons for the sudden increase 
observed in FY 2024-25 is due to the integration of ICMR’s capacity building programmes 
into the DHR–HRD scheme in January 2024. Prior to this integration, ICMR was operating 
its international fellowship programmes separately. After integration, a total of 65 
international fellowships were recorded, reflecting the consolidated numbers from both 
schemes.  
 
3.5.5 DHR has informed that the Guidelines of HRD schemes have been amended and 
scope of inclusion has been broadened in case of Startup Grant for Induction into Biomedical 
Research Scheme. Broadening of the scope of the Start Up grant has increased the number of 
applications and beneficiaries from biomedical and health researchers. While the applications 
granted support in the year 2024-25 were only 10, there was a quantum leap to 57 in the year 
2025-26 (approx. 5 times). There was a corresponding increase in the number of 
beneficiaries from the non- metro cities with 40% in year 2024- 25; to 71% in 2025-26. The 
awardees from Medical colleges also increased from 2 in the year 2024-25 to 8 in the year 
2025-26. 
 
3.2.6 Further, the Department has conducted a third-party evaluation through the National 
Productivity Council in the Financial Year 2025–26 to assess the reasons for poor proposal 
quality and low participation from young faculty and researchers in medical colleges. 
 
Major Key Findings: 
(i) Limited Awareness and Outreach: Many young faculty members were not 
adequately aware of the schemes and opportunities available under DHR; 
(ii) Capacity Constraints: Lack of structured mentorship and limited exposure to 
proposal writing and project management skills resulted in weak technical quality 
of submissions; and 
(iii) Time Constraints: Heavy teaching and clinical responsibilities left limited time for 
young faculty to engage in research activities.  
 
Follow-up Actions Initiated: 
(i) Enhanced Awareness Campaigns: Wider dissemination of scheme details through 
workshops, webinars, and social media outreach to enhance the participation; 
(ii) Capacity Building: Training programmes on proposal writing, research 
methodology, and project management targeted at young faculty and early-career 
researchers; 
(iii) Streamlined Processes: Increasing the frequency of Technical Evaluation 
Committee (TEC) meetings and simplifying application procedures to reduce 
delays; and 

57 
 
(iv) Mentorship Support: Guidance from experts for shortlisted potential applicants 
and helping young faculties in developing competitive proposals. 
 
3.5.7 Apart from social media posts and website advertisements, the Department of Health 
Research (DHR) has planned several targeted outreach programmes to enhance participation 
from medical colleges in Tier-2/3 cities, institutions in the North-East Region, and 
government institutions that traditionally submit fewer applications. These initiatives include: 
 
(i) Mass email communication to all NMC-registered medical colleges, including those 
in the North-East Region, with the objective of enhancing participation and 
increasing awareness regarding the provisions available under the HRD scheme 
(ii) Hand-holding support to applicants through the capacity-building initiatives, 
including grant writing workshops and training programmes on research 
methodologies. 
(iii) Webinars on a regular basis to disseminate information on open calls under the 
HRD Scheme. During these sessions, the HRD Scheme team will actively engage 
with applicants, providing clarifications and addressing queries related to the 
schemes and the application process.  
(iv) The communication channels of ICMR, including ICMR radio and institutional 
networks will be actively leveraged to disseminate information about the HRD 
Scheme. 
(v) a dedicated session on the provisions and opportunities available under DHR 
schemes is being incorporated into DHR-supported workshops conducted in 
Northeast region. 
 
3.5.8 DHR through its Fellowships abroad programme under HRD scheme supports 
advanced trainings of Indian scientists and researchers in foreign countries, with condition 
that they serve back in India utilizing the skills gained. To counter the challenge of “brain 
drain” and to retain skilled researchers in India, the DHR, has undertaken several specific 
initiatives aimed at providing better facilities and competitive career prospects comparable to 
international standards. The key initiatives include: 
 
(i) NRI/PIO/OCI Research Support Programme: This programme has been launched to 
encourage Indian-origin scientists working abroad to return and contribute to 
biomedical and health research in India. It provides contractual research positions and 
financial assistance, thereby leveraging the expertise of highly trained professionals 
with exposure to advanced technologies and methodologies. 
 
(ii) Training Fellowships: Researchers are supported through various training workshops 
and travel grants programmes for international exposure, enabling them to strengthen 
their skills and contribute effectively to national research priorities. 
 
(iii) Foreign Training and Repatriation Support: Faculties and researchers are facilitated 
for foreign training opportunities and are supported upon their return to India, 
ensuring knowledge transfer and capacity building. 
 

58 
 
(iv) Financial Assistance: The scheme provides a monthly stipend of Rs. 1.2 lakhs for 
three years, along with a research grant of up to Rs. Twenty lakhs per year to support 
research studies and projects. 
 
Outcomes Achieved (FY 2021–22 to FY 2025–26) include: 
 
(i) A total of 21 NRI scientists has been supported under the programme, with the 
highest number of fellowships (13) awarded in 2025–26. 
 
(ii) Notably, five fellows have secured regular positions in premier Indian institutions 
such as AIIMS Raebareli, PGIMER Chandigarh, IIT Kanpur, and NIPER Mohali. 
 
(iii) One awardee has further received the prestigious Ramalingaswami Fellowship. 
 
3.5.9 Research and Development is promoted with schemes like First in the World 
Challenge, where startups are made eligible to apply and compete for research grants from 
ICMR, VAIBHAV Summit, incentivises scientists and diaspora for "brain gain" and circular 
migration. Investment into world-class infrastructure, labs, and computing facilities to match 
global standard, and development of clear career paths, mentorship, and competitive 
compensation packages, especially in tech and academia. 
 
3.5.10 The Committee observes that DHR along with ICMR has done much in the post 
pandemic era under the Human Resource Development Scheme for Health Research to 
encourage health research among mid - career Scientists, medical students, etc. The 
hand-holding to applicants through grant writing workshops and webinars on a regular 
basis is quite appreciable. Further, the communication channels of ICMR, including 
ICMR radio and institutional networks have actively leveraged to disseminate 
information about the HRD Scheme among the researchers. The Committee, however, 
wants to be apprised about the parameters associated with First in the World Challenge 
scheme in R&D. 
 
3.5.11 The Committee notes that the Output-Outcome Monitoring Framework 
document of Ministry of Health & Family Welfare, shows Outputs 2026-27 for the 
number of fellowships awarded as 3100 and the number of fellowships awarded for 
women scientist as 30 under the scheme for the FY 2026-27. The difference in the 
number of fellowships awarded for women scientists against the total fellowships 
awarded is disappointing. The Committee recommends that special provisions for 
women, young NRI/PIO/OCI scientists/researchers and also scientists/researchers from 
underrepresented regions like the North-East and the Islands should be given more 
thrust. Further, DHR can also look into the possibility of collaboration with reputed 
private entities for co-funding fellowships, workshops, grants, etc, to reduce 
dependency on government budgets. Capacity building is another area where support 
of private entities can be factored in. The Committee also supports and recommends the 
steps taken towards reviewing the medical education curriculum to incorporate 
research training aspects among the future clinicians.
