3.5.19 The Committee queried ICMR on the methodology adopted and frequency 
maintained in the review/revision of uniform protocol cancer treatment and major changes 
made in the uniform protocol in the last five years. In reply, it was informed that Centre for 
Evidence for Guidelines under DHR has taken up the task of developing evidence-based 
national clinical guidelines for diseases. It is ongoing phased process. The first national 
evidence-based guidelines for lung cancer treatment and palliative care has been released 
with the objective of improving management, strengthening clinical decision-making, and 
reducing variations in care delivery across the country. 
3.5.20 The methodology followed for guideline development in Centre for Evidence based 
Guidelines, DHR involves three steps, i.e. collation of evidence from systematic reviews of 
existing literature on well-defined review questions, grading of evidence using GRADE 
approach to assess the certainty of evidence, and formulation of recommendations after 
applying of Evidence to Decision (EtD) framework which involves consideration of various 
contextual factors in addition to the evidence. The topics for guideline development are 
selected following a well-defined prioritization exercise chaired by Secretary, DHR. 
3.5.21 ICMR also informed that it has published a series of National Consensus Documents 
for Management of approx. 20 major Cancers in India between 2014 and 2020, covering 
major cancers based on national disease burden and clinical priority. While ICMR does not 
recommend “uniform cancer treatment protocol,” major national changes over the past five 
years has occurred through ICMR’s National Cancer Registry Programme (NCRP) and 
Activities Targets 
 
Establishing New DIAMOnDSR centres 03 
 
No. of patients tested for Breast and Lung cancer 3500 
 

63 
 
NICPR‑ICMR updates, which directly linked to national clinical guidelines. ICMR’s latest 
cancer burden, screening, and risk‑factor reports (2021–2024) improved evidence‑based 
decision making for treatment norms, especially for breast, cervical, childhood, and 
tobacco‑related cancers. In addition, new government‑ICMR initiatives—such as expanding 
district‑level Day‑Care Cancer Centres, exempting customs duty on key cancer drugs, and 
strengthening NPCDCS screening pathways—have standardized early detection and 
treatment delivery nationwide. 
3.5.22 Further, ICMR is strengthening evidence‑based oncology through priority trials, 
implementation models, and a large grants portfolio with measurable outputs. 
(i) NHRP portfolio (4 projects): (i) Stage II breast cancer care model across 13 states—
continuum‑of‑care including psychosocial support, rehab and follow‑up; (ii) ICMR–
NCG randomized trials call to scale high‑quality Indian oncology RCTs; (iii) 
prevention/screening for oral, breast, cervical cancer; (iv) Acute Lymphoblastic 
Leukemia project. 
(ii) Grants & outputs: 6 CAR, 35 Intermediate, 102 Small, 11 Cancer Consortium, 40 
RCTs, 77 Ad‑hoc, 104 Fellowships → 322 publications and 13 patents, advancing 
prevention, diagnosis, treatment and supportive care. 
3.5.23 In response to the Committee’s query whether ICMR has developed a protocol for 
cancer treatment in consultation with Tata Memorial and other leading cancer treatment 
institutions all across the global for uniform treatment of majority cancers occurring in 
India.DHR submitted that Centre for Evidence for Guidelines under it has taken up the task 
of developing evidence-based national clinical guidelines for various diseases including 
cancer. Tata Memorial Hospital is one of the Technical Resource Hubs of the Centre for 
Guidelines of DHR and is working on the evidence-based Guidelines for cancer. 
3.5.24 The Committee thereafter wanted to be updated on the status of Anti-cancer vaccine. 
In response ICMR submitted that the interest in the development of cancer is ever growing 
from a public health perspective. Efforts and scientific progress are being made globally in 
this field. At present, the availability of personalized mRNA-based vaccines for specific 
cancers and its clinical use will depend on the completion of ongoing trials and subsequent 
regulatory approvals. A generalised or universal “anti‑cancer vaccine” is unlikely to be 
introduced for public use in the coming year. It is important to distinguish these emerging 
therapeutic vaccines from preventive anti‑cancer vaccines, which are already established and 
in public health use. In this domain, the Government of India has taken a significant step 
through the planned national HPV vaccination campaign, which directly contributes to 
cancer prevention. The Government on 28 February 2026, has launched the HPV vaccination 
programme wherein all 14‑year‑old girls across the country will be offered a single dose of 
the HPV vaccine through government health facilities. This national initiative aims to 
substantially reduce the future burden of cervical cancer. 
3.5.25 The Committee takes cognisance of the work being undertaken by DHR/ICMR 
through the DIAMOnDSR scheme. According to the Department, the DIAMOnDS 
scheme focuses have been on lung and breast cancers. The Committee recommends that 
due focus maybe directed to other types of cancers as well as biomarkers.  
 

64 
 
3.5.26 On the Uniform Cancer Treatment Protocol, the Committee acquiesce with the 
submission of ICMR, being the field experts, that it does not recommend “uniform 
cancer treatment protocol”. Having said that the Committee observes that the 
treatment procedures and cost involved in a particular type of cancer in the country 
have wide gaps between a government institution and a private institution. While the 
Committee acknowledges that DHR does not have any say in the cost involved in 
treatment procedures, the Committee recommends the ICMR’s National Cancer 
Registry Programme (NCRP) along with the D/o H&FW should undertake a study to 
identify a way to address the issue of wide gaps in treatment procedures for a particular 
type of cancer between a government institution and a private institution. The 
Committee, however, is of the considered view that as a beginning, DHR should 
formulate treatment protocol for common cancers like oral, cervical and breast cancers 
and come up with a broad based for its treatment procedures and guidelines. Like the 
submission of ICMR, since National Institute of Cancer Prevention & research 
(NICPR)‑ICMR updates are directly linked to national clinical guidelines, the 
Committee perceives such a study would help to lessen the gaps. 
 
3.5.27 Regarding anti-cancer vaccine, the Committee has been impressed upon that 
efforts and scientific progress is being made in this field globally. The availability of 
personalized mRNA-based vaccines for specific cancers and its clinical use depends on 
the completion of ongoing trials and subsequent regulatory framework. The only anti-
cancer vaccination as of now available, both globally as well as in India, is the HPV 
vaccine. The Committee hopes that India, through ICMR, enters this field as the rising 
incidence of cancer among the population, especially among the youth, in recent times is 
alarmingly high. Accordingly, the Committee recommends ICMR should make efforts 
to make itself a part of on-going anti- cancer vaccination research projects globally in 
coordination with international entities like WHO and come forward for regulatory 
approval for clinical use.

3.6.3 Regarding the specific plans to utilise the BE of Rs. 351.06 crore for 2026-27 for 
Scheme, the Department stated that expenditure of some projects mentioned above is shifted 
to next FY. Further in the 16th FC period, new research infrastructure activities have been 
proposed- 
 

67 
 
(i) Setting up of 30 lnfectious Disease Research & Diagnostic Laboratories- These 
expand diagnostic capacity beyond virology to include bacteriology, mycology, and 
parasitology. 
(ii) Launch of "National lnitiative for Reliable Medical Device Development &

research data against emerging cyber threats. 
(iv) Establishment of National Virus Resource Facility (NVRF)- A central repository for 
authenticated viral strains is proposed to accelerate the development of indigenous 
diagnostics and vaccines during future public health emergencies. 
 
3.6.4 The Committee asked the Department to comment on the total allocation of funds 
under the Scheme in 2026-2027 &2025-2026 at₹351.06 crore and ₹350.49 crore, 
respectively, being stagnant as compared to a hike from 2024-2025 at₹ 212.21 crore. Also, 
whether the funding at ₹351.06 crore in 2026-2027 is sufficient for multi- sectoral and 
national platforms, given the interdisciplinary nature of One Health and Bio- security.  
 
3.6.5 As reply, DHR stated that the allocation for 2025–26 (₹350.49 crore) and 2026–27 
(₹351.06 crore) has remained broadly stable as the scheme is presently in a consolidation and 
transition phase. The objectives under the earlier plan period have largely been completed, 
and current expenditures are primarily directed towards committed liabilities and milestone-
based disbursements of projects already approved under the existing EFC/SFC framework. In 
2025–26, 207 ongoing research projects across thematic areas are being supported, and 
projects sanctioned in 2024–25 and 2025–26 are being implemented within the previously 
approved financial envelope; hence, annual allocations have not increased incrementally at 
this stage.Further, multi-sectoral/national platform components are being sequenced, with 
initial outlay kept conservative while foundational governance, interoperability, and 
institutional coordination are established. The requirement will be reviewed during the RE 
exercise, and allocations for 2026–27 may be re-assessed based on implementation progress 
and utilization trends. 
 
3.6.6 Regarding adequacy, DHR opined that ₹351.06 crore 2026-27 may be sufficient for 
sustaining ongoing projects and initiating incremental platform strengthening. However, 
given the interdisciplinary and cross-sectoral nature of One Health and bio-security, full-
scale national platforming and rapid expansion may require additional, phased investments 
and convergence with allied sectoral programs. Accordingly, funding needs will be reviewed 
at RE stage in line with the pace of platform roll-out and readiness of implementing entities 
 
3.6.7 Further, the Committee wanted to know the status of the scheme since PM-ABHIM is 
a Centrally Sponsored Scheme with a fixed terminal date of 31st March 2026. According to 
DHR, the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare had drafted an EFC Memo for continuation 
of PM-ABHIM Scheme at total cost of Rs.69,458.44 Crores for 2026-31. The same has been 
circulated for inter-ministerial comments. Apart from continuation of ongoing components 
till their completion, the draft EFC memo includes new proposed initiatives of DHR. 

68 
 
 
3.6.8 Reason for curtailment of the BE 2025-26 of Rs 350.49 crore to Rs 325.00 crores at 
RE for the Scheme was submitted as that the owing to procedural issues in (TSA) account 
opening for Research grants and lesser-than-targeted speed of some projects’ execution. For 
two consecutive years (2022-23 and 2023-24), the Department cited "inept tendering and 
procedural formalities" as the reason for large-scale unspent provisions under critical 
infrastructure projects (National Institute of One Health, Zonal NIVs, regional BSL-3 labs, 
etc.). In this regard, the Committee wanted to know the following:- 
 
a. The concrete administrative and procedural reforms introduced by DHR/ICMR 
since 2024 to prevent recurrence of such delays, specifically caused by tendering 
and approval processes, in the last year of the scheme. 
b. Revised timeline for each of the following pending projects: (a) National Institute 
of One Health, Nagpur (b) Four Zonal National Institutes of Virology (c) Regional 
BSL-3 laboratories (d) Upgradation of existing BSL-3 facilities. 
c. Specific incidents/bottlenecks identified as part of regular review meetings with 
CPWD and HITES, and the penalties, if any, imposed on these executing agencies 
for missing deadlines.  
 
3.6.9 In response, DHR submitted that in order to avoid tendering delays, ICMR has 
undertaken measures to involve experts and enhance its capacity to contribute to technical 
aspects of the tender. CPWD engineers have been given tours of NIV Pune to learn about the 
specialised bio-containment facilities. Validation responsibility has been taken up by ICMR 
to avoid approval delays and the phased inspection and approval process would be followed 
to ensure successful delivery of the project. Periodic meetings with CPWD at the higher level 
have also helped in enhanced scrutiny and tackling bottlenecks in the execution. 
 
3.6.10 On revised timeline for each of the pending projects: (a) National Institute of One 
Health, Nagpur (b) Four Zonal National Institutes of Virology (c) Regional BSL-3 
laboratories (d) Upgradation of existing BSL-3 facilities, DHR submitted as under:- 
 
(i) The physical progress of the NIOH Nagpur project is approximately 30%, and the 
tentative completion date assigned is 31 December 2026 
(ii) For ICMR-NIV South Zone, Bengaluru the physical status is 80% and revised 
expected timeline is 31 May 2026. 
(iii) For ICMR-NIV North Zone, Jammu the Change of Land Use has been accepted by 
the Jammu Development Authority and construction commenced on 14 July 2025. 
Currently the physical progress of the project is approximately 21% and the expected 
date of completion is 31 December 2026.  
(iv) For ICMR-NIV Central Zone, Jabalpur, the physical status is 60% and revised 
timeline is kept as 31 December 2026.  
(v) The physical progress of the ICMR-NIV East Zone, Dibrugarh is approximately 20%. 
It has taken time due to difficulty in successful tender, identification of the specialised 
agency for BSL-3/4 facilities, modifications suggested by consultant specialised 
agency and review and approval of the drawings due to cost implications The 
completion date is 12-16 months after the final drawings are approved and budgetary 
and cost implication issues are addressed.  

69 
 
(vi) ICMR has selected five VRDL that are strategically located for the upgradation of 
their laboratory infrastructure up to BSL-3 facility. The construction of the BSL-3 
facilities at AIIMS Raipur, AIIMS Rajkot and AIIMS Bibi Nagar are likely to be 
completed by May2026. The construction of the BSL-3 facility at AIIMS Rishikesh 
and GMC Kota is expected to be completed by December 2026. 
 
3.6.11 Regarding the incidents/bottlenecks identified and penalties imposed, it was informed 
that during regular review meetings with CPWD, HITES and other executing agencies, the 
following bottlenecks were identified: delay in identification of specialised BSL-3/4 
agencies, Revisions in drawings due to consultant recommendations, Budget escalation 
arising from GST increase and electrical scope changes, Change of Land Use (CLU) 
approvals (Jammu), Land documentation issues (Jabalpur),Adverse weather conditions 
(Dibrugarh), Limited participation in tendering (East Zone), Statutory and court-related 
issues. In certain cases, CPWD has withheld 1.25% of total tendered value (₹89,35,079) on 
account of delay. However, in projects progressing within revised timelines and where delays 
were attributable to statutory or approval-related factors, no penalties have been imposed. 
Regular follow-up is being undertaken with State authorities and executing agencies to 
mitigate further delays. 
 
3.6.12 The Committee has recommended that the number of mobile BSL-3 laboratories be 
"reviewed and upwardly revised" for greater inclusivity and coverage of remote/underserved 
areas. DHR-ICMR conducts regular reviews of all ongoing activities under its purview, 
including the deployment and utilisation of Mobile BSL-3 laboratory vans. During the most 
recent internal review, the requirement of Mobile BSL-3 vans has been specifically 
discussed. Based on operational experience and outbreak response needs, two Mobile BSL-3 
vans already in possession have been deployed at ICMR-NIV Pune and ICMR-RMRC 
Gorakhpur.  For the remaining two vans, ICMR has completed the tendering process and 
awarded the work to M/s Klenzaids Contamination Controls Private Limited and will be 
deployed at the Zonal ICMR-NIV, Bengaluru and ICMR-NIOH, Nagpur. These are adequate 
to cater to the entire country for outbreak response purposes. 
 
3.6.13 Since, the existing mobile BSL-3 labs are sufficient according to the current scenario, 
hence no additional Mobile BSL-3 units are proposed before March 2026. However, the 
requirement will continue to be reassessed periodically based on evolving epidemiological 
needs and outbreak situations. The procurement process for the remaining two Mobile BSL-3 
units has been completed, and delivery of these units is expected by March 2026, after which 
they will be operationalised following installation, validation, and personnel training as per 
prescribed biosafety norms. 
 
3.6.14 The Mobile BSL-3 unit stationed at ICMR-RMRC, Gorakhpur is positioned to cater 
to North India, while the Mobile BSL-3 unit at ICMR-NIV, Pune provides coverage to the 
western region of the country. The new Mobile BSL-3 vans that is to be deployed at ICMR-
NIOH, Nagpur and ICMR-NIV South Zone Bengaluru will cater to Central India, and the 
entire southern region of the country (including Lakshadweep, Andaman & Nicobar) 
respectively. For the eastern region, fixed BSL-3 laboratory are available at ICMR-RMRC, 
Bhubaneswar, ICMR-NIRBI, Kolkata, AIIMS Raipur and AIIMS, Patna, which together are 
assessed to sufficiently meet testing and outbreak response requirements in all eastern states 

70 
 
including Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand. Similarly, in the North-Eastern region, Zonal 
ICMR-NIV, Dibrugarh and ICMR-RMRC, Dibrugarh are equipped with BSL-4 facilities and 
BSL-3 facility respectively, capable of catering to the entire region. In the event of additional 
requirement, the Mobile BSL-3 unit stationed at ICMR-RMRC, Gorakhpur can be readily 
deployed to the North-Eastern States. 
 
3.6.15 The Committee takes into account the submission of the Department that the 
fund allocated under the PM ABIM scheme for 2025–26 of ₹350.49 crore and ₹351.06 
crore 2026-27 is broadly unchanged as the scheme is presently in a consolidation and 
transition phase. The objectives under the 15th FC plan period have largely been 
completed. The Committee also notes the submission of the DHR that regarding the 
adequacy of the fund of ₹351.06 crore of 2026–27 being is sufficient for sustaining 
ongoing projects and initiating incremental platform strengthening. Funding will be 
reviewed at RE stage in line with the pace of platform roll-out and readiness of 
implementing entities. The Committee, keeping into consideration the interdisciplinary 
and cross-sectoral nature of One Health and bio-security, full-scale national 
platforming and rapid expansion may require additional, phased investments and 
convergence with allied sectoral programs, recommends that DHR should persuade the 
Ministry of Finance to allocate funds as per needs at RE stage.  
 
3.6.16 Having acknowledged the statements of the Department, the Committee wants to 
point out that last year the Department had to review and revise timeline for each of the 
pending projects. The reasons being the delay in identification of specialised BSL-3/4 
agencies, revisions in drawings due to consultant recommendations, budget escalation 
arising from GST increase and electrical scope changes, change of land use approvals 
are some of them. The Committee feels that these technical/administrative delays will 
eventually culminate in budget escalation. In this regard, the Committee had 
recommended to streamline tendering/procurement and to fix responsibilities by 
imposing penalties. In view of the unchanged situation prevailing, the Committee wants 
to reiterate the same recommendation. Further, the Committee recommends the use of 
digital platforms for tendering/procurement, use Al technology for real-time 
surveillance of constructions of BSL -3/4 labs, VRDLs, etc. 
 
3.6.17  While appreciating the number of BSL-3 spread around the country as on date, 
the Committee feels that there are still areas which are underrepresented. Hence, the 
Committee recommends more BSL 3 labs in the scenario of rising zoonotic risks, 
climate-induced diseases, etc. The Committee also notes that there is only one BSL-4 lab 
in the country, in Dibrugarh. The Committee recommends that the Department should 
make efforts to construct at least three more BSL-4 labs in the 16th Plan period 
especially keeping in mind that the M/o H&FW has sought for Rs. 69,458.44 crores for 
2026-31for PM-ABHIM Scheme. 
 
 
 
 

71

3.7.1 The Projected Demand and he actual allocation of the scheme for the FY 2026-27 is 
shown below: -

• Collaboration of 13 Departments under PSA, DHR nodal dep. 
• National Institute of One Health 
• Integrated technology enhanced surveillance, outbreak inv. 
• Medical Counter measures development (NIPAH, KFD, Mpox) 
 
3.7.2 The Secretary, as part of his presentation on Demands for Grants 2026-27 of DHR 
informed that DHR is the nodal department for National One Health Mission. According to 
him, the Mission, which is under the leadership of Principal Scientific Advisor to 
Government of India, has multiple departments involved.  As of now there are at least13 
departments and more departments are being added; latest being ISRO. He also appraised 
that they are building a national institute of One Health in Nagpur which will be the home for 
National One Health Mission. 
 
3.7.3 The Department in its written submission also shared the key measures adopted to 
ensure co-ordination among different ministries to have maximum effectiveness of National 
‘One Health Mission’. They are as under: 
 
1. There is Steering Committee on One Health, headed by Hon. Minister for 
Health for setting the overall vision of the mission and reviews mission progress. There is 
also a Scientific Steering Committee on One Health was held under the chairmanship, 


72 
 
Principal Scientific Adviser to the Government of India which sets out scientific agenda 
of mission, reviews progress yearly and coordinates between different secretaries and 
heads of departments with the stakeholder ministries.  
2. There are also 4 Review and advisory committees, comprising of domain 
experts and external members set up for BSL3 lab network, Medical Countermeasures, 
Surveillance and Data sharing that are set up to review and offer recommendations on the 
work and recommend corrective measures in NOHM processes. Each R & C committee 
can also set up working groups for specific functionalities for a common objective or 
functionalities.   
3. All ministries also have a nodal officer who coordinates with PSA office and 
other department nodal officers, to coordinate scientific projects and pass information 
within ministries 
4. ICMR HQ, NIOH Nagpur and PSA office coordinate between ministries to 
inform all the ministries to invite scientific proposals on the different components of the 
mission for NOHM funding.  
5. ICMR-DHR has set up a PRC to examine the projects submitted by various 
ministries and examine inter-sectorality and recommend funding. The same PRC review 
project progress along with NIOH Nagpur.  
6. National Joint Outbreak Response Team is an outcome of NOHM 
intersectoral activity, for joint investigation of outbreaks comprising experts from NCDC, 
ICMR, DAHD, Wild life, Jal Shakti Vibhag and FSSAI. NJORT has so far contributed to 
several outbreaks like GBS Outbreak in Pune, Toxicity associated outbreak in Jammu, 
Nipah outbreak in West Bengal, ADD outbreak in Odisha. There have also been 2 mock 
drills conducted in India so far to introspect pandemic preparedness and response at 
national level in which outbreaks were simulated and system response have been 
independently assessed.  
7. The NOHM also has an network of BSL-3 labs, comprising 23 labs all over 
India, that are trained and equipped to test both human and animal samples, thus 
supporting outbreak response, faster turn around time and early integrated responses. 
3.7.4 According to the Department, the Mission has already been rolled out for integrated 
technology enhanced surveillance, for outbreak investigations and medical counter measures 
such as Nipah, KFD or MPOX. The National One Health Mission also involves creating a 
network of 23 Bio-Safety Level (BSL)-3 laboratories and forming the National Joint 
Outbreak Response Team (NJORT) with experts from relevant sectors. Other activities 
include surveillance projects at the animal-human interface, wastewater surveillance for 
antimicrobial resistance (AMR), and surveillance in slaughterhouses.  
 
3.7.5 The Committee observes that National One Health Mission has the involvement 
of around 13 Departments, and more may be added to the list in the future, also, the 
mandate of the Mission pans the whole of India. The Committee is of the firm belief that 
DHR, ably guided by the Principal Scientific Advisor to Government of India, is well 
endowed to be the nodal department for leading the mission to success. Nevertheless, the 
Committee recommends for measures like digitized procurement, expansion of 
VRDLs/IRDLs, unified platforms for real-time data accessible to all, domain specific 
research hubs, local community involvement at grassroot levels, etc., to avoid 
coordination delays and ensuring inter ministerial integration.  

73

3.8.2 The Committee sought comments from DHR about the increased BE allocation for 
North Eastern Areas under Developmental Heads in 2026- 2027 to ₹184.25 crore from 

74 
 
₹155.17 crore at RE in 2025-2026. In response, the Department stated that BE allocation for 
North Eastern Areas under Developmental heads in 2026-27 has risen in: 
1. Grant-in-Aid (General) due to increase in new initiatives and research projects 
in North-Eastern Region. 
2. Grant-in-Aid (Salary) for inflation adjustment and allowances. 
As the allocations of MRU, MRHRU, HRD schemes and ICMR has been increased in the BE 
2026-27. The NE allocation has also been increased accordingly keeping in view the 
earmarked fund for NE region. The Department also submitted the expenditure till 
31.01.2025 is Rs. 155.17 crore against Rs. 151.67 crore RE 2024-25. 
3.8.3 Further, the Committee queried to whether BE at ₹184.25 crore in 2026-2027 for 
North Eastern Areas under Developmental Heads is proportionate to the North East's 
population and health research gaps. In this regard, DHR commented that while the 
allocation is proportionate to the prescribed formula, the Department remains fully 
committed to addressing the unique population needs, health research gaps, and institutional 
capacities within the North Eastern States. In case additional requirements arise based on 
project proposals, research priorities, or State‑specific demands, the Department is open to 
consider enhanced fund support beyond the mandatory allocation, subject to availability of 
funds and due procedure. 
3.8.4 DHR also submitted before the Committee the efforts being taken to build research 
capacity in North Eastern Region. ICMR, is actively supporting investigator-initiated and 
priority research projects in NER. Currently, 19 ICMR-funded investigator-initiated projects 
are being implemented in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura, and Mizoram, enabling local 
institutions and researchers to build competencies in study design, implementation, data 
management, and dissemination. 
3.8.5 In addition, three National Health Research Priority projects have active sites in 
Assam, Meghalaya and Tripura, facilitating exposure to multicentre research protocols, 
standardized methodologies, and national-level collaborations. ICMR in collaboration with 
AIIMS Guwahati and state-level health authorities, launched the National Health Research 
Priority (NHRP) DIGI-CARE project—Digital technologies for optimizing the Continuum of 
CARE for Diabetes and Hypertension Management in the Public Healthcare System in India. 
As part of this initiative, a four-day Training of Trainers (ToT) programme was organized to 
strengthen research and implementation capacities of 65 field-level functionaries and 
researchers. The Discovery Division is also supporting one Centres for Advanced Research 
(CAR) on Bioengineered In-vitro Liver Models for Evaluation of Hepatotropic Infections 
and Therapeutics at Indian Institute of Technology, Guwahati. 
3.8.6 MDMS under ICMR has set up a Centre for Excellence (ICMR-DHR-CoE) at IIT 
Guwahati in 2021 for 5 years for promoting development of affordable and accessible 
indigenous Medical Device & Diagnostic innovations, aligned with Nation Health Priorities. 
VRDL scheme: During 2025-26, one Medical College–level VRDL has been sanctioned at 
the Nagaland Institute of Medical Science and Research (NIMSR), Kohima. Prior to this 
sanction, no VRDL was operational in the State of Nagaland. 
 
3.8.7 The Department has also undertaken several targeted measures to strengthen research 
capacity in the North Eastern Region (NER) under the HRD Scheme. DHR has implemented 

75 
 
multiple initiatives to enhance participation, expand outreach, and support research 
institutions across the NER. The key efforts are outlined below: 
 
(i) Special emphasis has been placed on supporting research in the North-Eastern States. 
During the FY 2023-24 and FY 2024-25, 33 fellowships amounting to Rs. 7.12 crore 
were awarded to 28 research institutes across the states of Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, 
Mizoram, Meghalaya, Nagaland and Sikkim. 
(ii) Social media campaign has been launched exclusively for North-Eastern region on 
various platforms like Twitter (X platform) and LinkedIn platform. 
(iii) Mass email communication has been initiated to all 706 NMC‑registered medical 
colleges, including those in the North‑East Region, with the objective of enhancing 
participation and increasing awareness regarding the provisions available under the 
Human Resource Development (HRD) Scheme of the Department of Health Research 
(DHR). 
(iv) Workshop on Grant Writing aimed at encouraging participation in grant and 
fellowship opportunities has been conducted for researchers and institutions in the 
North Eastern States of India. Webinar also conducted for the North Eastern States and 
disseminated the DHR HRD Schemes. The details are as follows:

3.8.8 The Committee acknowledges the work done by the Department under its 
various schemes in NER, especially setting up of VRDLs (Viral Research & Diagnostic 
Labs), activities under the HRD scheme, etc. Regarding the fund requirement, the 
Committee notes the submission of DHR that while the allocation to NER is 
proportionate to the prescribed formula, in case of additional requirements based on 
project proposals, research priorities, or State‑specific demands, the Department is 
open to consider enhanced fund support beyond the mandatory allocation, subject to 
availability of funds and due procedure. 
3.8.9 The Committee is aware that NER is prone to vector-borne disease burden due 
to its humid climate, dense forests and border proximity to high-risk areas. Considering 
this, the Committee recommends that ICMR should initiate investigator-initiated 

76 
 
research projects on vector-borne disease which can yield tailored vaccines, diagnostics, 
and prevention strategies, reducing mortality rates in NER. This would be a step 
toward the national campaign of Malaria Elimination Program.  
3.8.10 Another field in NER where DHR along with Ministry of AYUSH should invest 
is the cataloguing and testing of medicinal plants involving local universities and tribal 
communities. Such activities would be a boost to Traditional and Integrative Medicine 
practices. Research hubs for AI-driven telemedicine, mobile health apps, and remote 
diagnostics for chronic diseases are another area which can benefit the NER 
considering its terrain. NER is highly vulnerable to climate change, with frequent 
disasters exposing the people living there to conditions like malnutrition, hypertension, 
etc. Studies co-relating climate events and health outcomes, including mental health and 
nutritional security should be considered to understand and help people who are 
repeatedly exposed to natural disasters. Accordingly, the Committee recommends these 
initiatives from the part of DHR/ ICMR for NER. 
 
 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

77 
 
CHAPTER – IV  
INDIAN COUNCIL OF MEDICAL RESEARCH (ICMR) 
 
4.1 The Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), headquartered in New Delhi, is 
India's apex organization for formulating, coordinating, and promoting biomedical research. 
As one of the world's oldest medical research institutions, ICMR operates under the 
Department of Health Research (DHR) within the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, 
Government of India. Its research agenda aligns closely with national health priorities, 
focusing on communicable diseases, fertility control, maternal and child health, nutritional 
disorders, environmental and occupational health issues, and non-communicable diseases 
such as cancer, cardiovascular conditions, diabetes, blindness, and mental health disorders. 
 
4.2 The Secretary, DHR in his oral evidence related to the examination of Demand 2026-
27of DHR submitted that in BE 2026-27, the Department has got a budgetary allocation from 
the Government of Rs.4,821 crore. Out of which, on the allocation of Rs.4,000 crores for 
ICMR, he stated that this does not include any money from revenue or from any other source 
from within the ICMR.  This is the funding from the Government to the ICMR 
 
4.3 While explaining the ICMR budgetary system, the Secretary submitted that major 
portion is Grants-in-Aid General which is basically the money spent on research i.e. research 
projects and research institutions. The remaining amount is for the salary of ICMR scientists, 
technical staff and administrative staff, capital assets, etc.  He also informed that ICMR 
pensions are not paid out of salaries head but out of the Grants-in-Aid General. Under the 
research budget, there is intramural research, which means research in ICMR institutions, 
and there is extramural research, where ICMR funds more than 300 institutions in the 
country to do research.  
 
4.4 The Committee was also informed by the Secretary, DHR that ICMR was an inward-
looking organization in the past, but now it has adapted itself to be an outward looking 
organization. ICMR are now spending majority of their funds on extramural grants, which 
means that all the other medical and research institutions in the country are funded through 
ICMR.  According to the Secretary, DHR who is also DG, ICMR, that is a very substantial 
change. Further, before 2023, ICMR was doing largely descriptive research. After that there 
has been very substantial reduction in money being spent on descriptive research. As of now, 
ICMR is spending close to 40 per cent for development research that is, actual development 
of new technologies and their clinical evaluation. 
 
4.5 A graph showing the funding of ICMR from 2017, till now is given below:-

4.6 The Committee understands that over recent past, ICMR has shifted focus on 
two core areas – from descriptive to developmental research and gravitating towards 
extramural research instead of intramural. Such change in approach is appreciated and 
is viewed as a measure towards future readiness/preparedness to combat health 
challenges. The Committee is of the considered opinion that ICMR should continue 
funding more developmental and extramural projects so that larger numbers of 
research entities are encouraged to participate in the nation’s progress in health 
research.

4.7.5 The Committee notes the fact that out of the total allocation of DHR for the FY 
2026-27 of Rs.4,821 crores, an amount of Rs.4,000 crores is the share of ICMR. Further, 
it is also understood that amount does not include any fund from revenue or from any 
other source from within the ICMR but it is the budgetary allocation from the 
Government to ICMR. The submission of the Secretary, DHR that drew the attention 
of the Committee was that the ICMR pensions are not paid out of the salaries Head, but 
out of the Grants-in-Aid General which is basically the amount of money reflected to be 
spent on research i.e. research projects and research institutions. The Committee finds 
that the objective of Grants-in-Aid Scheme is to provide fundfor research projects. 
While Grants-in-Aid General Head cover funding for other items alongwith research 
project. The Committee seeks the Department to submit a clarification in the matter.  
 
4.7.6 The Committee is appreciative about the effective utilisation of funds by ICMR. 
Over the years ICMR has always made efforts for maximum utilization of allocated 
funds. This year the allocation is a remarkable hike from previous years, which is an 

81 
 
addition of Rs 850.00 Cr. Even though the projected demand for 2026-27 by ICMR was 
Rs. 4550 crores, it got an allocation of Rs 4000 crores. Considering that ICMR has 
demanded for more funds, the Committee reckons that it has more projects planned for 
the year. Nevertheless, going by the trend of last two years, ICMR has always been 
granted higher allocation at the RE stage. Of course, such increase at RE stage is not at 
par with the projected demand. Addition at RE stage suggests optimal utilization of 
allocated funds and project efficiency. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
ICMR keep their good track record of actual utilization of allocated fund and physical 
progress of its projects against measurable outcomes. 
 
4.7.7 On the Committee’s recommendation that ICMR is to be allowed to retain the 
revenues generated by them rather than depositing all of it to the Consolidated Fund of 
India, DHR had submitted that the revenue so generated is being appropriately 
recorded by ICMR Accounts section and shall be reflected in the Demand No 47. 
However, the Committee doesn’t find the refection of the same in the Demand No 47- 
2026-27. Accordingly, the Committee reiterates its recommendation on the issue. 
 
4.7.8 As part the examination of budgetary allocation of ICMR for 2026-27, the Committee 
sought a comparison of ICMR corresponding with research institutes of Developed countries 
on public funding of health research. The details so furnished by ICMR are that the 
Performance Evaluation Committee has often referred to corresponding Research Institutes 
of Developed countries while assessing the functioning of ICMR. It is noted that the U.S. 
National Institutes of Health receives about $47.77 billion annually (0.17% of GDP), the UK 
Medical Research Council $12.33 billion (0.36% of GDP), and Australia’s National Health 
and Medical Research Council $1.34 billion (0.07% of GDP). In comparison, India’s 
Department of Health Research (DHR) receives about$385 million annually, 0.01% of GDP. 
It is apparent that is inadequate for India’s scale, needs, future and potential. Further, the 
Performance Evaluation Committee’s prominent recommendations pertaining to its 
comparative study include- 
 
1. To improve research quality and impact, implement a peer review and monitoring 
system integrating constructive feedback, modelled on the NIH (National 
Institutes of Health), USA. 
2.  ICMR/DHR should enhance its presence, visibility and influence in the WHO 
system. Apart from continuous engagement, active participation at the World 
Health Assembly is of paramount importance to showcase India’s health research. 
3. ICMR should actively participate in other major global health forums, foster 
leadership roles in international research consortia, and increase representation in 
global policy and advisory bodies. 
 
4.7.9 The Committee notes that Performance Evaluation Committees of ICMR are 
formed every 5 years and the last committee was constituted in 2022. Its report reflects 
0.01% of GDP as Government’s spending on Health Research in India, But, on the 
examination of Demand for Grants of DHR during2026-27, it was informed that the 
Government spends 0.02% of GDP for Health Research vis-a vis U.S. National 
Institutes of Health receives about $47.77 billion annually (0.17% of GDP), the UK 

82 
 
Medical Research Council $12.33 billion (0.36% of GDP), and Australia’s National 
Health and Medical Research Council $1.34 billion (0.07% of GDP). The percentage of 
the Government’s spending on Health Research in India has been stagnant for the last 
5 years. Nevertheless, the statistics is disappointing. The Government is doing a lot of 
things in its attempt to make India a developed country. Viskit Bharat@47, which is a 
part of this attempt, is a revolutionary thought and to achieve that the nation need a 
young population as well as a healthy population. The Committee therefore 
recommends for higher budgetary support to DHR as percentage of GDP as 
comparable to leading Asian economies such as China, South Korea, Japan, etc. Efforts 
to be made to choke out a road map to bridge the research and investment gap by 
extracting measurable outcome from intramural and extramural project, especially in 
terms of publications, patents and clinical guidelines. The Committee further 
recommends for adequate budgetary support towards theoretic development advanced 
trails and indigenous innovations. Action plans should be chalked out for PPP based 
funding on mission mode to reduce long term dependency on expensive imported 
therapies.  
4.7.10 Every health scheme/program/research/study should be conceived as a step 
towards creating a healthy population who would directly/indirectly contribute to the 
GDP of the country. The Committee feels that at 0.02% of its GDP, India is at a very 
lower position compared to developed countries in the field of Health Research 
expenditure. As of now, India’s representation and contribution to the global health 
forums is not much remarkable. The Committee feels that India has no dearth of 
talents. Accordingly, the Committee recommends the Department to timely fulfil their 
commitments towards the on-going projects, which would in turn help them secure 
more financial assistance from the Union Government for completing their research 
projects in time. The Committee reiterates its recommendation that ICMR should be 
allowed to retain the revenues generated by ICMR rather than depositing all of it to the 
Consolidated Fund of India as well as collaborate with prominent private players in the 
field of medical research to enhance its funds. The Committee desires that ICMR 
should strive to raise extra budgetary resources upto 25% from other sources to fulfil 
its requirements for undertaking research projects. 
4.7.11 Further, the Committee endorses the suggestions of the Performance Evaluation 
Committee on having a peer review and third party monitoring system integrating 
constructive feedback as well as enhancement of India’s presence, visibility and 
influence in the WHO system through active participation in global health forums. 
Accordingly, the Committee recommends necessary steps to be taken in achieving the 
suggestions of the Performance Evaluation Committee. 
 
4.8 Physical outcomes of ICMR projects 
 
4.8.1 Subsequently, the Committee wanted to know the physical outcomes of ICMR 
projects during 2025-2026. DHR gave the details as under:- 
 
 
 
 

83 
 
Disease wise outcomes-  
 
A. Nipah Virus: During the Nipah outbreaks in Kerala, rapid containment and timely 
public health interventions have been facilitated due to the deployment of mobile BSL-
3 laboratories. A colorimetric isothermal (RT-LAMP) assay for rapid detection of 
Nipah virus has been developed and the technology has been transferred for 
commercialisation through Patent Mitra. Finally, monoclonal antibodies that can be 
utilised for treatment have been developed.  
 
B. Kyasanur Forest Disease (KFD): The KFD Virus Clinical Management Protocol that 
would provide a standardised framework for clinical management, diagnostic 
approaches, and therapeutic decision-making has been developed and finalised. Risk 
mapping of KFD using species distribution modelling that supports the implementation 
of targeted prevention strategies has been developed. An inactivated KFD vaccine 
candidate, which shows promising potential in preclinical studies has been developed. 
 
C. Leptospirosis: The identification of Leptospira as a major contributor to mortality 
associated with Acute Febrile Illness and Acute Encephalitis Syndrome in eastern Uttar 
Pradesh has led to the inclusion of leptospirosis testing within the diagnostic protocols. 
 
D. HIV: Results from Antiretroviral Therapy (ART) retention studies highlighted 
programme redesign with a focus on early engagement and integrated TB-HIV care 
aligns with national efforts to achieve the 95-95-95 benchmarks.  
 
E. Post Kala-azar Dermal Leishmaniasis: To support ophthalmological monitoring in 
PKDL, ICMR developed national and global databases. Field validation of three 
platforms for visceral leishmaniasis and PKDL, including qPCR-based test-of-cure, 
RPA suitcase laboratory, and cell-free DNA-based diagnostics have been completed. 
Finally, Infectious Diseases Data Observatory (IDDO)-Oxford database has been 
developed for VL, PKDL and VL-Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV) for data 
sharing and reuse. 
 
F. Dengue: Development of a molecular point-of-care pan-Dengue assay that can detect 
all four Dengue Virus serotypes using RT-RNA amplification with CRISPR/Cas12a 
fluorescence detection has completed. A paper-based dengue type-2 detection kit using 
77 blinded clinical samples has been validated. Results from the school-led dengue 
prevention under the Dengue Reduction through Education, Awareness, and 
Monitoring in Schools (DREAMS) programme has showed potential for scalability 
through community engagement and policy alignment.  
 
G. Tuberculosis: ICMR has validated and deployed three indigenous handheld x-ray 
technologies which supports TB screening among the underserved populations of the 
country. Various other diagnostic technologies like the Quantiplus® MTB kit, the 
UniAMP MTB Card, the PathoDetect™ and the CyTB skin test are in various stages of 
validation. DeepCXR has been integrated into the National TB elimination Programme 
(NTEP), to provide rapid, standardised and scalable interpretation of chest x-rays. An 
ICMR clinical trial on a modified BPaL (mBPaL) regimen established that optimisation 

84 
 
of the Linezolid dose improved tolerability while maintaining efficacy and enhancing 
treatment adherence among Multidrug-Resistant TB (MDR-TB) patients. The findings 
of the Reducing Activation of Tuberculosis by Improvement of Nutritional Status 
(RATIONS) trial reinforced the correlation between nutrition and TB prevention and 
care. In Tamil Nadu, the Tamil Nadu KasanoiErappilaThittam(TN-KET) care model, 
using a severe illness triage tool, utilised five clinical indicators to identify high-risk 
patients which led to 20% reduction in early TB deaths and 10% reduction in overall 
TB-related mortality. ICMR-NIRT has entered collaborations with national and 
international partners for paediatric TB clinical trials, drug-resistance surveillance, 
diagnostic innovation, vaccine immunology, and TB innovation hubs. 
 
H. Malaria: Two CRISPR-based ultra-sensitive platforms for simultaneous detection of 
Plasmodium falciparum and artemisinin resistance has been developed. The first 
recombinant multi-stage malaria vaccine (AdFalciVax) has entered the technology 
transfer phase, and industry engagement has been initiated. A MoA have been executed 
for malaria elimination research and Phase III long-lasting insecticidal net (LLIN) 
trials.  
 
I. Enteric Diseases: A multiplex two-tube real-time RT-PCR kit (En-ViroQPlex) for 
enteric viruses has been developed. A broad-spectrum glycoconjugate vaccine effective 
against typhoidal and non-typhoidal Salmonella, and an outer membrane vesicle-based 
vaccine against Shigella, have progressed to the technology transfer stage. Adjunctive 
strategies have been evaluated to test the potential of existing antibiotics, including the 
enhancement of ciprofloxacin activity against Shigella flexneri. Preclinical evaluation 
in BALB/c mouse models demonstrated the therapeutic potential of a newly 
characterised Shigella bacteriophage.A patent has been filed for a multiplex PCR assay 
that differentiates hypervirulent and classical Klebsiella pneumoniae. 
 
J. Sickle Cell Disease (SCD): Expansion of screening services of SCD in underserved 
populations has been achieved through the validation of 25 affordable Rapid Diagnostic 
Tests (RTD). A hydroxyurea dose-optimisation trial comparing 10 mg/kg/day and 20 
mg/kg/day which optimises treatment has been completed. To address stigma-related 
barriers to care among the tribal populations, a sickle cell disease stigma scale was 
developed. The BHARAT CARES initiative has been launched as a five-year national 
cohort to track SCD progression, treatment outcomes and long-term impact of the 
disease across the country. 
 
K. Cancer: National cancer surveillance has been expanded through a network of over 600 
cancer registry sites. Drone-based medical logistics has been deployed to support the 
rapid transport of corneal grafts and oncology samples. 
 
L. Cardiovascular Disease: Under the India Hypertension Control Initiative (IHCI), 
standard treatment protocols have been established in 157 districts across the country. 
A tele-ECG enabled ST-Elevation Myocardial Infarction (STEMI) care model that 
demonstrated the feasibility of thrombolysis at district and community health centres 
has by the Uttar Pradesh Government as HridaySetu, which is operational in 12 hubs 
across 46 districts.  

85 
 
 
M. Stroke: A public-sector Mobile Stroke Unit integrating CT-enabled ambulances with 
the 108-emergency system has been operationalised in Assam. Neurologist-led stroke 
units established across three hospitals enable early diagnosis and intervention. Stroke 
surveillance has been expanded through a network of over 100 stroke registry sites. 
 
N. Poly-Cystic Ovarian Syndrome (PCOS): Evidence from 75 high-impact publications 
informed updates to clinical guidance and lifestyle recommendations for the national 
PCOS cohort. 
 
O. Human Papilloma Virus: An indigenous HPV DNA point-of-care assay has been 
validated.

4.8.3 The Committee takes note of the progress made by ICMR in the previous years 
on various aspects like core research, research papers published, patents filed and 
patents granted, etc. The Committee also acknowledges that ICMR has contributed to 
addressing the health issues affecting the masses of the country. Nevertheless, the 
Committee is concerned to note that a portable X-Ray machine is being cited as being 
made under Make in India despite its important components like HV tank and X ray 
tube not being made indigenously. The Committee recommends that ICMR should set 
up a standardised guideline to classify its products under Make in India so that it forms 
a bench mark for the private Sector to imbibe and inculcate. Mere assembly of 
imported components should not form the basis for classification under Make in India. 
The Committee appreciates that ICMR has developed its own AI platform for rapid 
diagnosis of Chest and other X-rays. Accordingly, the Committee strongly recommends 
that this AI should be provided free of cost to all CHCs/PHCs and Government 
Hospitals so that primary diagnosis can be made under the TB Muktbharat campaign. 
The Committee also recommends that the pricing of AI needs to be justified in a 
country like India where AI can be a game changer to provide affordable health care on 
a population scale.

carried out by ICMR, through a Third-Party, to establish a structured mechanism to 
monitor and regulate the outcomes and overall impact of these initiatives. In this

4.9 Initiatives of ICMR 
 
4.9.1 As part of the oral evidence related to the examination of Demand 2025-26 of DHR, 
the Secretary, DHR submitted the plans of ICMR for 2026-27.  Some of the initiatives 
include  a TB Research Accelerator to further innovation-driven strategies for the next three 
years, a Science Excellence for Health through Agricultural Transformation which is a joint 
programme with the Indian Council of Agricultural Research to use agricultural 
innovations and understand their impact on health, Biopharma Shakti Mission to establish  
1000 clinical trial sites,  a Grant Challenge to fight Anaemia,  particularly in women and 
girls, Centre for Collaborative Research, strengthening our intramural-extramural 
relationships, and starting Waste Water Surveillance in 47 Indian cities for multiple viruses.  
 
4.9.2 The other initiatives undertaken under the umbrella of ICMR assubmitted before the 
Committee are as under: 
 
i. Medical Innovations Patent Mitra:  
4.9.3 The Medical Innovations Patent Mitra initiative was launched in 2025for supporting 
medical innovators with end-to-end guidance on patent filing, intellectual property 
management, and technology transfer to industry. It aims to accelerate commercialisation of 
public-funded biomedical innovations and strengthen India’s medical innovation ecosystem. 
As per the provisions of the Patents Act, 1970, the statutory processing timeline generally 
extends to approximately three to four years from the date of filing of the complete 
specification, subject to the prescribed examination schedule, issuance of First Examination 
Reports (FERs), submission of responses thereto, and compliance with all statutory 
requirements.In view of the average statutory timeline stipulated under the Patents Act, 1970 
and the rules framed thereunder, none of the patent applications filed under the Patent Mitra 
initiative have been granted to date, as such applications are presently at various stages of 
examination and prosecution.However, during the past three years, ICMR has secured 39 
patent grants, which emanated from applications filed during the period 2012–2022. 
 
ii. MedTech Mitra 
 
4.9.4 MedTech Mitra is a joint initiative by ICMR and Central Drugs Standard Control 
Organisation (CDSCO) launched in December 2023 to foster development of affordable and 
accessible indigenous therapeutics, vaccines, medical devices/ in vitro diagnostics, by 
providing strategic handholding support to MedTech innovators for clinical evaluation, 
regulatory facilitation and uptake of new products. Over 400 innovators, start-ups, and 
industry partners have been engaged through this platform helping them overcome 
challenges in the process of developing regulation compliant products, their clinical 
validation, and scaling-up. Since its inception, Medtech Mitra has provided support to the 
MedTech innovators as enumerated below:  
 

87 
 
(i) 24 technologies received test license as a step for regulatory approval processes. 
(ii) 24 technologies recommended for testing support in compliance with standards at 
ICMR-CAR-AMTZ centre 
(iii) 41 technologies recommended for clinical study protocol drafting and/or clinical 
evaluation 
Challenges faced by innovators applying on Medtech Mitra Platform:  
 
(i) Limited awareness of Medical Device Rules. 
(ii) Absence of clear and specific regulatory frameworks for Software as Medical devices 
(SaMD) & AI-based technologies. 
(iii) Limited no. of Medical Device Testing Laboratories (MDTLs) and CDSCO-approved 
laboratories for performance evaluation of IVDs. 
(iv) Restricted access to appropriate clinical samples required for performance evaluation 
of new IVDs. 
(v) Lack of funding opportunities for test batch manufacturing of medical devices/IVDs. 
 
Proposed solutions for these challenges are as follows: 
 
(i) Implementation of structured nationwide capacity-building programs for awareness 
on MedTech regulations for innovators and stakeholders. 
(ii) Development of dedicated, risk-based regulatory guidelines for SaMD and AI/ML 
medical devices aligned with global best practices. 
(iii) Creation of a nationwide network of public and private testing laboratories for testing 
of highly diverse In-vitro diagnostic technologies. 
(iv) Establishment of centralised biobanks and streamlined mechanisms for timely access 
to clinical samples. 
(v) Dedicated grant and shared manufacturing support schemes to bridge the gap between 
prototype development and commercialisation. 
Through the MedTech Mitra Initiative, 8 technologies have received support for 
obtaining manufacturing licences and are being commercialized. These 
technologies/products are mentioned below: 
a) CEREBO (Bioscan Research Private Limited): Detect Intracranial haemorrhages 
including Epidural, subdural, intracerebral, subarachnoid haemorrhages, and 
contusion and oedema including cerebral and cerebellar oedema. 
b) Spandan ECG (Sunfox Technologies Pvt Ltd): Portable AI Based 12-Lead ECG 
machine for Point of Care Diagnostics and Self Diagnostics. 
c) FibriTimer - Zeta 01: Multipurpose Haemostasis System for Small Throughput Labs- 
Diagnose and detects the abnormalities leading to Bleeding (Haemorrhage) and 
Thrombosis (DVT, DIC, MI, VT etc.) in a patient. It also helps in anticoagulation 
dosage management be it during surgery or oral anticoagulation management. 
d) Sample Collection Tube (M/s Lords Mark Industries Limited): The device is designed 
to collect samples which may be used for various purposes such as genetic testing. 
e) InoHeal (Inochi Care): The InoHeal wound healing device is a wound care device to 
be used for various types of chronic and acute wounds in healthcare settings under the 
supervision of a healthcare professional. 

88 
 
f) Mobilab (M/s Primary Healthtech Private Limited): It is a portable, in-vitro 
diagnostic device use for quantitative determination of multiple clinical chemistry 
analytes which help in the diagnosis of various disorders related to Kidney, Liver, 
Cardio-vascular, Diabetes, and Anaemia from the blood samples. 
g) AccuSmear (Accuster Technologies Pvt.Ltd.): Semi-Automatic Device used for 
making Blood Film or Peripheral Smear for Examination of the Blood Cells 
Microscopically. 
h) HELO MAXX (M/s Adarsa Private Limited): A Multi-parametric diagnostics device 
engineered to provide healthcare practitioners with valuable insights into a patient's 
health status swiftly and accurately. The device is supported with accessories like the 
ECG Cable, BP Probe, SPO2 Probe, High Accuracy Strain Gauge Smart Scale as well 
as consumables like Test Strips (Quantitative as well as Qualitative).   
 
4.9.5 The Committee recognizes the relevance of initiatives like MedTech Mitra and 
Patent Mitra. These are handholding measures for innovators for clinical evaluation, 
regulatory facilitation, execution of license agreements and uptake of new products. 
The Committee believes that the Department should make women, young and rural 
innovators primary beneficiaries in their handholding measures. In this regard, the 
Committee recommends phased funding at different levels of product readiness as 
innovators struggles to reach towards finished products/technology. There should be 
regional hubs related to these handholding initiatives to cater to local innovators which 
will also mitigate urban bias. The Department should seek to address the issue of low 
market penetration of new products/technology under these initiatives. This is mainly 
due to the mismatch between the demand and the final product. Last, but not the least, 
the Committee recommends effective capacity building programmes for nascent 
innovators about the various technical and procedural complexities involved in  market 
entry with new products/technology. According to the Committee, start-ups and 
innovators must be supported enough so India becomes a global hub for affordable 
health solutions. 
 
iii. Medical Innovations Development Acceleration Council (MIDAC) 
 
4.9.6 Medical Innovations Development Advancement Company (MIDAC) is envisioned 
as a Section-8 company under the Companies Act, 2013, MIDAC aims to serve as a catalyst 
for translational research and technology development. MIDAC will operate through the 
implementation of strategic schemes such as the Candidate Development Scheme, IP and 
Technology Transfer Facilitation Scheme, Clinical Validation and Evaluation Support 
Scheme, and MedTech Mitra Scheme. 
 
4.9.7 The Committee noted that MIDAC was introduced in Budget 2025-26 and wanted to 
know the status of the final approval for establishing MIDAC. In reply, DHR informed that a 
CEE/EFC meeting convened under the Chairmanship of Secretary, Department of 
Expenditure on 17.11.2025, appraised the Section 8 Company MIDAC’s proposal. The 
Minutes of Meeting conveying appraisal was received on 5th January 2026. In pursuance 
thereof, a draft Cabinet Note has been prepared and upon the approval of competent 
authority, has been circulated to the different Ministries/Departments concerned for their 
comments vide. OM No. M-11013/15/2023-CG(pt.)/8255524 dated 3rd February 2026, as per 

89 
 
the prescribed procedure. As soon as the inputs of different Ministries/Departments are 
received, the cabinet note will be submitted for final approval from Union Cabinet. All 
timelines and procedural requirements as laid down in O.M. No. 1/50/2/2024 Cab. Dated 
14.5.2025 regarding revised timelines for Inter-ministerial consultations have been duly 
followed. The time taken reflects the required stages of several inter-ministerial 
consultations, CEE/EFC consideration, budgetary alignments, and thoughtful deliberations in 
the preparations of the Cabinet Note.  
 
4.9.8 The Committee recalls that Medical Innovations Development Advancement 
Company (MIDAC) was presented before it as a transformative initiative of DHR last 
year during the examination of DFG of DHR. MIDAC, envisioned as a Section-8 
company under the Companies Act, 2013, aims to serve as a catalyst for translational 
research and technology development. Within five years, it intends to support the 
development of 25 regulation-compliant products, including formulations, vaccines, and 
medical devices, and 50 regulation-compliant diagnostics. However, the Committee 
finds that the much-anticipated company is still entangled in procedural matters 
connected to approvals. The Committee is of the considered view that DHR should not 
linger the formation of MIDAC, a Section-8 company under the Companies Act, 2013. 
It is apparent to the Committee that DHR has taken shelter behind the caveat of every 
procedural requirement being followed. In view of this, the Committee strongly 
recommends that DHR shall vigorously pursue the final approvals for MIDAC and 
ensure that the delay should not extend beyond a month with the Registrar of 
Companies and the Company must see the light of the day during the ensuing financial 
year. 
 
iv. Indian Clinical Trial and Education Network (INTENT):  
4.9.9 As part of examination of Demand of Grants of DHR, 2026-27,  the Secretary, DHR 
informed the Committee that ICMR - INTENT was initiated to establish a robust ecosystem 
for clinical trials by integrating institutions, investigators, and resources across India to 
conduct high-quality, multicentric, regulatory-compliant trials with 45 sites and later 
expanded to 75 institutions, including public and private medical colleges, hospitals, research 
institutes, and ICMR institutions.  
 
4.9.10 In this regard, the Committee wished to understand the role DHR played in regulating 
clinical trials and ethical guidelines for biomedical research in India. In reply, DHR stated 
that it registers Ethics Committees involved in the review of Biomedical and Health Research 
involving human participants through the NAITIK portal. This ensures that the registered 
Ethics Committees are appropriately constituted and provides accountability and 
transparency. DHR issues provisional registration for newly registered Ethics Committees, 
which is valid for 2 years, and final registration, which is valid for 5 years. DHR also 
monitors compliance through periodic review of submissions, scrutiny of documents, and 
renewal processes to ensure continued adherence to prescribed ethical and regulatory 
standards. 
 
4.9.11 On the Clinical Trials Registry - India (CTRI) established by ICMR, it was submitted 
that it registers trials prospectively, providing public access to necessary trial information, 

90 
 
reducing publication bias, selective reporting and strengthening public trust in 
research.  CTRI is one of the Primary registries of WHO's International Clinical Trials 
Registry Platform (ICTRP).  At present more than one lakhs trials have been registered with 
CTRI.  Mandatory disclosure of WHO Trial Registration Data Set items improves 
reproducibility and reliability of findings.  In order to further strengthen on accessibility, 
functionality, operational efficiency and transparency, the following reforms are being 
undertaken: 
 
a) Introduction of multilingual interface, mobile friendly platform,  
b) Mandatory registration tied to funding/publication,  
c) Awareness campaigns for public, researchers & institutions,  
d) Integration of CTRI, Ethics Registration (NAITIK portal) and DCGI portal using API 
Link 
 
4.9.12 Further, DHR Bioethics Unit works closely with WHO and its global partners to 
advance ethical governance, shape policy, harmonise frameworks, and support Member 
States on emerging ethical issues. Bioethics is a WHO Collaborating Centre for 
Strengthening Ethics in Biomedical and Health Research. Head, ICMR Bioethics Unit is 
currently serving as Chair of the Global Network of WHO Collaborating Centres in 
Bioethics, reflecting global recognition of India’s leadership in research ethics and its 
growing influence in international ethical standards and collaboration. ICMR is also an 
executive committee member of the Council for International Organisations of Medical 
Sciences (CIOMS) and contributes to reviewing international guidelines 
 
4.9.13 ICMR Ethical Guidelines provide comprehensive direction for conducting biomedical 
and health research in India. The ICMR National Ethical Guidelines for Biomedical and 
Health Research Involving Human Participants, 2017 have legal status under the New Drugs 
and Clinical Trials Rules, 2019, and mandate that all biomedical and health research be 
conducted in accordance with these guidelines. 
 
4.9.14 On the announced in the Budget 2026-27, about the establishing of over 1000 
accredited India Clinical Trial Site Network nationwide to boost medical research and 
innovation linked to ICMR priorities, it has n informed that the focus of Biopharma SHAKTI 
is on strengthening a large-scale clinical research ecosystem in the country, by developing 
1000 being utilised by the Indian Pharma industry. More than 200 sites spread across the 
country are conducting clinical trials funded by ICMR through its various schemes. These 
clinical trial regulatory-compliant clinical trial sites to conduct ethical, high-quality, 
scientifically robust, and efficient clinical trials. This will be enabled by skilled human 
resources, expanding clinical research infrastructure including digital platforms and a robust 
governance framework.  
 
4.9.15  Furthermore, to boost medical research and innovation aligned with health research 
priorities, ICMR proposes to leverage its prior experience and strength for creating and 
strengthening 1000 clinical trial sites, by adopting a ‘Hub and Spoke model’ to meet the 
needs of the Indian Pharma Industry. To develop a nationwide network of 1000 clinical trial 
sites, the Government and private hospitals, medical colleges, research institutions, various 

91 
 
networks and consortia, which are actively involved in conducting clinical trials, either 
investigator-initiated or industry-led will be mapped. The existing clinical trial sites will be 
assessed and upgraded, and new potential clinical trial sites will be developed, in a phased 
manner to achieve the goal over the period of 5 years. These sites will be diverse in their 
geographical location to conduct both hospital-based and community-based clinical trials in 
Tier 2, 3 cities and rural areas. Public-private partnership will also be actively encouraged in 
the proposed initiative of strengthening the clinical research ecosystem in the country.   
 
4.9.16 The Committee appreciates the Budget announcement 2026-27 regarding 1000 
clinical trial sites under BioPharma SHAKTI Mission with an outlay Rs 10,000 crores 
over five years indented to mark  a strategic shift towards strengthening India’s 
biopharma ecosystem. The Committee has given to understand that creation of a 
national network of 1000 clinical trial sites is one of the components of Biopharma 
SHAKTI which will be led by the ICMR. The Committee firmly believes Biopharma 
SHAKTI Mission, a core strategic intervention aimed at strengthening India’s 
biopharmaceutical ecosystem will definitely serve as an essential pillar required to 
meet the growing demands of the Indian pharma industry and to position India as a 
competitive global hub for biopharma innovation. The Committee in this regard, 
recommends ICMR to leverage its prior experience and strength by creating and 
strengthening 1000 clinical trial sites, and adopting a ‘Hub and Spoke model’ to meet 
the needs of the Indian Pharma Industry.  
4.9.17 The Committee reckons that to regulate clinical trials, DHR registers Ethics 
Committees (EC) to review Biomedical and Health Research which also involves human 
participants through the NAITIK portal. Further, the Committee also acknowledges 
the reforms undertaken by DHR to facilitate public access to necessary trial 
information, like introduction of multilingual interface, mobile friendly platforms, 
mandatory registration tied to funding/publication, awareness campaigns for public, 
researchers & institutions, etc. In this regard, the Committee holds the view that 
regulatory reforms and ease of doing research, while maintaining ethical standards, 
needs to be encouraged. Maintaining ethical standards itself is a challenge viz. hurdles 
include uneven Ethics Committees quality, delays, and vulnerability in multicentric 
trials, etc. Hence, the Committee recommends further strengthening of the role of DHR 
as a regulatory entity through frequent trainings for Ethics Committees members and 
participants, introduction of digital tools for monitoring and timely review of 
guidelines. ICMR should ensure that participation in clinical trials is based on genuine 
informed consent and all measures to ensure this shall be taken by DHR. 
 
4.10  Contemporary and Critical Areas of Research in India 
 
4.10.1 When the Committee enquired about the measures taken by ICMR to translate its 
expanding NCD research portfolio into public health interventions, viz. including its research 
outcomes with national initiatives on National Programme for Prevention and Control of 
Cancer, Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke (NPCDCS), etc. DHR furnished the 
following domain wise details: - 
 

92 
 
a) Oncology 
(i) ICMR, in collaboration with Tata Memorial Centre-ACTREC, developed a powder 
for oral suspension (PFOS) of 6-mercaptopurine (PREVALL), which helps in 
accurate dosing for children undergoing treatment for Acute Lymphoblastic 
Leukaemia (ALL).  
 
(ii) ICMR’s CARE-Clinical Pharmacology initiative focuses on therapeutic 
monitoring of anti-cancer drugs like sunitinib and docetaxel, which demonstrates 
that drug monitoring improves patients' quality of life (QOL) and reduces 
treatment costs.  
 
(iii) ICMR’s pilot project on screening and early detection of cervical, breast, and oral 
cancers in Cachar, Assam demonstrated the effectiveness of frontline health 
workers, such as ASHAs, in promoting home-based screening.  
 
(iv) Incidental Gall Bladder Cancer study provided information on prevalence and 
factors associated with IGBC and other premalignant conditions (chronic 
cholecystitis, hyperplasia, metaplasia, dysplasia, carcinoma in situ) so that an 
algorithm can be developed for early detection of GBC in India.  
 
(v) The pattern of care and survival studies (POCSS) provided information on 
improved care strategies and early detection measures. 
(vi) ICMR conducted multicentric studies on breast cancer that demonstrated that 
pesticide exposure, benign breast diseases, family history of breast cancers, family 
history of other cancers, age at menopause, and physical activity are considered 
important risk factors.  
 
(vii) ICMR evaluated the efficacy of multiple point-of-care systems for detection of oral 
potentially malignant disorders (OPMDs) and to develop a prognostic model 
combining clinical/ auto-fluorescence images, saliva markers, cytology, histology, 
and multi-omics data for relapse/recurrence.  
 
(viii) With a multi-centric approach, ICMR standardised the method of detection and 
criteria for positivity of Epstein B Virus (EBV).  
 
(ix) Additionally, for women’s cancers specifically, the “CONFRONT CANCER” 
project is empowering frontline workers in Punjab, Uttar Pradesh, and Meghalaya 
through educational resources and cluster Randomised Controlled Trials (cRCT) to 
prevent and manage malignancies.  
 
b) Cardiovascular Diseases 
 
(i) The Centre for Advanced Research (CAR) for cardiovascular disease (St. John’s, 
Bengaluru) has developed a synergistic, low-cost Intervention Package (IP) for 
CVD burden reduction. A key translation measure is the “task shifting” model, 
where Non-Physician Health Workers (NPHWs) are trained to deliver protocol-
based care.  

93 
 
 
(ii) India Hypertension Control Initiative (IHCI) in private sector: ICMR initiated 
IHCI in the private sector with the goal to generate evidence for the government, 
and other stakeholders to inform a targeted approach for effectively engaging the 
private sector for hypertension screening, diagnosis, management, and control.  
 
(iii) ICMR – STEMI ACT: The aim is to examine whether the creation of a network of 
hub (medical college) and spokes (secondary public health facilities - district 
hospitals and community health centres) in a district can improve thrombolytic 
rates in ST elevated myocardial infarction (STEMI) patients through a physician 
based tele-ECG-guided thrombolytic therapy model.  
 
c) Diabetes 
 
(i) ICMR’ Young Diabetes Registry is the largest national registry for youth onset 
diabetes in the world providing data on details of hospitalisation, prevalence of 
complications and co-morbidities at registration and stratified by diabetes type, 
treatment pattern and mortality.  
 
(ii) ICMR’s India Diabetes Study (INDIAB) has been completed across 37 States and 
Union Territories and provides the first comprehensive national and state-wise 
assessment of metabolic non-communicable diseases (NCDs) in India, covering both 
urban and rural populations.  
 
(iii) ICMR also validated the Indian Diabetes Risk Score (IDRS) as a cost-effective, 
population-level screening tool for identifying individuals at risk of diabetes and pre-
diabetes, strengthening its utility for large-scale public health programmes.  
 
(iv) Under CARE Young Diabetes, the PATHWAY trial improved transition from 
paediatric to adult care among adolescents with T1DM in India.  
 
(v) Under the CARE FIG trial, it was identified that provision of appropriate lifestyle 
interventions after childbirth to women with gestational diabetes improved the 
glycaemic status and or their weight.  
 
(vi) The study on comparison of seroprevalence of SARS-CoV2 among people with and 
without diabetes and/or hypertension among existing cardiovascular community 
cohort in Delhi provided useful information on the co-occurrence and interaction of 
COVID-19 and NCDs.  
 
d) Mental Health 
 
(i) ICMR’s multistate implementation research study on integration of screening and 
management of mental and substance use disorders with other non-communicable 
diseases (ICMR-MINDS). 
 

94 
 
(ii) In response to persistent gaps in community-based mental health care and limited 
skills among Community Health Officers (CHOs), a standardised, efficient, and 
actionable mental health management workflow and management cards have been 
developed for CHOs at the primary care level.  
 
(iii) The National Suicide Prevention Strategy (NSPS) of India aims to reduce suicide 
mortality through surveillance, integration of prevention services, and mental well-
being curricula in schools, while strengthening leadership, community resilience, and 
evidence-based research.  
(iv) ICMR Centre for Advanced Research on Digital Interventions for Mental Health Care 
has been established at NIMHANS, fostering research on digital mental health 
solutions. 
(v) ICMR Centre for Advanced Research on Addictive Behaviours (CAR-AB) is being 
established at AIIMS Delhi, with a focus initiative for delivering evidence-based 
interventions targeting excessive and problematic use of technology and associated 
stress, depression, anxiety, and addiction.  
 
(vi) ICMR Centre for Advanced Research on Neuromodulation, has been established at 
AIIMS New Delhi. The centre has validated the safety and efficacy of high-frequency 
repetitive Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation (rTMS) for patients with somatic 
symptom disorders and schizophrenia.  
 
e) Obesity  
 
(i) The ongoing SAMPADA study is generating the latest nationally representative data 
on the prevalence of overweight and obesity across different age groups in India, 
providing critical inputs for policy and programme planning. 
(ii) ICMR has undertaken systematic evidence collation based on high-quality systematic 
reviews published over the last decade to identify packages of community-level 
interventions that are effective for the prevention and management of obesity. 
(iii) In parallel, a national-level brainstorming with experts across the country has been 
scheduled to contextualize global and national evidence and support translation of 
research findings into public health interventions aligned with national NCD 
programmes. 
 
f) Stroke 
 
ICMR established a population-based stroke registry at Dibrugarh and 
observed that stroke incidence rate was 160 /100000 population in this region. An 
unusually higher rates of haemorrhagic stroke (65-70%) were observed in the stroke 
patients in this population. In view of this, ICMR with support from the Govt. of 
Assam, had initiated a research project to establish a clinical stroke care pathway for 
management of acute stroke cases at Dibrugarh and Tezpur. MSU (Mobile Stroke 
Unit) can play a crucial role at the sub-district (block or CHC) level, especially in 
rural and hard-to-reach areas as acute stroke is a time sensitive emergency and is a 
leading cause of death.  
 

95 
 
g) Geriatrics 
ICMR is building national capacity for healthy ageing through cohorts, task forces 
and international collaborations focused on dementia prevention and elder care. 
(i) SMRUTHI (cmRCT) cohort: 3,000 at‑risk elders via MRHRUs in Tripura, 
Himachal Pradesh, Karnataka, Rajasthan; multimodal dementia‑prevention
