bundle (early detection, locally appropriate nutrition, yoga, cognitive & physical 
activity) with materials in 4 languages; rural and tribal representation. 
 
(ii) Task forces & studies: pan‑India assessment of psychological & economic 
burden of dementia caregiving (Kolkata, Delhi, Bengaluru, Jodhpur, Dibrugarh); 
evaluation of Ayushman Bharat HWCs on out‑of‑pocket spending in Rajasthan, 
Karnataka, Tripura, Puducherry. 
h) Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD) 
ICMR’s CKD program integrates discovery, population surveillance and service 
innovations, with emphasis on CKDu hotspots. 
(i) Discovery & diagnostics: CAR at SGPGIMS Lucknow with two patents on urinary 
exosome miRNA biomarkers for early CKD and diabetic nephropathy differentiation. 
 
(ii) Burden & care: multicentric urban CKD study (2018) across 7 cities, n=15,372 → 
9.4% prevalence (city range 4.9–17.2%); medication‑therapy‑management study 
improved knowledge 42→73.5 and adherence/clinical outcomes. 
 
(iii) Genomics & precision care (ongoing): FSGS genetic panel standardization; 
multi‑institution Kidney Genetics Service for adolescent/adult monogenic disease; 
miRNA longitudinal markers; Alport syndrome whole‑exome and transcriptomics 
with genotype–phenotype correlation. 
 
(iv) CKDu hotspots & environment: HEAL‑CKDu (AP) linked heat exposure to impaired 
kidney function; studies on environmental toxins (metals, pesticides, water) in Central 
India; Udupi: validated CKDu risk questionnaire and ongoing prevalence & 
multimodal intervention study. 
ICMR has informed that the rapid shift towards urbanization, sedentary lifestyles, and 
unhealthy dietary habits has led to a significant rise in risk factors for CKD and liver 
diseases. Both diabetes and hypertension are leading causes of CKD, particularly through 
mechanisms like diabetic nephropathy and hypertensive nephrosclerosis. Environmental 
factors, including contaminated water and exposure to heavy metals, pesticides, and 
pollutants, have become significant contributors to CKD. The ICMR HEAL-CKDu project in 
Andhra Pradesh highlighted how occupational heat exposure, particularly in industries like 
agriculture, brick kilns, and construction, increases the risk of kidney damage, especially in 
areas with poor water quality. Additionally, studies on Chronic Kidney Disease of Unknown 
Etiology (CKDu) in areas like Uddanam and the microplastics research have shown how 
environmental toxins are linked to kidney dysfunction and disease progression. Preventive 
strategies like healthier diets, regular exercise, better control of diabetes and hypertension, 

96 
 
and addressing environmental toxins are crucial to reducing the growing burden of these 
diseases. 
 
i) Fetal anomalies 
 
ICMR has informed that under the National Health Research Priority (NHRP) on 
stillbirth, being implemented across seven states, the focus is on prevention of stillbirth 
through improving the uptake and coverage of evidence-based antenatal and intrapartum 
interventions. One of the strategies under this project is early detection of fetal anomalies by 
promoting uptake of recommended antenatal ultrasonography, particularly the second-
trimester anomaly scan, through improved utilisation of existing public health platforms. 
This includes early pregnancy registration and attendance at Pradhan Mantri Surakshit 
Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA) clinics to ensure that pregnant women receive the indicated 
ultrasonography for detection of fetal anomalies, and additional ultrasound examinations 
where clinically required. 
 
j) Hypertension 
 
ICMR has conducted a study on “Genetic Evaluation of Hypertension in the North East 
population during the period of 2015 to 2018.The goal of the study was to find association of 
the genetic makeup of vasoactive modulators of the human essential hypertension taking into 
account the level of various risk factors especially in Indian context with special emphasis on 
the Apelin (APLN), Apelin Receptor (APLNB), Guanine Nucleotide Binding Protein Subunit 
Beta -3 (GNB3) and Endothelial Nitric Oxide Synthase(NOS3) genes and associations 
toward predisposition to hypertension.  
k) Sickle Cell Disease (SCD) 
ICMR has played a pivotal role in advancing Sickle Cell Disease (SCD) as a national 
public health priority, directly supporting Government of India efforts under the National 
Sickle Cell Anaemia Elimination Mission. Through integrated research, diagnostics 
validation, and health-system strengthening, ICMR has generated actionable evidence to 
guide policy and programme implementation, particularly in high-prevalence tribal 
populations. With a special emphasis on tribal and underserved populations, CRMCH 
actively works in key frontier areas aligned with the objectives of the National Sickle Cell 
Anaemia Elimination Mission, thereby contributing to improved prevention, early diagnosis, 
treatment, and long-term care of affected individuals. 
l) Japanese Encephalitis (JE) and Snake bite 
ICMR published a White paper on venomous snakebite in India in 2020* which was 
acknowledged in the “National Action Plan for Prevention and Control of Snakebite 
Envenoming (NAPSE)”, launched by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare in March 
2024, with the aim to halve snakebite-related deaths and disabilities by 2030 in India. ICMR 
has concluded a multi-centric research study to estimate incidence, mortality, morbidity, risk 
factors and economic burden due to snakebite in India. This study is being conducted in 13 
states, 31 districts, 416 Blocks across the country covering nearly 7% population as of the 
2011 census. This study is expected to provide insight on the burden of snakebite along with 
risk factors, treatment pattern and health seeking behaviour, and socioeconomic fallout. The 

97 
 
final analysis and report of this study is being compiled and may be available in the next six 
months. ICMR–DHR has done significant research and public‑health interventions have been 
undertaken in Uttar Pradesh and Bihar to reduce mortality from Japanese Encephalitis 
(JE)/Acute Encephalitis Syndrome (AES) and snakebite envenoming. To address the chronic 
JE/AES burden in eastern UP, ICMR established the ICMR–RMRC Gorakhpur, originally a 
National Institute of Virology (NIV) field unit in 2008, now a full research centre conducting 
basic, clinical and operational research, including molecular and serological diagnostics for 
JE and other AES pathogens, directly strengthening early detection and case management in 
high‑burden districts. For snakebite envenoming, ICMR–DHR has launched India’s first 
large-scale, community‑based epidemiological studies through its National Task Force, 
covering 31 districts across 13 states including high‑burden regions of UP and Bihar to 
determine incidence, mortality, treatment‑seeking behaviour, and economic burden, revealing 
substantial pre‑hospital deaths and gaps in antivenom access. These findings directly 
informed the National Action Plan for Prevention and Control of Snakebite Envenoming 
(NAP‑SE), launched by the Union Health Ministry, which aims to halve snakebite deaths by 
2030 through a One‑Health approach involving universal antivenom availability, provider 
training, community awareness, and a pilot national snakebite helpline (15400).   
4.10.2 Almost all areas of research taken up ICMR are those which can bring in 
transformative impact in accordance with the National Health Policy outcomes. The 
Committee extends all its support to the ICMR Centre for Advanced Research on 
Addictive Behaviours (CAR-AB) being established at AIIMS Delhi, with a focus 
initiative for delivering evidence-based interventions targeting excessive and 
problematic use of technology and associated stress, depression, anxiety, and 
addiction. The Committee is of the considered view that mental health is an area where 
integration of Indian Knowledge Sytsem through methodology like yoga meditation can 
bring in lot of positive changes in behaviour science. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that both DHR/ICMR and Ministry of Ayush adopt steps to develop a 
treatment protocol wherein safe integration of traditional/ alternative treatment along 
with allopathic treatment can be effectively used in the area of mental health. 
4.10.3 The Committee recommends that ICMR should undertake a study to look into 
the possibility of developing indigenous Sickle Cell Anaemia from Sugarcane 
derivatives as has been developed in countries like USA/Japan.  Regarding 
hypertension, ICMR had conducted a study on “Genetic Evaluation of Hypertension in 
the North East population during the period of 2015 to 2018’, the Committee 
recommends that ICMR should also undertake a study on the impact of hypertension 
and diabetic among the people of India in the post pandemic period. Further, ICMR 
has informed that there are many studies from various groups in India on genetic 
factors related to diabetes. Study of genetics of monogenic diabetes is already in routine 
clinical practice. The Committee understands that millets help in the management of 
body weight, which is of utmost importance in diabetic patient. In this regard, the 
Committee desires that ICMR should undertake a study on the merits of the millet diet 
as part of promotion of its usage especially among children. Such studies should help 
including millet in the nutritional status of children in POSHAN Abhiyaan.   
 

98 
 
 
4.10.4 The Committee has been given to understand that ICMR has published a White 
paper on venomous snakebite in India in 2020* which was acknowledged in the 
“National Action Plan for Prevention and Control of Snakebite Envenoming 
(NAPSE)”, launched by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare in March 2024, 
with the aim to halve snakebite-related deaths and disabilities by 2030 in India. The 
Committee in this regard recommends that ICMR should identify the specify area of 
more snake bite and undertake research on various types of snakes bites especially by 
King Cobra, Russell’s viper, Karaita, etc which are highly venomous snake species and 
adequate arrangement be made for anti-venom doses in district hospitals and rural 
block hospitals.  Anti-venom doses of specific species of snakes may be stored in 
hospitals of the area where such species of snakes are found in plenty. 
4.10.5 The Committee, taking note of the fact that ICMR–DHR has done significant 
research and public‑health interventions in Uttar Pradesh and Bihar to reduce 
mortality from Japanese Encephalitis (JE)/Acute Encephalitis Syndrome (AES), 
however, the Committee observes that Japanese Encephalitis are much more prevalent 
in North India, ie, from Uttar Pradesh to North Bihar (from Muzafarpur to 
Gorakhpur and Gaya) and there is toll of death of children who are vulnerable to the 
disease. The Committee therefore recommends ICMR and DHR to undertake in-depth 
research in JE and desires that the government should give more grants to support the 
research projects on the subject. 
4.10.6 The Committee views that DHR should promote R&D under PPP model in 
order to extract maximum outcome for preventing the prevalence of various diseases in 
specific part of the country. The Committee desires that multinational companies or 
companies manufacturing drugs on large scale and earning high profit should be 
guided to make expenditure of certain percentage of profit on R&D with the purpose of 
reducing the cost of drugs and treatment of disease at an affordable cost on common 
diseases like JE, kidney failure, fatty liver, etc. The Committee is of the opinion that 
each regional AIIMS may be entrusted with health research on specific disease 
prevalent in that area with enhanced budgetary support to undertake R&D projects. 
The Committee notes that ICMR has informed that under the National Health 
Research Priority (NHRP) on stillbirth, being implemented across seven states, the 
focus is on prevention of stillbirth through improving the uptake and coverage of 
evidence-based antenatal and intrapartum interventions. One of the strategies under 
this project is early detection of fetal anomalies by including early pregnancy 
registration and attendance at Pradhan Mantri Surakshit MatritvaAbhiyan (PMSMA).  
The Committee in this regard recommends that the ICMR should expand the NHRP on 
stillbirth across the country instead of limiting it in just seven states. The Committee 
feels that the role of frontline workers likes Accredited Social Health Activist (ASHAs) 
and Auxiliary Nurse Midwife (ANM) is cardinal in early pregnancy registration and 
attendance at PMSMA, especially in rural/tribal areas accordingly adequate training 
should be imparted for ASHAs and ANM in this area.  
  
 

99 
 
(i) Non-Communicable Diseases (NCDs) 
 
4.10.7 The Committee was keen to know the strategy is in place to shift research focus and 
funding towards the rising burden of Non-Communicable Diseases (NCDs). In this regard 
DHR informed that India is experiencing a shift in disease patterns, with NCDs now causing 
nearly 60% of all deaths. Despite this, infectious diseases remain a significant concern. 
DHR/ICMR has responded with a dual approach that advances NCD research while 
maintaining a strong focus on infectious disease preparedness. 
 
4.10.8   ICMR coordinates the collection of data that is required to monitor the National 
Multi-sectoral Action for Prevention and Control of common NCDs. As part of this, ICMR 
has established large-scale studies and disease registries, including the national cancer 
registry. ICMR has also started fourteen National Health Research Programmes with NCDs 
as areas of focus, specifically in areas like ambulatory NCD care, mental health, cancer, 
health coverage, emergency care and development of digital technologies.As part of 
collaboration with national initiatives such as NP-NCD and Ayushman Bharat, ICMR has 
supported research that would improve prevention, early detection and management of 
NCDs. Research focus has further expanded to nutrition, lifestyle, environmental risk factors, 
and social determinants that are related to these NCDs.  
 
4.10.9 Concurrently, for communicable diseases, the National One Health Mission has been 
launched, to create a national network of existing and upcoming high-risk pathogens 
laboratories (BSL-3/4) labs across departments and keeping them functional through 
continuous R&D and use during outbreak response. Research on tuberculosis, malaria, 
emerging and re-emerging infections, and antimicrobial resistance have been advanced, and 
these areas are considered national priorities.The sub-scheme namely (i) establishing 
Multidisciplinary Research Units (MRUs) in Medical Colleges/Research Institutes, undertake 
research primarily in Non- Communicable Diseases. There is a provision of Rs. 288.11 Cr 
for the sub-scheme for the 15th FC period. 
 
4.10.10 The Committee takes into account the submission of DHR that ICMR has 
adopted a dual approach that advances NCD research while maintaining a strong focus 
on infectious disease preparedness. The Committee is given to understand that while for 
communicable diseases, the National One Health Mission has been launched while 
Multidisciplinary Research Units (MRUs) in Medical Colleges/Research Institutes 
undertake research primarily in Non- Communicable Diseases. Considering that India 
is experiencing a shift in disease patterns, with NCDs, causing nearly 60% of all deaths, 
the Committee recommends DHR to revisit the funding pattern and strategy by giving 
thrust to NCD research at the same time sustaining the intensity on infectious disease 
projects. The Committee recommends that preventive healthcare with focused research 
on early detection of lifestyle diseases and its affordable treatment should be 
prioritized. 
 
(ii) Anti-Microbial Resistance 
4.10.11 Over the last five years, ICMR has significantly revised national antimicrobial 
use and stewardship protocols. Major updates include the 2025 National Treatment 
Guidelines for Antimicrobial Use (Version 2.0) jointly released by ICMR–NCDC, which 

100 
 
standardized empiric antibiotic therapy and aligned national protocols with India’s updated 
AMR Action Plan (2025–2029). These updates collectively strengthened surveillance 
consistency across the ICMR- AMR Network and improved antimicrobial stewardship 
nationwide. 
4.10.12 ICMR’s stewardship drive has shifted hospitals from case to case antibiotic use to 
structured, data‑driven practice and is expanding Antimicrobial Stewardship Program 
(AMSP) beyond tertiary care to primary care with community engagement. 
● Hospital AMSP impact (20 tertiary hospitals): monthly point‑prevalence audits 
now in all 20 (from 3); 19/20 formed multidisciplinary AMS teams and conducted 
capacity‑building; 20/20 created antibiograms; 18/20 developed antibiotic policies; 
formulary restrictions rose 2→12 hospitals; prescription audit & feedback 6→16. 
● Tiered AMSP expansion: ICMR‑NIRBI’s tier‑specific AMSP framework aligned 
to NAP‑AMR 2.0 (by 2030); pilot hub‑and‑spoke AMSP at 6 rural primary‑care 
hospitals (WB) linked to district medical colleges. 
● Awareness & advocacy: WAAW (Nov 2025) webinar & AMR surveillance 
network report; UNEP–ICMR‑NIRBI report “Priorities for the Environmental 
Dimensions of AMR in India” released 24 Nov 2025; pilot IEC to improve 
antibiotic literacy; sustained advocacy through national conferences (e.g., Hopecon 
2025, IPHA). 
4.10.13 According to DHR, ICMR contributes significantly to the core objectives of the 
NAP-AMR 2.0 (2025–2029). This is channelled through specific research and surveillance 
initiatives. DHR/ICMR operates a national AMR surveillance network, which includes 
around thirty laboratories and hospitals. This network is crucial for continuously monitoring 
and mapping resistance patterns in key pathogens found in clinical settings, providing the 
evidence base for policy interventions. The plan mandates the expansion of genomic 
surveillance, a key research initiative to detect emergent resistance genes early. This 
scientific approach helps healthcare facilities respond to new threats effectively.  
 
4.10.14 Further, DHR is also involved in expanding surveillance capabilities to 
include targeted monitoring of antibiotic residues and resistant microorganisms in 
environmental samples, such as hospital wastewater, thereby integrating environmental data 
into human health research. Anchored by Centre for Cellular and Molecular Platforms(C-
CAMP) and chaired by the Office of the Principal Scientific Adviser (OPSA), with DHR 
involvement, the innovation hub serves as the innovation arm of the NAP 2.0. It fosters 
collaborations among national and international stakeholders to develop new technologies 
and solutions for AMR mitigation. Recognising the interconnectedness of human, animal, 
and environmental health, the DHR collaborates with other key ministries and departments, 
including agriculture, environment, and animal husbandry, to ensure research findings across 
sectors are integrated into a cohesive national response. Research data generated by DHR 
initiatives will inform the development of evidence-based strategies, ensuring that 
interventions are tailored to the specific context of antibiotic-resistant strains.  
 
4.10.15 Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR) identified as a major future threat is already 
impinging treatment protocols. DHR through ICMR operates an AMR Surveillance 
Network and contributes evidence on key resistant strains of pathogens found in 

101 
 
clinical settings. Also, the National Action Plan (NAP)-AMR 2.0 (2025-2029) emphasizes 
One Health surveillance and thereby integrates environmental data into human health 
research. In this backdrop, the Committee recommends that DHR/ICMR should 
establish AMR-specific hubs and promote community One health training, focusing 
inter alia on animal-human interface. The Committee also holds the opinion that the 
Department should also launch a national risk mapping app for zoonoses and integrate 
its findings into antimicrobial stewardship and protocols nationwide. 
 
4.10.16 Further, the Committee is given to understand that the use of 
technology/products like BIOFIRE are reported as a major step towards the 
antimicrobial fight. Patients who earlier had to remain in the ICU for around 10 days, 
with the help of BIOFIRE, can now receive antibiotic sensitivity reports within just 2 to 
4 hours, leading to faster and more targeted treatment. This has substantially reduced 
the overall cost and improved clinical effectiveness following its introduction. However, 
the Committee is told that the technology/products like BIOFIRE are currently 
imported and are expensive per se. Accordingly, the Committee recommends DHR to 
look into the possibility of developing such technology/products indigenously so that it 
can be made further cost effective and accessible more commonly and to smaller 
healthcare setups also as part of affordable health care inititiatives. 
 
(iii) HPV Vaccine 
 
4.10.17  The Committee desired to know the primary objectives & scope of the study titled 
"Antibody Response following One Dose of Cervavac® as compared to One Dose of 
Gardasil Vaccine in Healthy Girls Aged 9-14 Years: A Non-inferiority, Multicentre, Double-
blind, Randomized Controlled Trial" under ICMR.  
 
4.10.18  Replying to the queries of the Committee, DHR submitted that the primary objective 
of the study was to determine whether the antibody response elicited by one dose of 
Cervavac® (study product) is non-inferior to one dose of Gardasil® (comparator) against 
HPV types 16 and 18 among healthy girls in the age group of 9-14 years, 24 months post-
vaccination. Scope of the study are generation of evidence to aid in adoption of WHO’s 
single dose HPV vaccination strategy as a public health initiative by India and having an 
understating about the utilisation of a Make-In-India vaccine for the same adolescent age 
group.  
 
4.10.19 DHR added that Gardasil® was selected as the reference vaccine because it is a 
vaccine with robust clinical trial and real-world data, including evidence supporting 
immunogenicity following reduced-dose schedules. The vaccine is one of those 
recommended by WHO-Strategic Advisory Group of Experts (SAGE) for one dose schedule 
for girls aged 9-14 years, and thus has a well-established immunogenicity, safety and 
extensive global clinical use. Finally, both Gardasil® and Cervavac® contain virus-like 
particles targeting HPV genotypes 16 and 18, enabling a scientifically valid comparison of 
antibody responses based on similar antigenic composition and mechanism of action. 
Therefore, it was an appropriate reference for a non-inferiority design and allows rigorous 
assessment of whether a single dose of Cervavac® elicits a non-inferior antibody response in 
healthy girls aged 9–14 years. 

102 
 
 
4.10.20  Further, the Committee asked about how the results of this trial could potentially 
impact India’s national HPV vaccination program, especially regarding the use of the 
indigenous vaccine Cervavac®. Besides this, the cost and availability of Cervavac® 
(developed by Serum Institute of India) compare with Gardasil®, possibility of positive non-
inferiority results accelerating its inclusion in public health programs, etc were also areas of 
interest to the Committee. DHR submitted in this regard that the National Technical 
Advisory Group on Immunisation had requested ICMR to conduct this study as data 
regarding single dose of Cervavac® was not available in the public domain. If the results are 
positive, then this indigenously developed vaccine could be deployed in public health 
programmes. 
 
4.10.21 As informed by DHR, Gardasil® is available as a single dose of prefilled syringes 
and needs to be imported. As per the submission of DHR, the current market rate of 
Gardasil® single dose pre-filled syringe is around ₹3000- ₹4000. On the other hand, 
Cervavac® a “Make in India product”, is available in single as well as multidose vials. The 
current market rate of single dose vials of Cervavac® is around ₹1400 to ₹1800 with 
additional syringe and needles cost of ₹10 to ₹15 per dose. The Committee are however not 
sure of the exactness of the price of Gardasil®single dose pre-filled syringe vaccine available 
in India as on date.  
4.10.22 Regarding the economic benefits arising from developing a home-grown single-dose 
HPV vaccine and how it could reduce the price of the HPV vaccine and accelerate its 
integration into India's Universal   Programme, DHR submitted that Home-grown single dose 
vaccines, if proven to be non-inferior, would save import costs and duties, thus rendering the 
vaccination more affordable. These lower prices would make universal public rollout 
financially possible. If the country purchases the vaccine for the Universal Immunisation 
Programme, price negotiation would ensure that the costs are much lower for larger scale 
vaccination. Single dose strategy will help achieve coverage of greater than 80% which 
would make it cost effective. Additionally, internal transport costs are reduced if the 
manufacturer could deliver the vaccines to the major vaccination sites in the country while 
maintaining a cold chain. All girls between 9 and 14 years can be vaccinated in a campaign 
mode to achieve near complete coverage in this age group.  
4.10.23  Delving deeper into the matter the Committee sought to know how single-dose HPV 
vaccine impact cancer incidence among women in India. DHR informed the Committee in 
this connection that girls who are vaccinated before their sexual debut are less likely to 
develop precancers or cervical cancer that are due to oncogenic HPV type 16/18 after 30 
years of age. Provided >80% vaccination coverage is achieved in girls between 9-14 years of 
age, the impact of this vaccination programme can be observed one and half decades after the 
vaccination, when they have grown into the age group at risk. Globally, focus is placed in the 
use of nonvalent vaccines because of the identification of cancer-causing HPV that are not 
covered with the current quadrivalent vaccine in use.  
 
4.10.24 The Committee reckons the importance of national HPV vaccination 
programme. The Committee understands that ICMR is engaged in the "Antibody 
Response following One Dose of Cervavac® as compared to One Dose of Gardasil 

103 
 
Vaccine in Healthy Girls Aged 9-14 Years: A Non-inferiority, Multicentre, Double-
blind, Randomized Controlled Trial" to determine whether the antibody response 
elicited by one dose of Cervavac® (study product) is non-inferior to one dose of 
Gardasil®. The result of the trial would to aid in adoption of WHO’s single dose HPV 
vaccination strategy as a public health initiative by India. Further, the Home-grown 
single dose vaccines, if proven to be non-inferior, would save import costs and duties, 
thus rendering the vaccination more affordable as well as effective. The Committee 
feels that to make the trial result oriented, a transparent narration of the facts related 
to the merits of the vaccination connecting it to cervical cancer prevention to build 
public trust is important. It is also pertinent to make the public aware that the impact 
of vaccination programme can be observed one and half decades after the vaccination, 
when the recipients have grown into the age group at risk. The Committee firmly 
believes that HPV vaccination is a corrective and a way forward step. The delay in the 
roll out of the Universal Immunization Programme (UIP) maybe due to lack of 
information among the public. Hence, the Committee strongly recommends publishing 
of a detailed fact sheet on the ICMR/DHR website outlining the primary objectives and 
scope of HPV. The Committee is of the view that HPV vaccination if implemented with 
transparency and community involvement, the programme would emerge as a 
consequential public health achievement in protecting next generation of women from 
preventable malignancies, and thus reclaiming preponderance of prevention of cancer 
in national health policy. 
 
4.10.25 The Committee further recognises the fact that if the trial could demonstrate 
Cervavac®’s single-dose non-inferiority, it would be a "major fillip" by enabling faster 
rollout in the Universal Immunization Programme (UIP). Positive culmination of the 
trial would be an unequivocal boost to indigenous production and lower cost offers 
economic benefits like forex savings, job creation in biotech, etc. Single dosage can 
make UIP more successful as human lapses and follow up of multiple doses can be 
avoided. Accordingly, the Committee wants to render all its support to and pin its 
expectations on to the Department to act on the recommendations of the Committee.  
(iv) Oral Health 
4.10.26 Anant Muskaan, ICMR's National Health Research Priority Program, is aimed at 
promoting awareness of the importance of oral health in school children. According to DHR, 
these interventions are much more affordable than out of pocket expenditure for expensive 
oral diseases treatment in the country. 
 
4.10.27  As per the study conducted by Sharda et al., it was observed that the per capita cost 
of the School-Based Toothbrushing programme is lower than the out-of-pocket expenditure 
in real- scenarios in India. The per capita cost of the STB programme is ₹29,339 (95% CI: 
26,487-32,381) and lifetime caries experience with the programme is 2.72 teeth (95% CI: 
2.69–2.76) while the lifetime caries experience without the programme is 3.74 teeth (95% CI: 
3.69-3.78). Out of pocket expenditure studies reveal that ₹44,874 is saved per person with 
30% public treatment while ₹26,160 is saved per person with 100% public treatment. Thus, it 
has been observed that in real-world scenarios in India, the STB programme is expected to be 

104 
 
a cost-effective strategy for reducing the burden of dental caries and the associated treatment 
costs. 
 
4.10.28 According to DHR, the data collection process is still undergoing. The costing data 
will be collected at the end of one year of full-scale implementation (February 2026) and 
again at the end of two-year of full-scale implementation phase (February 2027). As per the 
data collected till date, the average estimated per-child annual running cost of the Anant 
Muskaan intervention is ₹79.96 (range: ₹53-₹105.46). This cost includes the toothbrushing 
kit, oral health education materials, and training and demonstration activities. The extension 
of the Anant Muskaan Project beyond the current seven districts will be considered upon 
successful completion of the two-year implementation phase (February 2027) and 
achievement of ≥80% coverage among primary school children. At the end of the two-year 
implementation phase, the Anant Muskaan Project is likely to be ready for national scaleup 
and will be handed over to the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare as the nodal agency 
for nationwide rollout.  
 
4.10.29 Anant Muskaan, ICMR's National Health Research Priority Program, is aimed 
at promoting awareness of the importance of oral health in school children. The project 
focus on primary school-based toothbrushing and oral health education to prevent 
costly oral diseases and reduce out-of-pocket expenditure. It is a cost-effective 
preventive intervention. The Committee perceives that the extension of the Anant 
Muskaan Project beyond the current seven districts would be considered upon 
successful completion of the two-year implementation phase in February 2027. The 
Committee recommends that the project be expanded nationally which would address 
the issue of oral disease burden and wants the project to be integrated with the School 
Health & Wellness Programme (SHWP), under the Ayushman Bharat. This would be 
an ideal move towards affordability over treatment. Also, the learnings gained from this 
programme can be extrapolated to other similar spheres to synthesise comparable 
benefits. 
 
(v) Food safety 
 
4.10.30 ICMR, through its 100 years of existence, has contributed significantly to nutrition 
science and policy in the country along with food safety. The following are some of them: 
 
1. Early studies on nutrient deficiency disorders: The studies on parboiled and milled 
rice helped to explain the incidence of beriberi among rice eaters in the east coast of 
Andhra region where milled rice was consumed largely and no incidence in Madras 
where parboiled rice was consumed more. 
2. Stomatitis associated with the deficiency of B group of vitamins, particularly 
riboflavin, was described for the first time in India, and a treatment with yeast and 
skimmed milk was found to be effective. 
 
3. Nutritive values of Indian foods:  The Nutritive Value of Indian Foods and Planing 
of Satisfactory Diets was published in 1937, which turned out to be a very popular 
contribution. Apart from this, the nutritive values of fruits and vegetables were also 
determined during 1960s. This publication was thoroughly revised and rewritten 

105 
 
with data obtained after updating some methods of nutrient estimation and was 
published as 'Nutritive Value of Indian Foods' in 1970s. More recently, the database 
profiling the nutritive values of Indian foods has been readied in 2017. The new 
'Indian Food Composition Tables (IFCT) 2017 is the compilation of nutritional 
information on 151 discrete food components for 528 key foods. 
 
4. Formulation of recommended dietary allowances (RDAs): The first 
recommendations on Recommended Dietary Allowances (RDA) were made for the 
Indian context in 1944. Later, the RDAs were modified in the early 1960s, and then 
in the early 1970s, 1980, 1991 and last in 2010. These are the recommendations of 
how much of the nutrients should be ideally consumed by people of different age 
and physiological groups based on their activity 
 
5. Dietary guidelines for Indians: A manual with information on the key practicable 
points for diets and physical activity for Indians to ensure optimal health and 
freedom from disease was proposed for the first time in 1998. In 2011, after the 
RDAs were revised and the guidelines were redrafted, the revised version was 
released. 
 
6. Studies on protein-energy malnutrition (PEM):  The institutes research paved way 
for a new hypothesis that malnutrition among underprivileged children was due to 
energy deficiency and not solely due to protein deficiency. 
 
7. Contribution to national programmes and policies:  ICMR-NIN’s research was also 
the basis for the Government of India in the 1970s, to launch a supplementary 
feeding programme to provide 300 Kcal/day to improve the nutritional status of 
children in rural areas and in urban slums, which was operated under the Integrated 
Child Development Scheme (ICDS). 
 
8. Contribution to the National Prophylaxis Programme against nutritional blindness 
due to vitamin A deficiency: ICMR-NIN studies paved the way for a nationwide 
vitamin A prophylaxis programme with its inclusion in the Fourth Five-year plan in 
1969. Based on the recommendations of the ICMR-NIN, it became a national 
programme and is being implemented throughout India. 
 
9. Supplementation of iron and folic acid to pregnant women: ICMR-NIN 
recommended that a programme of iron folate supplementation be taken up for 
pregnant women and preschool children as a prophylactic measure. The government 
introduced the National Nutritional Anaemia Prophylaxis Programme aimed at 
pregnant women and young children in 1970. 
 
10. Operational research to strengthen nutrition programmes: The Institute carried out 
operational research studies on Integrated Child Development Services (ICDS) to 
assess its impact on the nutritional status of children, including process and input 
evaluation. The study results helped in strengthening of the programme and 
subsequently, it was expanded in all the villages by universalizing ICDS services. 
 

106 
 
11. Development of Nutrition Surveillance System (NSS): In order to promote effective 
functioning of ICDS and nutritional well-being of the beneficiaries, the ICMR-NIN 
developed 'AAA' (triple A) approach covering Assessment, Analysis and Action 
involving the entire ICDS mechanism from the grassroots level to the supervisory 
level. This involved 'Assessment' of health and nutritional status on a continuous 
basis amongst vulnerable population groups, identifying area-specific determinants 
(Analysis) of nutrition problems and food insecurity and integrating various 
departments to take appropriate 'Action' in the prevention and control of 
undernutrition. 
 
12. Regular nutritional surveys: The National Nutrition Monitoring Bureau (NNMB) 
under ICMR-NIN carried out regular surveys in 10 to 16 States and generated a 
dynamic database on diet and nutritional status of the communities. Many surveys 
were regularly conducted among rural, tribal and urban populations. The most recent 
survey was the 'Diet and nutritional status of urban population in India and 
prevalence of obesity, hypertension, diabetes and hyperlipidaemia in urban men and 
women' released in 2017 
 
13. Balamrutham: ICMR-NIN in collaboration with State governments has developed 
micronutrient-fortified (iron, folic acid, B1, B2, B12, niacin, zinc, vitamin A, 
vitamin C, calcium) take-home ration called Balamrutham for 6-36 month old 
children, which is being implemented in all districts of Andhra Pradesh and 
Telangana. Earlier, the integrated State of Andhra Pradesh used to provide Modified 
Therapeutic Food (MTF) to children (aged 6-35 months) under Supplementary 
Nutrition Programme of ICDS. The ICMR-NIN conducted a study comparing the 
new ready-to-eat (RTE) formulation with the existing MTF and proved that it was a 
superior food supplement. This led to the introduction of Balamrutham, which 
reached the mothers and caregivers of over 10 lakh children in Telangana State. 
 
14. Food safety and toxicology: Studies on food toxins explored some major areas such 
as neurolathyrism, aflatoxicosis, argemone toxicity and epidemic dropsy and erucic 
acid toxicity in edible oil. Significant contributions have been made by the Institute 
in identifying the nature of the toxic factors and in providing solutions for prevention 
and control. 
 
15. Investigations on epidemic dropsy: In 1998, epidemic dropsy was reported from 
several States such as West Bengal, Bihar, Orissa (now Odisha), Madhya Pradesh, 
Haryana, Assam, Jammu and Kashmir, Uttar Pradesh, Gujarat, Delhi and 
Maharashtra, mainly due to consumption of food cooked in argemone oil mixed with 
mustard oil. The ICRM-ICMR-NIN carried out a study on epidemic dropsy 
prevalent in the areas of State of Madhya Pradesh and recommended to the 
government to stop the unauthorised use of adulterated oil. Selective cultivation of 
yellow mustard, strict enforcement of regulation and exemplary punishment to 
unscrupulous traders were followed as preventive measures. 
 
16. Knowledge, attitude, belief and practice (KABP) study on food safety: ICMR-NIN 
spearheaded the knowledge, attitude, belief and practice study on food safety in 
2005-06 which was conducted among households and other stakeholders such as 
doctors, regulators and teachers in 28 States of the country. 

107 
 
 
17. Non-communicable diseases (NCDs): Most recently, in 2017, the ICMR-NIN has 
generated data on the prevalence of overweight/obesity and non-communicable 
diseases (NCDs) in urban and rural areas from 16 States of India. The urban survey 
alone covered as many as 1, 71, 928 individuals from over 52,500 households of 
1097 wards of 16 States. 
 
18. Fenugreek seeds in the management of diabetes: It was found out that fenugreek 
seeds could improve the glucose tolerance and ameliorate other symptoms of 
diabetes mellitus (type 2). It was reported that the mechanism of action of fenugreek 
seeds on improving glucose tolerance in diabetes was due to improvement in glucose 
utilization at the cellular level.  
 
19. Research on edible oils: In the 1980s when there was a concern about the inadequate 
production of edible oil in India, the Government of India's policy of looking at 
promoting the use of unconventional edible oils got attention. Systematic studies on 
the nutritional quality and safety evaluations of unconventional edible oils such as 
rice bran oil were carried out at the ICMR-NIN. 
 
20. Recommended use of multiple oils: The relative role of n-3 and n-6 polyunsaturated 
fatty acids (PUFAs) in regulating the plasma profile was studied, and the desirable 
ratio of these fatty acids in Indian diets was worked out. Based on these studies, it 
was advocated to blend or use a combination of oils to reap the benefits of n-3 and n-
6 fatty acid form of different oils.ICMR- ICMR-NIN recommended the use of more 
than one oil as diverse sources of oils confer the additional advantage of providing a 
variety of minor components (present in non-glyceride fraction of oils). 
 
21. Health effects of trans fats: Based on the studies of the ICMR-NIN  the Food Safety 
and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) fixed the norms for trans fatty acid 
content of vanaspati, limiting to 10 per cent, and proposes to bring it down to five 
per cent and to two per cent or lesser than that in the next couple of years. 
 
22. Double-fortified salt (DFS): ICMR-NIN's research efforts focused on developing a 
technology for double fortification of salt with both iodine and iron to combat both 
IDD and iron deficiency anaemia (IDA) led to the development of a successful 
formula after exploring several stabilizers to ensure the stability of iodine on storage, 
no discolouration and bioavailability of both micronutrients when taken with food. 
The technology of DFS has been transferred to the industry. The ICMR-NIN also 
contributed to the preparation of the regulatory specifications for FSSAI for DFS. 
 
4.10.31 The Committee is impressed with the number of programmes/studies 
undertaken by ICMR in the country in the field of nutrition science and policy along 
with food safety. The Committee notes that majority of the studies undertaken by 
ICMR are hinged on the biomedical aspect. The Committee feels ICMR would be the 
ideal forum to initiate studies on India's diverse food ecosystem. For this, ICMR should 
include experts from agriculture (ICAR), environment (MoEFCC), and FSSAI while 
identifying the subjects of study to address real-world problems. The Committee 
strongly recommends a Multi-Disciplinary Food Safety Research Panel for this. 

108 
 
Another recommendation of the Committee is that due attention shall be given to 
changing food habits of the population across the societal strata keeping in mind 
aspects like easier access to fast food with the advent of fast food delivery at door steps 
primarily through platform-based services. At the same time, there is also an emerging 
cross section of population who is very health conscious. The Committee strongly feels 
that if the gist of findings in this field can be communicated in lay man’s language, it 
can reach the public in highly appreciated and acceptable manner. Further, considering 
the recent reports of rampant Vitamin D deficiency, especially among the urban 
population, the Committee recommends that ICMR shall consider commissioning a 
study on the deficiencies of Vitamin D and countermeasures. The Committee also 
favours a study on the impact of packaged food, including on the mechanisms that are 
in place to assess and regulate the quantity of preservatives used by commercial 
manufacturers. 
 
4.11   Treatment Procedure, Vaccine and Medical Devices Developed By ICMR 
 
4.11.1 The Committee sought to know the scientific advancement, methodology adhered to 
as per set SOP and innovations each in terms of drugs, treatment procedure, vaccine 
development over the period of last five years. According to the ICMR, it undertakes 
formulation, coordination and promotion of biomedical research including the development 
of innovative drugs, treatment procedures and vaccines through standardised processes. 
 
I: ICMR intramural and extramural investigator-initiated competitive grants 
 
4.11.2 The extramural and intramural principal investigator-initiated grants through schemes 
such as First-in-the-world challenge, Anveshan (Small) grants, Nishchayak (Intermediate) 
grants, Centres for Advanced Research, Centres of Excellence and the Follow-on grants. The 
scheme calls always mention the priority areas of research that includes on the scientifically 
and technically advanced MedTech such as cell and gene therapy, robotics, AI tools. The list 
of the priority areas are dynamic and newer areas are decided by a standardised process of 
holding brainstorming meetings with domain experts and with approval from a high-level 
committee at ICMR.The standardised procedure of proposal selection for these grants 
involves a rigorous 2-3 tiered process that involves scoring of the proposal for innovation 
(scientific advancement), methodology and impact besides the rationale and feasibility by the 
domain experts without any potential conflict of interest or bias.  
 
II: ICMR’s initiatives of MedTech MITRA, Patent Mitra and INTENT 
 
4.11.3 Besides these competitive grants, MedTech MITRA, an ICMR-CDSCO initiative with 
guidance from Niti Aayog, supports MedTech innovators of drugs, vaccines and medical 
devices to support them with an end-to-end development. These portals are accessible to 
every innovator 24X7 and each applicant innovator is invited for a hybrid-mode interaction. 
The multiple knowledge-partner panel (Includes senior officials from Atal Innovation 
Mission, IIT experts, Clinicians, Department of Health Research, Department for Promotion 
of Industry and Internal Trade, Atomic Energy Regulation Board, Department of 
Pharmaceuticals, Department of MSME) interacts with innovators one-on-one to facilitate 
incubation, proof of concept generation, patent filing and maintenance and technology 
transfer by ICMR’s Biomedical Innovations PATENT MITRA, regulatory strategy, 

109 
 
prototype development, Pre-clinical and clinical development, Field feasibility studies, 
Health technology Assessment and scale-up for government procurement/ exports. ICMR’s 
Indian Clinical Trial and Education Network (INTENT) with its 85 clinical trial sites across 
the country and Centre for Advanced research facilitate the regulation compliant clinical 
investigation of medical innovations. There are high-level Technical Advisory Groups for 
MedTech MITRA, PATENT MITRA and INTENT initiatives for selecting the innovators for 
undertaking the regulation compliant development. 
 
III: ICMR’s National Health Priority Projects (NHRP) 
 
4.11.4 ICMR works in close collaboration with various Ministries and Government 
Departments for research and development in priority health conditions of national 
importance and more than 30 such National Health Priority Projects are ongoing across 
multiple districts in India. ICMR invites innovators, academic institutions, researchers, start-
ups, and industry for their expression of interest to collaborate in the development and 
evaluation of MedTech Innovations of national health priority. 
 
IV: Vaccine R&D-  
 
4.11.5 Covid Vaccine:  ICMR developed India’s first RT-PCR testing protocol in January 
2020 and issued national testing guidelines. ICMR Jointly developed Covaxin India’s first 
indigenous COVID vaccine. ICMR provided preclinical support and strain characterization. 
ICMR also provided clinical trial site coordination and gave Immunogenicity assessment 
support. 
 
Kyasanur Forest Disease (KFD) Vaccine- ICMR is actively working towards the 
development of an improved vaccine against KFD in collaboration with Indian 
Immunologicals Limited (IIL), Hyderabad. The GLP-grade vaccine material has already been 
developed, and the necessary regulatory permissions have been obtained from the drug 
regulator. The clinical trial phase is about to commence.  
 
Zika Virus: ICMR is coordinating the Phase I trial for Zika virus vaccine with Indian 
Immunologicals Limited (IIL), Hyderabad as the manufacturing partner. This trial is 
expected to enrol participants in 2026. 
Influenza virus:  ICMR is working with MynvaxPvt. Ltd. to support the development 
of an indigenously developed adjuvanted recombinant Influenza vaccine. This will support 
the efforts for pandemic preparedness. This Phase I/ II trial is expected to enrol participants 
in 2026 
Avian Influenza Vaccine- ICMR had invited expressions of interest (EoIs) from 
vaccine manufacturers for the development of candidate vaccines against the highly 
pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) H5N1 virus. Proposals from three Industry partners were 
selected for collaborations with ICMR. The names of the industries and proposed vaccine 
platforms are listed below. 
 
 
 

110 
 
1 Serum Institute of India Pvt. Ltd., Pune- Live attenuated influenza vaccine 
2 Ribbonate Technologies (Premas Biotech), Gurugram-Virus-like Particles based 
vaccine 
3. Gennova Biopharmaceuticals, Pune-mRNA-based vaccine. 
 
V.  Medical Devices 
 
4.11.6 On the process involved in registration of medical devices and the steps taken to make 
the process user friendly, ICMR submitted that it has no regulatory role in the approval of 
medical devices. MedTech MITRA, an ICMR-CDSCO initiative with guidance from Niti 
Aayog, supports MedTech innovators including those of medical devices to support them 
with an end-to-end development. ICMR’s Indian Clinical Trial and Education Network 
(INTENT) and Centre for Advanced research facilitate regulation of compliant clinical 
investigation of medical devices. Further, ICMR facilitates the clinical validation of such 
devices already approved by Atomic Energy Regulatory Board (AERB) and Central Drugs 
Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO)by independently assessing claims related to the 
image quality of X-ray outputs. This includes evaluating the image quality and diagnostic 
adequacy of chest X-rays, benchmarking them against standard digital X-ray systems through 
blinded review by qualified radiologists, and generating evidence to support their effective 
programmatic deployment. 
4.11.7 ICMR, in collaboration with Institute of Plasma Research (IPR), has developed and 
validated an indigenous AI-based tool (DeepCXR) for automated interpretation of chest X-
rays. This tool classifies images as normal or abnormal and can be used with chest X-rays 
generated from both fixed digital X-ray systems and handheld X-ray devices to improve the 
TB screening under the National TB Elimination Programme. On the AI enabled X-Ray 
device for Tuberculosis Screening has been assembled by including Chinese components, 
ICMR stated that it has procured Mine2IN handheld X-ray devices through the Government 
e-Marketplace (GeM) under the Class I “Make in India” category. However, certain 
components such as the X-ray tube (a part of the X-ray source) and the detector, which are 
integral to the handheld X-ray system were imported, as per details provided by the supplier 
(Lipomic India Pvt. Ltd.).  
4.11.8 On the information regarding details of vaccines at various stages of 
development/approval by ICMR, the Committee feels that the information is scattered 
across press releases and annual reports, leading to difficulty in keeping track. 
Accordingly, the Committee recommends a dedicated page on the ICMR official 
website, clearly indicating the vaccine name, target disease, development stage, and 
expected timeline for each stage. 
 
4.11.9 The Committee understands that ICMR has no regulatory role in the approval of 
medical devices. MedTech MITRA, an ICMR-CDSCO initiative with guidance from 
Niti Aayog, supports MedTech innovators including those of medical devices. Further, 
ICMR’s Indian Clinical Trial and Education Network (INTENT) and Centre for 
Advanced research facilitate regulation compliant of medical devices. Also, ICMR 
facilitates the clinical validation of such devices already approved by Atomic Energy 
Regulatory Board (AERB) and Central Drugs Standard Control 
Organisation (CDSCO), resulting in regulatory overlap creating an uneven protocol for 

111 
 
development of medical devices. The Committee observes that the current medical 
device registration process involves multiple organizations and ICMR has no role after 
AERB approval. Hence, the Committee strongly recommends a more simplified, single-
window system which would reduce timelines and improve transparency. 
 
4.11.10 The Committee observes that ICMR, in collaboration with Institute of Plasma 
Research (IPR), has developed and validated an indigenous AI-based tool (DeepCXR) 
for automated interpretation of chest X-rays. The Committee recommends that ICMR 
make such indigenous technology available throughout the country free of cost which 
would be a step towards accessible and affordable healthcare. Even while making 
progress towards developing indigenous technology, the Committee opines that our 
country still relies heavily on imported medical technologies and equipment such as 
MRI, CT scans, PET-CT, and LINAC systems, which are procured from international 
companies like Philips and Siemens. ICMR has acquiesces that certain component such 
as the X-ray tube (a part of the X-ray source) and the detector, which are integral to the 
handheld X-ray system developed by them were imported substantiates this fact. If the 
core component like, X-ray tube, HV tank, etc is being imported ICMR should refrain 
from claiming it as their own technology. The better way for ICMR would be to engage 
itself in depth research for indigenous production, design, and development of medical 
devices. The medical devices developed viz the portable x-ray should be completely 
indigenous including X-ray tube, HV tank. Medical innovation and products should not 
be an assembly of components to be cited as ‘Made in India’. In this context, the 
Committee recommends that ICMR should set up a benchmark for ‘Made in India’ 
initiatives and derive a protocol wherein ‘Atmanirbhar Bharat’ is encompassed on a 
true scale. In case ICMR has to go for compilation of medical devices, the protocol 
should be clearly highlighted on the dashboard. Having said that the Committee also 
recommends that ICMR should make maximum efforts to develop some mechanism to 
manufacture these machines in our medical device parks with 100 % percentage of 
indigenous value addition which is essential for regaining public trust in the field of AI. 
 
4.12 ICMR and Young Researchers 
 
4.12.1 ICMR has developed Impact of Research and Innovation Scale (ICMR-IRIS), which 
provides a standardized framework for multidimensional impact assessment. This approach 
converts diverse research outputs into a single metric termed Publication-Equivalent (PE). 
Impact is measured across indicators including peer-reviewed publications, highly cited 
research, contributions to clinical or public health guidelines, granted patents, development 
and commercialization of health technologies and evaluation studies supporting regulatory 
approval or procurement. Aggregated PE scores are further normalized as PE per year, per 
unit of funding, and per scientist-year to enable benchmarking.  
 
4.12.2 This framework ensures comprehensive evaluation of scientific, translational, 
technological, and policy outcomes of publicly funded research. Improving research 
indicators in the country requires sustained investment in skilled human resources and 
enhanced participation of young researchers. Under the HRD Scheme, the DHR places 
special focus on the youth pursuing undergraduate/postgraduate medicine and Ph.D. 

112 
 
programmes, as well as young faculty and women scientists with career breaks to build a 
strong pipeline of biomedical researchers comparable to global standards. 
 
4.12.3 To promote health research in the country, particularly among the youth, DHR has 
undertaken the following initiatives: 
 
(i) Targeted Support for Young Researchers: The HRD Scheme is primarily designed 
to benefit early-career biomedical researchers across medical colleges and research 
institutions, enabling them to develop research skills and scientific thinking at an 
early stage. 
(ii) Fellowships and International Exposure: DHR provides overseas fellowships and 
travel grant support to young scientists and researchers for short-term training 
abroad and participation in international conferences. This exposure helps them 
gain insights into cutting-edge global research practices. 
(iii) Encouraging Research Culture Among Students: By focusing on undergraduate, 
postgraduate, and doctoral students, the Scheme aims to inculcate scientific 
curiosity, promote hands-on research experience, and motivate youth to pursue 
long-term careers in biomedical research. 
4.12.4 Also, the new ICMR–AcSIR PhD Programme is expected to strengthen India’s 
clinical research ecosystem and foster indigenous innovation in medical devices and 
therapeutics by integrating clinicians into structured PhD training alongside biomedical and 
public health researchers. Following the ICMR–AcSIR MoU, all 27 ICMR Institutes and the 
Headquarters became affiliated academic centres, enabling 376 ICMR scientists to be 
recognised as faculty and nearly 298 medical scientists and biomedical students enrolled in 
PhD programmes, with an additional fifty clinical scientists from 11 ICMR CCoEs under the 
DHR–Young Faculty Scheme joined the programme. This initiative will build a cadre of 
clinician-scientists, enhance translational research capacity, and support technology 
development, contributing to self-reliance in health innovation. 
 
4.12.5 Further, ICMR is addressing gaps in structured mentorship by introducing formal 
coursework in research methodology, biostatistics, and publication ethics through ICMR-NIE 
(Chennai) and ICMR-NIMR (New Delhi) as part of the AcSIR PhD curriculum, with 
centralised annual examinations ensuring uniform standards. Additionally, protocol 
development workshops and related training programmes are being conducted to improve 
study design, ethical conduct, and scientific rigor, thereby shifting the academic culture from 
quantity-based outputs to quality-driven research. 
 
4.12.6 The Committee notes with appreciation the measures adopted by ICMR to 
promote health research among the youth of the country. Targeted support for Young 
Researchers for early-career biomedical researchers across medical colleges and 
research institutions, Fellowships & International Exposure to support young scientists 
and researchers for short-term training abroad and participation in international 
conferences; and Encouraging Research Culture Among Students focusing on 
undergraduate, postgraduate, and doctoral students are three initiatives the Committee 
has been appraised of. Further, the Committee notes that under the HRD Scheme, the 

113 
 
DHR places special focus on the youth pursuing undergraduate/postgraduate medicine 
and Ph.D. programmes, as well as young faculty and women scientists with career 
breaks to build a strong pipeline of biomedical researchers comparable to global 
standards. A new ICMR–AcSIR PhD Programme has been launched to strengthen 
India’s clinical research ecosystem and foster indigenous innovation in medical devices 
and therapeutics. Efforts are also on to address gaps in structured mentorship.  
 
4.12.7 To attract and engage the new generation to research is a critical initiative to 
ensure a continuum in Health Research. The Committee feels that it is important to 
promote students and researchers at all levels - from popularising bio sciences at higher 
secondary levels to handholding young individual and well as institutional researchers. 
Mentoring, especially at higher levels, is a key factor in producing skilled researchers 
and has to be given high priority and for this, making mentoring appealing cannot be 
ignored. The Committee wants DHR to devise appropriate initiatives to invite senior 
researchers and clinicians from both public as well as private spheres to mentoring and 
aim at forming a national mentorship grid. Targeted scholarships and small grants for 
researchers could be considered to earmark funds for the facilitation programme. The 
Committee also recommends internships at ICMR Institutes for students and young 
researchers can offer an opportunity to them to be part of real projects, while the 
Institutes also benefit.  
 
 4.12.8 The Committee also hold the firm view that assessment of outcomes based on the 
Publication-Equivalent(PE)Metric, allowing beyond short-term training opportunities 
abroad supported by fellowships, targeted outreach campaigns for underserved regions 
and institutions alongwith seed funding for medical colleges in underserved areas as 
well as effective feedback loop for review of the promotion programmes can be of 
immense help in this direction. 
 
4.13 World Trends in Health Research and ICMR 
 
4.13.1 The Committee wanted to know about the latest health research trends around the 
world and the role of DHR/ICMR in these areas of research. The DHR responded as under:  
 
Latest Global Health Research Trends 
 
1. AI-Driven Health Innovation: Artificial intelligence and machine learning are 
now central to epidemic surveillance, diagnostics, drug discovery, and 
personalized medicine. AI algorithms increasingly power real-time outbreak 
detection, predictive models, and health systems planning.  
2. Digital Health and Health Data Integration: Telemedicine, wearable sensors, 
digital health records, and secure interoperable data platforms are transforming 
care delivery, improving chronic disease monitoring and enabling decentralized 
clinical research models.  
3. Pandemic Preparedness and Early Warning Systems: Strengthened global 
surveillance and predictive models aim to detect novel pathogens early and 
accelerate countermeasure development.  

114 
 
4. Climate Change and Health Interactions: The health impact of climate change 
(vector-borne diseases, air pollution effects, disaster response) is a growing 
priority across research agendas.  
5. Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR): Research is firmly prioritizing AMR, studying 
stewardship programs, alternative therapies, and global collaboration to slow 
resistance trends. 
Role of DHR/ICMR 
1. AI and Digital Health: Indian Council of Medical Research is validating AI-
based tools for TB chest X-ray interpretation, diabetic retinopathy screening, and 
digital pathology, while strengthening national registries and structured datasets to 
enable predictive analytics and precision medicine applications. 
2. Digital Health and Health Data Integration: For data integration &. genomic 
surveillance, during COVID-19, ICMR supported integrated laboratory reporting 
systems and contributed to genomic surveillance platforms in collaboration with 
INSACOG, strengthening real-time outbreak analytics. 
3. Pandemic Preparedness and One Health: ICMR expanded laboratory 
networks, led national serosurveys, and strengthened genomic sequencing during 
COVID-19, and continues to support zoonotic disease research and AMR 
surveillance under a One Health framework. 
4. Disease Surveillance and Public Health Research: Through national cancer 
registries, NCD risk factor surveys, the India TB Research Consortium, and 
infectious disease surveillance networks, ICMR generates policy-relevant 
epidemiological evidence that directly informs national health programmes. 
5. Anti-Microbial Resistance: ICMR established the AMRSN in 2013, linking 
tertiary care hospitals across India to generate standardized, quality-assured data 
on resistance patterns in priority pathogens. The network produces periodic 
national AMR reports that inform India’s National Action Plan on AMR and 
hospital antibiotic policies. 
4.13.2 Further, the Committee sought to know about the major on-going research projects 
DHR is engaged with major international entities like WHO or private sector partners for 
collaborative research. The Department submitted that it has international collaborations in 
different exposure programs, contributing in organization of scientific events like Science, 
Technology and Innovation Conclave, International Science Festival tours of DHR scientists 
for advanced trainings like Leading Innovations at Imperial College in UK etc. A joint 
collaboration with the leading Health Science/Research Institute of the world, viz., National 
Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) UK is also under process for capacity 
building of Centre for evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in 
India (HTAIn) attached office of DHR. In addition, DHR/ICMR have signed MoUs with 16 
foreign Organisations/Countries between 2021 and 2025.. Over the last five years, the ICMR 
institutes have demonstrated extensive collaborative research activity in the national and 
international levels. These include various collaborative projects including Regional 
Research Platform under PM-ABHIM scheme. Nationally, the institutes have coordinated on 
601 projects nationally and 70 projects internationally that resulted in 561 publications, and 
45 products. Commitments have been made for 79 projects nationally and 21 projects 

115 
 
internationally. The CD, NCD and RCN divisions have collectively collaborated on 100 
projects nationally and two projects internationally that resulted in 60 publications. 
4.13.3 Major ongoing research projects that are implemented under formal international 
cooperation agreements in priority areas of public health and biomedical research. 
These include collaborative research under research projects are in various areas such 
as under MoU with Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschafte.V. (DFG), Germany (areas: 
Toxicology, Neglected tropical diseases & Rare diseases); LoI with INSERM, France (areas: 
Diabetes & Metabolic disorders, Bioethics with focus on Ethics & regulatory issues of Gene 
editing techniques, Rare diseases); Implementing Arrangement with National Institute of 
Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases, National Institutes of Health, USA (areas: 
diabetes research); MoU with Department of Biotechnology and International AIDS Vaccine 
Initiative, USA (areas: HIV, COVID-19, Tuberculosis and other emerging and infectious and 
neglected diseases); LoA with Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, USA (areas: 
Communicable diseases – Enteric and diarrheal diseases; Vector-borne diseases; Neglected 
tropical diseases; Pneumonia; Tuberculosis; Polio; HIV; Family Planning; Maternal, 
Newborn, and Child Health and Nutrition Research; Disease modeling; Artificial Intelligence 
and other advanced data analytics concerning health research; Telemedicine for rural/remote 
health improvement); MoI with Forte, Sweden (areas: Ageing and Health); MoU with Global 
Alliance for Chronic Diseases (areas: Control of diabetes and obesity; characterization, 
quantification of risk factors (tobacco and environmental pollution), chronic obstructive 
airways disease, cancer, cardiovascular disease and other disorders; and implementation 
research); MoU with African Union (areas: health research collaboration, HIV/AIDS, 
Tuberculosis, Cancer, Emerging and re-emerging infections, Vector control, Health 
Systems); MoU with Global Antibiotic Research and Development Partnership Foundation, 
Switzerland (areas: Anti-Microbial Resistance); LoI with Swiss National Science 
Foundation, Switzerland (areas: One Health); MoU with Drugs for Neglected Diseases 
initiative, Switzerland (areas: neglected diseases, communicable diseases and infectious 
diseases outbreaks); MoU with University of Oxford, United Kingdom (areas: infectious 
diseases commonly affecting socially marginalized groups and emerging health); LoI with 
National Institute for Health and Care Research, UK (areas: High-performance, affordable 
and cost-effective point of care diagnostics, including for AMR stewardship; Combination 
interventions for the prevention and treatment of cardiometabolic diseases including 
hypertension and diabetes); MoU with Department of Medical Services Thailand (areas: 
pharmacogenomics, vaccines, drug resistance diagnostic test, internet of things and artificial 
intelligence, emerging and re-emerging infectious diseases, genomic and precision 
medicine); MoU with Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, Norway (areas: 
vaccines against priority pathogens; Capacity strengthening of health personnel and 
researchers; epidemiological studies; Development of standards, assays and diagnostics; 
Development of an epidemiology data-driven prioritization process); LoI with Colombia 
(areas: build capacity and develop sustained cooperation for research areas of mutual interest 
that are of public health). 
4.13.4 The Committee takes into cognisance the latest trends in health research across 
the globe, as submitted by DHR, and how DHR and ICMR are pitching in with their 
initiatives in these cutting-edge areas. It is observed that ICMR is validating AI-based 

116 
 
tools for TB chest X-ray interpretation, diabetic retinopathy screening, and digital 
pathology, the Committee recommends that ICMR must make such indigenous 
technology available throughout the country which would ensure certainly a step 
towards accessible and affordable healthcare. The Committee also notes the major on-
going research projects DHR is engaged with international entities like WHO or private 
sector partners for collaborative research. In this regard, the Committee is pleased to 
note these ongoing research projects that are implemented under formal international 
cooperation agreements in priority areas of public health and biomedical research. 
4.13.5 The Committee feels that one of the areas DHR should prioritise is building 
capacity in AI ethics and related guidelines. International best practices may be 
factored in while doing this. One of the possible ultimate outcomes of this would be 
garnering trust of the world in our health innovations. Further, the Committee also 
favours climate-health research initiatives in collaboration with global partners on One 
Health, zoonosis, etc. Such steps would help us to be better prepared for outbreaks and 
position DHR as a torchbearer of resilient health research.  
 
4.13.6 Another aspect the Committee wants to highlight is the importance to involve 
and encourage getting young health researchers to foray into these frontier fields 
through fellowships and other appropriate supporting mechanisms. It also appears to 
the Committee that cultivating efficient public-private partnerships by incentivising the 
industry for pandemic preparedness technologies, such as co-developing affordable 
diagnostics and vaccines would be a prudent idea to equip the nation with the ability to 
deploy rapid countermeasures.

Chapter V 
Way Forward For DHR 
 
5.1 Strategic Plan of DHR (2024–29) 
 
5.1.1 DHR has formulated a comprehensive Strategic Plan 2024–29 aligned with the 
national vision of Viksit Bharat @2047. The strategic goals for the next 5–10 years for 
advancing health research in the country are outlined as follows: 
 
1. Promotion of Indigenous, Affordable, and Globally Competitive Health 
Technologies DHR aims to significantly enhance India’s capability in developing frontier 
health technologies through the following initiatives: 
 
(i) ‘First in the World’ Challenge: Funding support for 100 high-risk, high-reward 
research ideas to encourage disruptive innovations. 
(ii) Frontier Technologies: Advancing work in areas such as cell and gene therapy, 
synthetic organs, therapeutic vaccines, advanced robotics, and other emerging 
medical technologies. 
(iii) Section-8 Company (MIDAC): Establishment of the Medical Innovation 
Development and Assessment Corporation (MIDAC) to facilitate industry 
partnerships, development of clinical trial batches, technology transfer, and 
validation processes. 
(iv) Med-Tech Mitra: Providing end-to-end support to start-ups and MSMEs for 
medical device innovation and development. 
(v) Centres of Excellence (CoEs) at IITs: Strengthening research and innovation 
ecosystems through dedicated CoEs in premier technical institutes. 
 
2. Accelerated Solutions for Difficult and Resistant Health Challenges:- 
 
To address critical public health problems, DHR proposes: 
 
1. Increased grant allocations for priority areas such as anaemia, antimicrobial 
resistance (AMR), non-communicable diseases (NCDs), child development and 
malnutrition, and the health impacts of climate change. 
2. Promotion of Randomized Controlled Trials (RCTs) registered in the Clinical 
Trial Registry of India (CTRI). 
3. Development of implementation models to address complex health challenges 
more effectively. 
4. National Health Research Policy (NHRP): Formulation of a comprehensive 
policy to strengthen India's health research ecosystem. 
5. Transformation and modernization of existing ICMR institutes. 
6. Establishment of dedicated Institutes for Bacterial Infections and Child Health. 
 
3. Advancing Digital Health Research and Infrastructure 
 
DHR proposes to harness digital innovations through: 
 

118 
 
1. Creation of a National Gold Standard Data Repository for high-quality, 
interoperable health data. 
2. Establishment of the ICMR – National Institute of Digital Health Research to 
lead research in digital health systems and advanced analytics. 
 
4. Translating Research Evidence into Actionable Public Health Interventions 
DHR aims to ensure that scientific evidence translates into improved health 
outcomes through: 
 
1. Development of 25 optimized, evidence-based intervention packages for 
priority health issues. 
2. Expansion of Health Technology Assessment (HTA) and cost-effectiveness 
studies for decision-making. 
3. Strengthening the Centre for Guidelines to produce rigorous, evidence-based 
clinical and public health guidelines. 
4. Capacity enhancement of Centres of Excellence and MRU/MRHRUs for 
implementation research. 
 
5. Strengthened Surveillance Systems and Accelerated Medical Countermeasures 
To prepare for future health emergencies and emerging pathogens, DHR 
proposes: 
1. Expansion of environmental surveillance, strengthening a national network of 
BSL-3 and BSL-4 laboratories, and supporting the development of vaccines, 
monoclonal antibodies, and antiviral drugs. 
2. Establishment of a National Institute of One Health (NIOH) to integrate human, 
animal, and environmental health surveillance. 
3. Upgradation of the existing Viral Research & Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) 
into Integrated Research & Diagnostic Laboratories (IRDLs). 
 
6. Enhancing Global Standing of Indian Medical Research 
To ensure India becomes a global leader in health research, DHR outlines: 
1. Targeting placement of five Indian institutions within the top 5% of global 
medical research rankings. 
2. Expansion of MD–PhD fellowships to increase the number of physician-
scientists. 
3. Introduction of Patent Mitra for handholding innovators in patent filing and 
intellectual property support. 
 
5.1.2 Further, the Department share the major policy decisions adopted by them in the last 
few years to expand its network of research institutions and collaborations to achieve self-
reliance in health innovations. DHR’s focus has been on expansion of research infrastructure 
during last 5 years. For this, it has sanctioned 38 MRUs, 11 MRHRUs and 15 DIAMOnDS 
centres in medical colleges and in proximity of Rural Health Centres in different states/UTs.  
 
5.1.3 During the XV Finance Commission period, a key mandate of DHR was the strategic 
expansion of the Viral Research and Diagnostic Laboratory (VRDL) network to strengthen 
national capacity for epidemic preparedness and outbreak response. The approved target was 

119 
 
to establish 42 new VRDLs across the country. To date, 44 VRDLs have been sanctioned, 
including 1 Regional VRDL, 2 State-level VRDLs, and 41 Medical College-level VRDLs. 
This expansion has significantly strengthened the nationwide laboratory network, enhancing 
India’s capabilities in disease surveillance, early outbreak detection, and pandemic 
preparedness, thereby contributing to self-reliance in health research and diagnostics. 
 
5.1.4 In alignment with the vision set forth by NITI Aayog, ICMR in collaboration with the 
Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO), established the ‘MedTech Mitra’ 
platform. The platform is being utilised by scientists, start-ups, industry, academia and 
students for healthcare prototypes developed by them. Collaborative and focused research and 
development for pandemic preparedness has been initiated through the National One Health 
Mission for which DHR is the nodal department and National Institute of One Health has been 
functionalized under the aegis of ICMR. 
5.1.5 A new Faculty of Medical Research (FMR) has been created through AcSIR -ICMR 
collaboration. In the first year, 140 PhD students were enrolled in FMR. Medical scientists 
from ICMR have also enrolled for PhD in FMR, in addition to those from outside.ICMR- 
Research Infrastructure Sharing Ecosystem (I-RISE) Portal and policy provides a structured 
overview for utilizing the research facilities in ICMR laboratories for research and 
development purposes. These ICMR facilities, including high-tech infrastructure in Tier II 
cities, can be utilized by researchers from medical colleges, universities, other institutions, 
startups, and MSME industries. 
5.1.6 Apart from this, DHR's also updated the Committee on its vision for improving 
research equity, particularly in underserved rural and tribal areas. The major achievements and 
challenges in attaining the vision included sanctioning of 39 MRHRUs in India, typically 
located near existing state health facilities like Primary Health Centres (PHCs) or Community 
Health Centres (CHCs) to create research infrastructure at the periphery to facilitate the 
transfer of modern health technologies and research outcomes to rural populations. These units 
operate in an "operational research mode" to test the applicability and feasibility of new 
technologies and interventions in a rural context. Each MRHRU receives significant funding 
for infrastructure, equipment, and an annual grant for manpower and research activities, which 
includes recruiting staff on a contractual basis. DHR emphasises closer integration and 
coordination with programmes of the Department of Health & Family Welfare (DoH&FW) 
and other scientific departments to ensure that research findings are translated into deliverable 
products and implemented effectively within the existing public health system. 
 
5.1.7 Health Technology Assessment (HTAIn): Established under the DHR, HTAIn 
conducts studies on the cost-effectiveness, clinical effectiveness, and safety of health programs 
and technologies to support evidence-based decision-making and policy formulations, 
ensuring value gained from the health budget and promoting standardised, cost-effective 
interventions that can be scaled in underserved areas. 
 
5.1.8 DHR has planned several targeted outreach programmes to enhance participation 
from medical colleges in Tier 2/3 cities, institutions in the Northeast Region, and government 
institutions that traditionally submit fewer applications. These initiatives include: 
 

120
