(i) Hand holding support will be extended to applicants through the organization of 
capacity building initiatives, including grant writing workshops and training 
programmes on research methodologies. 
(ii) Webinars will be conducted on a regular basis to disseminate information on open 
calls under the HRD Scheme. During these sessions, the HRD Scheme team will 
actively engage with applicants, providing clarifications and addressing queries 
related to the schemes and the application process. This will be facilitated through 
one to one interactions within the webinar platform, ensuring that applicants receive 
direct guidance and support. 
(iii) The communication channels of the ICMR, including ICMR radio and institutional 
networks, are being actively leveraged to disseminate information about the HRD 
Scheme.  
(iv) As a continuous activity, a dedicated session on the provisions and opportunities 
available under DHR schemes is being incorporated into all DHR supported 
workshops conducted in the Northeast region. 
(v) The existing networks of MRUs, MRHRUs, and VRDLs will be utilised for outreach 
and awareness generation for disseminating information, mobilizing applications, and 
fostering engagement with medical colleges and research institutions across the 
country. 
5.1.9 DHR also enumerate its role in India's goal of achieving Universal Health Coverage 
(UHC) through research-driven policies. At the core of DHR’s contribution is its oversight of 
the Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR), the country’s apex biomedical research 
body. Through ICMR’s nationwide network of institutes and field units, evidence is 
generated on communicable diseases, non-communicable diseases, maternal and child health, 
rare diseases, and emerging infections. This research directly informs national health 
programmes, clinical protocols, vaccination strategies, and disease control initiatives. By 
aligning scientific priorities with India’s disease burden, DHR ensures that public health 
policy is grounded in indigenous evidence. 
I. Cost Effectiveness of Medical Technologies through Health Technology 
Assessment 
A defining contribution of DHR to UHC lies in institutionalizing priority setting 
through Health Technology Assessment in India. By generating cost-effectiveness and 
budget impact analyses, HTA informs decisions on which technologies, drugs, and 
procedures should be financed through publicly funded schemes such as Ayushman Bharat - 
Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana. HTAIn provided cost evidence for 1573 Health benefit 
packages and supported revision and rationalization of 857 Health Benefit Packages under 
the AB-PMJAY. HTA also recommended that the cost of these packages may be utilized by 
CGHS and other insurance companies. This ensures that limited public resources are directed 
toward interventions that deliver maximum health gains and financial risk protection. The 
development of standardized HTA methods and the creation of Regional Resource Centres 
have further strengthened transparent and evidence-based decision-making at both national 
and state levels. 
Taken together, DHR’s role in India’s UHC agenda is not that of a service delivery 
agency, but of an enabler and steward of evidence. By generating robust scientific data, 

121 
 
strengthening infrastructure and human capital, standardizing care, and institutionalizing 
economic evaluation, DHR ensures that the expansion of health coverage in India is not only 
broader but also smarter, more equitable, and financially sustainable. 
II. Strengthening Public Health Research Infrastructure 
Beyond knowledge generation, DHR invests in strengthening research infrastructure 
within Government Medical Colleges and public institutions. The establishment of advanced 
diagnostic laboratories, genomics facilities, biorepositories, and clinical research units 
improves access to quality diagnostics and supports translational research. These investments 
enhance the public sector’s ability to respond to emerging health challenges while improving 
service delivery capacity. Through Human Resource Development (HRD) Scheme, DHR 
builds indigenous capacity in biomedical research, clinical trials, epidemiology, and health 
systems research, ensuring sustained availability of technical expertise required for evidence-
informed governance of UHC. 
III. Development of Evidence-Based Clinical Guidelines 
DHR also strengthens the quality dimension of UHC through evidence-based 
guidelines developed in collaboration with experts across the country. These guidelines aim 
to reduce unwarranted variation in care, promote rational drug use, and support uniform 
standards across public facilities. Standardization is essential in a large and diverse health 
system, particularly when financial protection schemes depend on clearly defined packages 
of care. 
As part of understating the hurdles by DHR in attaining its visions, the Committee  
sought to know the main challenges faced by the DHR in terms of infrastructure, human 
resources, and inter-departmental coordination. The Department responded that in case of 
MRHRUs, there is a delay by the State Government in allotment of land for the purpose of 
construction of MRHRU building. There are challenges involved in hiring requisite 
manpower at remote locations in rural/tribal areas. Delays in opening of TSA accounts for 
small research grants and relative lack of specialized agencies in India for construction of 
high biosafety labs are some major challenges faced during PM-ABHIM scheme 
implementation.  
 
5.1.10 On the recommendations/ suggestions of DHR to bridge the shortage of researchers in 
the country and encourage more students towards studying core science, thereby promoting 
more scientific minds, DHR replied as below:- 
 
5.1.11 There is a critical need to bridge the gap between biomedical research and clinical 
practice to enable effective integration of research insights into patient care. Strengthening 
this linkage is essential for advancing biomedical research, fostering collaboration between 
clinicians and researchers, and driving innovation in healthcare. In this direction, DHR has 
started Medical PhD program focusing on developing clinician-scientists through structured 
mentoring, training in research methodology, and exposure to advanced research techniques, 
ensuring participants can continue as active researchers and secure competitive grants in the 
future.  
 

122 
 
 
Other suggestions include- 
(i) Strengthening R&D with the new scheme like First in the World Challenge, where 
startups are made eligible to apply and compete for research grants from ICMR.  
(ii) VAIBHAV Summit: Incentivises scientists and diaspora for "brain gain" and circular 
migration. 
(iii) Enhanced R&D Ecosystem: Investing in world-class infrastructure, labs, and 
computing facilities to match global standards. 
(iv) Improved Career Prospects: Developing clear career paths, mentorship, and 
competitive compensation packages, especially in tech and academia. 
 
5.1.12  The Department placed before the Committee the ways through which research 
indicators can be improved in the country in comparison to other developed countries, 
especially by promoting health research amongst the youth. On these lines, ICMR developed 
the Impact of Research and Innovation Scale (ICMR-IRIS), which provides a standardized 
framework for multidimensional impact assessment. This approach converts diverse research 
outputs into a single metric termed Publication-Equivalent (PE). Impact is measured across 
indicators including peer-reviewed publications, highly cited research, contributions to 
clinical or public health guidelines, granted patents, development and commercialization of 
health technologies, and evaluation studies supporting regulatory approval or procurement. 
Aggregated PE scores are further normalized as PE per year, per unit of funding, and per 
scientist-year to enable benchmarking. This framework ensures comprehensive evaluation of 
scientific, translational, technological, and policy outcomes of publicly funded research. 
 
5.1.13 Improving research indicators in the country requires sustained investment in skilled 
human resources and enhanced participation of young researchers. Under the HRD Scheme, 
the DHR places special focus on the youth pursuing medical undergraduate, postgraduate, 
and Ph.D. programmes, as well as young faculty and women scientists with career breaks to 
build a strong pipeline of biomedical researchers comparable to global standards. 
 
To promote health research in the country, particularly among the youth, DHR has 
undertaken the following initiatives: 
 
(i) Targeted Support for Young Researchers: The HRD Scheme is primarily designed 
to benefit early-career biomedical researchers across medical colleges and research 
institutions, enabling them to develop research skills and scientific thinking at an 
early stage. 
(ii) Fellowships and International Exposure: DHR provides overseas fellowships and 
travel grant support to young scientists and researchers for short-term training 
abroad and participation in international conferences. This exposure helps them 
gain insights into cutting-edge global research practices. 
(iii) Encouraging Research Culture Among Students: By focusing on undergraduate, 
postgraduate, and doctoral students, the Scheme aims to inculcate scientific 
curiosity, promote hands-on research experience, and motivate youth to pursue 
long-term careers in biomedical research. 

123 
 
5.1.14 The Committee notes that DHR has formulated a comprehensive Strategic Plan 
2024-29 aligned with the vision of Viksit Bharat @ 2047 under which the following 6 
focus areas have been identified – 
 
(i) Promotion of Indigenous, Affordable and Globally Competitive Health 
Technologies; 
(ii) Accelerated Solutions for Difficult and Resistant Health Challenges; 
(iii) Advancing Digital Health research and Infrastructure; 
(iv) Translating Research Evidence into actionable Public Health Interventions; 
(v) Strengthened Surveillance Systems and Accelerated Medical 
Countermeasures; and 
(vi) Enhancing Global Standing of Indian Medical Research. 
 
5.1.15 The Committee finds theses identified core areas to be potential drivers of 
progress in Health sector that are aimed at self-reliance, solutions for health problems 
of future, accelerating innovation, improving deliver and positioning India as a global 
leader. It is going to be a demanding task that warrants meticulous planning and 
execution through different vehicles. Establishment of MIDAC, Med-Tech Mitra, CoEs, 
setting up of National Gold Standard Data Repository, expansion of HTA, 
augmentation of MRUs and MRHRUs, National Institute of Digital Health Research, 
Establishment of a National Institute of One Health, formation of Integrated Research 
& Diagnostic Laboratories, introduction of Patent Mitra inter alia are defining these 
drivers. The Committee strongly recommends in this direction the formulation of a 
well-defined and updated National Health Research Policy (NHRP) as guiding factor to 
embark upon this ambitious task.  The Committee also is of the firm opinion that 
distinguishing health research as one of the National priorities is another unavoidable 
step to embellish the pursuit.  
 
5.1.16 As emphasised by DHR, it is imperative to establish closer integration and 
coordination with programmes of the Department of Health & Family Welfare 
(D/oH&FW) and other scientific departments to ensure that research findings are 
translated into deliverable products. But the prime driver of all the initiatives shall 
remain ICMR. The Committee would like to appreciate the effort to create a new 
Faculty of Medical Research (FMR) through AcSIR-ICMR collaboration, as ultimately, 
we need human minds to lead us. Another matter of high interest to the Committee is 
the role of DHR in India's goal of achieving Universal Health Coverage (UHC) through 
research-driven policies, which will be pivoted by ICMR and ably supported by 
HTAIn. 
5.1.17 The Committee has been made aware of the main hurdles in the path towards 
attainment of the Viksit Bharat Goals. As per DHR, there is a critical need to bridge the 
gap between biomedical research and clinical practice to enable effective integration of 
research insights into patient care. In this direction, DHR has started Medical PhD 
program focusing on developing clinician-scientists. The Committee notice that ICMR 
has developed the Impact of Research and Innovation Scale (ICMR-IRIS), which 
provides a standardized framework for multidimensional impact assessment. This 
approach converts diverse research outputs into a single metric termed Publication-
Equivalent (PE). The Committee also note that to promote health research in the 
country, particularly among the youth, DHR has undertaken measures like Targeted 

124 
 
Support for Young Researchers, Fellowships and International Exposure to young 
scientists, Encouraging Research Culture among Students by focusing on 
undergraduate, postgraduate, and doctoral students, etc. Further, the Committee is 
appreciative of the unique ‘First-in-the-World Challenge’, inspired by Chandrayan-3 
landing, making India the first country to do so. This initiative holds the promise of 
creating technologies, products, or solutions that are truly ‘first in the world.’ 
Accordingly, the Committee are extending its firm support to these endeavours and 
expects that DHR would be able to back up these initiatives with the requisite financial 
and infrastructural support. With regard to ‘First-in-the-World Challenge’, the 
Committee recommend that there should be prescribed criteria/parameter for selecting 
projects and allocating funds under the said category of research. 
5.2 Research on Traditional and Integrative Medicine Practices  
 
5.2.1 Even when the nation is turning towards AI for finding solutions in healthcare and 
research, traditional and alternative medicines hold its sacred place in the sphere of 
medicines. Accordingly, measures have to be taken to ensure that research on traditional and 
integrative medicine practices is conducted with rigorous scientific standards, allowing for 
evidence-based integration with modern medicine. DHR have taken many steps towards this 
like signing of the Memorandum of Agreement (MoA) with the Ministry of AYUSH on May 
11, 2023, ICMR and the Ministry of AYUSH established various joint working groups 
consisting of scientists and heads of institutions from both sides (ICMR and CCRAS, 
Ministry of AYUSH). All major decisions and any modifications to programs are made with 
approval during meetings co-chaired by the two Secretaries. The progress of programmes, as 
well as the planning and execution of studies, are collaboratively managed in working group 
meetings overseen by the heads of ICMR divisions and the Deputy Director General (DDG) 
of the Central Council for Research in Ayurvedic Sciences (CCRAS) from the Ministry of 
AYUSH. 
 
5.2.2  Subsequently, DHR also submitted to the Committee that a collaboration has been 
made with Ministry of AYUSH and Five AYUSH-ICMR Advanced Centres for Integrative 
Health Research have been setup at four AIIMS. ICMR also has dedicated National Institute 
of Traditional Medicine (NITM) in Belagavi to advancing the research of traditional 
medicine in India. Established with the vision of integrating traditional knowledge systems 
with modern scientific approaches, ICMR-NITM has undertaken various cutting-edge 
research projects fostering innovation and promoting evidence-based practices in traditional 
medicine for the benefit of public health. The following measures are employed by ICMR to 
encourage scientific rigour in research on traditional and integrative medicine: 
 
a) Investigators and staff are well-trained and knowledgeable in Good Clinical Practices 
(GCP).  
b) Study plans are supported by a clear and appropriate rationale, with well- defined and 
clearly articulated study objectives.  
c) To account for confounders and effect modifiers related to the Traditional (Ayush) 
interventions being evaluated, the study population is selected using stringent inclusion 
and exclusion criteria.  

125 
 
d) Sample size calculations are performed using appropriate statistical methods to ensure 
that the study has sufficient power.  
e) A comparative or control group is included, utilising either standard care treatment or a 
placebo. 
f) All subjects are randomised to prevent selection and treatment bias. 
g) Whenever possible, double blinding is implemented for both patients and investigators, 
or single blinding for patients. 
h) A third treatment arm, which combines standard treatment with traditional medicine, is 
maintained for integration when necessary. 
i) For the evaluation of efficacy and safety of interventions, standard modern tools that are 
objective in nature are used as outcome parameters. 
j) Comprehensive methodological details for each study parameter are provided to support 
transparency and reproducibility in research on traditional and integrative medicine. 
k) All adverse events are meticulously recorded, including their intensity, type, and 
frequency, to ensure safety and enhance confidence in the integrity of the study. 
l) Protocols are reviewed and modified by the Protocol Development and Selection 
Committee, which comprises a team of subject experts from relevant areas. 
m) Studies are initiated only after obtaining IRB/EC approval and in strict accordance with 
applicable regulatory guidelines to ensure adherence to scientific and regulatory 
standards. 
n) Ayush products under study are of standard quality, accompanied by a certificate of 
analysis, and are prepared in a GMP-certified manufacturing facility. 
o) Investigators and staff are thoroughly trained in GCP. 
p) Sample handling and analysis are conducted in either NABL-accredited laboratories or 
according to good laboratory practice guidelines. 
q) Data recording, data capture, and record keeping are done using standard software or 
digital tools. 
r) Study sites are equipped with all necessary infrastructure and facilities to ensure the 
smooth completion of the study. 
s) A statistical plan is provided for data analysis. 
5.2.3  As part of understanding the progress of research leads of valid AYUSH remedies 
translated into tangible health products, the Committee sought from DHR the findings of two 
institutions, i.e, of National Institution of Traditional Medicines (NITM) and Tribal Health 
Research Forum (THRF). 
 
a) National Institution of Traditional Medicines (NITM) 
 
5.2.4  According to DHR, NITM, Belagavi is working on various aspects of traditional 
medicines from both codified and non-codified systems including their scientific validation. 
The Institute documented the ethnomedicinal practices and the results have been compiled 
into a Database on ethnomedicinal plants of the Western Ghats (https://nitmmedplantsdb.in/). 
Selected leads from these practices are being taken for scientific validation. Meantime, the 
institute is also working on selected Ayurvedic formulations for their safety and efficacy. The 
following are the Traditional Medicine formulations, which are under final stages of 
validation (Clinical Trials) at ICMR-NITM: 
 
(i) Herbal formulation (an ointment) for the management of diabetic foot ulcers: 
Under multicentric clinical trial 

126 
 
(ii) Herbal topical application (a Gel) for Osteoarthritis: Under multicentric clinical 
trial 
(iii) Herbal formulation (a lotion) for dermatophytes: Under multicentric clinical trial 
(v) Herbal formulation (extract tablet) for reduction of adverse events of 
chemotherapeutic agents in cancer therapy: Under multicentric clinical trial. 
 
The following are the Ayush practices being evaluated at ICMR-NITM: 
 
(i) Ayush-GMH as add-on to Vitamin E in Grade I and II Non-alcoholic Fatty Liver 
Disease (NAFLD): A Double-Blind Randomized Placebo Controlled Clinical 
Trial 
 
(ii) Triphala as an Add-on Intervention for Sustained Weight Loss in Overweight 
and Obesity: A Randomized Placebo-Controlled Clinical Trial 
 
(iii) Integrative Therapy of Drakshavaleha with Oral Iron Folic Acid Supplements in 
Management of Mild to Moderate Iron Deficiency Anaemia in Women of 
reproductive age group: A Randomized Controlled Trial 
 
(iv) PunarnavadiMandura alone and in combination with Drakshavaleha compared 
to iron folic acid in the treatment of moderate iron deficiency anaemia among 
non- pregnant women of reproductive age group: a community-based three arm 
multicentre randomized controlled trial 
 
b) Tribal Health Research Forum (THRF)  
 
5.2.5  In August 2010, ICMR initiated the “Tribal Health Research Forum” (THRF) at 
National Institute of Research in Tribal Health (formerly Regional Medical Research Centre 
for Tribals) NIRTH, Jabalpur, which brought together different Institutes that work on and 
for tribal populations in their own regions, on a single platform, with the primary objective to 
improve the health of tribals. Under this THRF, set up by ICMR involving 15-16 Institutes / 
Centers (located in different regions of India with health research mandate) along with some 
of the major Divisions at ICMR Hqrs. as partners, in order to facilitate the work to be carried 
out as different institutes / centers of ICMR, 10 Tribal Health Research Units (THRUs) were 
established by ICMR in two phases. 
 
Phase - I THRUs: Key Achievements / Contributions to the National Health Research 
Programmes / Policies: 
 
Through these THRUs, the Institutes / Centres were strengthened to carry out the 
research activities on the tribal health issues and have supported Govt. of India by 
generating evidences for policy changes and contributed in various ways in the 
National Health Research Programmes, as indicated below: 
 
Hemaoglobinopathies:  
 
1. Established Sickle Cell diagnostic Clinics in 27 tribal districts of MP and in a 
remote tribal district Baster in CG (NIRTH, Jabalpur in collaboration with 
State Government). 
2. Conducted regional training workshops / training of Master Trainers on 
diagnosis of SCD and management (NIRTH jointly with MOTA) - Inducted in 
19 States. More than 1.5 crore tribal school children screened. 

127 
 
3. Established facilities for newborn screening for SCD, prenatal diagnosis of β-
thalassemia syndrome, sickle cell disorders and micro mapping of G6PD 
deficiency, in Madhya Pradesh, Assam and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands. 
 
Malaria: 
1. Developed evidence-based malaria intervention model (Dindori) for control of 
forest and tribal malaria. The model up-scaled to make a model in malaria 
elimination through PPP mode in Mandla district of MP through MEDP 
project. 
2. Prepared “Tribal Malaria Action Plan” as a part of NHM - Contributed to 
Policy formulation for Malaria-in-Pregnancy. 
3. Evaluated bivalent malaria rapid kits (RDTs) – Inducted into National 
programme. CDSCO/DCGI approved NIRTH for QA of malaria RDTs. 
4. Monitoring therapeutic efficacy in malaria endemic tribal regions of the 
country to support the National programme. 
5. Showed a sharp decrease in Malaria incidence in Car Nicobar since the 
Tsunami (API decreased from 105 in 2005 to <1 in 2013). 
 
Tuberculosis: 
1. Several studies identified and reported very high TB prevalence (upto 3000 / 
1,00,000) among Saharias, a PVTG in India. 
2. Intervention study among the Saharia tribe demonstrated decline in 
Tuberculosis (TB) prevalence. An intensified TB control program (Research 
cum Intervention study) - Launched in collaboration with the MP State 
Government. 
3. Repeated surveys conducted showed a declining prevalence of pulmonary 
tuberculosis in Car Nicobar, which is attributed to the better socio-economic 
status and housing brought about the post-tsunami reconstruction and 
rehabilitation initiatives. 
 
Fluorosis: 
Developed Universally accepted Nutritional supplementation and safe drinking 
water-based Intervention Model for Fluorosis Prevention and Control 
developed. 
 
Lymphatic Filariasis: 
 
Made and implemented a programme for elimination of lymphatic filariasis 
from its only focus in India viz. the Nancowry of islands. The programme has 
almost achieved its objective of bringing down the mf rate <1%. 
5.2.6. According to the Committee, millions rely on traditional systems like Ayurveda, 
herbal remedies, for primary care, especially for NCDs. The WHO Global Traditional 
Medicine Strategy 2025–2034, adopted by the Seventy-eighth World Health Assembly, 
aims to ensure universal access to safe and effective traditional, complementary, and 
integrative medicine (TCIM). From the Committee’s view point, the merits of traditional 
medicines include primarily cost-effectiveness, limited hospitalizations, reducing over-
reliance on pharmaceuticals and, last but not the least, the fact that traditional practices 
represent centuries of accumulated wisdom. But a host of demerits overshadow the 
benefits. The concerns of the Committee related to Traditional and Integrative Medicine 
practices are that these practices are largely individualized, generates unreliable or 
insufficient data, research inadvertently leads to pseudoscientific claims, presence of 

128 
 
heavy metals and pesticides in herbal products, inconsistent quality, methodological 
hurdles lowering investment returns, etc. All these factors combined with lack of 
training, governance, and patient awareness can erode trust in traditional healthcare 
systems. The Committee extends all its support to the DHR initiatives with Ministry of 
AYUSH in establishing Five AYUSH-ICMR Advanced Centres for Integrative Health 
Research (AI-ACHIR) at four AIIMS. The Committee recommends that both 
DHR/ICMR and Ministry of Ayush adopt propitious steps to address the concerns 
expressed by the Committee and take pro-active steps forsafe integration oftraditional 
and alternative medicinesinto mainstream healthcare systems. 
5.3 AI in Health 
5.3.1 With the rapid integration of Artificial Intelligence (AI), digital health platforms and 
data-driven research into healthcare delivery and public health programmes, the Department 
of Health Research (DHR) considers digital health research to be a priority area requiring 
sustained and predictable financial support. AI-enabled healthcare technologies such as 
clinical decision-support systems, digital diagnostics, digital screening and surveillance tools, 
disease prediction models, population surveillance analytics and real-world evidence 
platforms are increasingly essential for improving efficiency of national health programmes 
and early disease detection and optimisation of healthcare expenditure. Accordingly, DHR 
expects that the Ministry of Finance may support a structured expansion of budgetary 
allocations specifically earmarked for digital and AI-enabled health research. 
5.3.2 DHR has already planned strengthening of national digital and translational research 
capacity through programmes such as establishment of Infectious Disease Research & 
Diagnostic Laboratories for surveillance and outbreak response, the NIRMAN network for 
indigenous medical device development and clinical evaluation, creation of a national-grade 
data centre with AI/ML and cybersecurity infrastructure across ICMR institutes through PM-
ABHIM and a National Virus Resource Facility to support vaccine, diagnostic and 
therapeutic research; accordingly, the Department seeks sustained and dedicated budgetary 
support from the Ministry of Finance to scale such digital, AI-enabled and preparedness-
oriented research initiatives. 
5.3.3 Requirements for ICMR-NIRDHDS: To attain its objectives of accelerating research 
for development and evaluation of Digital and AI based solutions in health, the institute will 
benefit from a state of the art building infrastructure with high performance/ super computing 
facilities. Currently, the institute is actively exploring options for enhanced space and 
infrastructure to expand its scientific and technical workforce, thereby enabling the uptake of 
large-scale collaborative studies in digital health research. Digital health research is an 
emerging yet rapidly evolving interdisciplinary field that demands a diverse array of 
specialized skills. To strengthen its scientific capacity and address this expanding domain, 
the institute plans to recruit approximately 20 additional scientists over the next few years. 
Currently, the institute has three Scientists on board, with two more expected to join in the 
near future. 
5.3.4 Therefore, DHR’s considered expectation from the Ministry of Finance is not merely 
incremental increases in routine funding, but a mission-mode, ring-fenced financial support 
framework for AI and digital health research covering infrastructure, validation, regulatory 
evaluation, human resource development and implementation research. Such support would 

129 
 
enable evidence-based adoption of emerging technologies, promote indigenous innovation 
and ultimately improve efficiency, accessibility and affordability of healthcare delivery in the 
country. 
5.3.5 Further, the Committee’s query DHR on its plan to integrate emerging technologies 
like AI, genomics, and big data into its research framework. In reply, DHR stated that DHR/ 
ICMR plans to integrate AI, genomics, and big data into its research framework by (i) 
strengthening dedicated institutional capacity for digital health and data science, (ii) building 
enabling platforms and standards for trustworthy data use, and (iii) accelerating translation 
through targeted funding, regulatory handholding, and structured Intellectual Property (IP) 
support. 
5.3.6 A key step is the establishment of ICMR–National Institute for Research in Digital 
Health and Data Science (ICMR-NIRDHDS), which was repurposed from the erstwhile 
National Institute for Research in Medical Statistics (NIMS) in April 2024. The institute is 
mandated with research in development, evaluation and implementation of high impact and 
affordable digital and AI based health solutions. Development of India-specific, accurate AI 
algorithms, which depend on robust, secure, and representative health data systems that 
safeguard privacy while enabling responsible innovation. To address this need, the Indian 
Council of Medical Research (ICMR) has established the Health Research Data Repository, 
providing researchers with access to standardized, interoperable, and ethically governed 
health research data. Equally critical is rigorous evaluation and evidence generation to ensure 
that AI solutions demonstrate real-world effectiveness, safety, and equity. In this regard, 
ICMR’s National Institute for Research in Digital Health and Data Science (NIRDHDS) is 
collaborating with clinical, technical, and governmental partners to build a network for 
systematic technical, clinical, economic, and implementation evaluations of AI health 
technologies. ICMR has further strengthened the translational research ecosystem through 
initiatives such as MedTech Mitra and Patent Mitra, which support innovators from 
validation to regulatory and intellectual property guidance. 
5.3.7 For genomics and high-dimensional biomedical data, ICMR is strengthening compute 
and analytic capacity to support rapid processing of large sequencing datasets and 
downstream applications such as outbreak investigation and AI-enabled discovery. For 
example, ICMR’s NAKSHATRA high-performance computing cluster for virological 
research is intended to enable faster analysis of complex genomic and bioinformatics data 
and support more efficient outbreak response and preparedness. 
5.3.8 ICMR is pushing emerging technologies into the translational pipeline through 
structured intramural and extramural funding mechanisms, including dedicated MedTech 
development grant calls.To ensure that innovations are protected and transferred effectively, 
ICMR has also operationalized “Medical Innovations Patent Mitra,” which offers services 
such as patentability assessment, patent filing support, prosecution assistance, and facilitation 
of technology transfer to industry partners. 
5.3.9 The Committee, thereafter, wished to know about the assessment of Artificial 
Intelligence (AI) penetration in diagnostics in India. DHR submitted that AI is being 
integrated into various diagnostic processes, particularly in medical imaging (radiology, 
pathology), to enhance speed, accuracy, and efficiency. Government initiatives like the 
Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM) and the National Health Mission are creating the 

130 
 
digital infrastructure and regulatory frameworks to support the integration of AI. ICMR has 
also released ethical guidelines for AI use in healthcare.  
 
5.3.10 As part of understanding emerging technologies, the Committee asked about the steps 
taken by DHR to enhance electronic project management system (E- PMS) to streamline 
submission and monitoring of projects under its schemes like HRD, VRDLs (Epidemic 
Labs), MRHRUs & MRUs. The Department responded by stating that under the HRD, 
VRDL, MRU, and MRHRU schemes, integrated digital platforms have been developed to 
strengthen governance, transparency, and evidence-based programme oversight. The HRD 
Scheme now supports end-to-end digital management of fellowships, training, and 
institutional capacity-building initiatives, with automated workflows for proposal 
submission, expert review, funding approvals, and comprehensive reporting and analytics to 
support policy and programme monitoring. 
 
5.3.11 The VRDL Network has been digitally enabled for centralised oversight of laboratory 
infrastructure, diagnostics capacity, manpower, research outputs, and training activities, with 
interactive dashboards supporting quality assurance and network performance monitoring. 
Similarly, the MRU and MRHRU schemes are supported through standardised KPI-based 
monitoring, institutional profiling, geospatial mapping, and structured reporting frameworks 
to track infrastructure utilisation and research outcomes. Data governance mechanisms, 
including periodic data freezing and enhanced reporting structures, ensure data integrity and 
policy-relevant analytics. These enhancements are ongoing and iterative in nature, with 
continuous upgrades planned to further strengthen functionality, interoperability, and user 
experience. 
 
5.3.12 VRDL Scheme: The e-PMS portal has been developed and operationalized to enable 
online submission and real-time monitoring of utilization certificates, progress reports, 
training and capacity-building activities, infrastructure status, manpower details, and other 
laboratory activities. The portal provides a centralized digital platform for tracking physical 
and financial progress, and fund releases to VRDLs are made based on expenditure and 
performance data captured through the system, thereby enhancing transparency, efficiency, 
and accountability. 
 
5.3.13 HRD Scheme: The steps taken to enhance electronic project management system 
(EPMS) to streamline submission and monitoring of projects under HRD scheme are as 
follows: 
Submission: 
- Integration of new components with existing databases to maintain seamless 
data flow.  
Integration of new portals into the DHR ePMS:  
(i) Short Term Studentship 
(ii) MD Thesis Support 
(iii) Post Doctoral Fellowship 
(iv) UC/SOE and report uploading functionality, enabling applicants to upload 
annual reports, UC, and SOE in the ePMS portal. 
(v) Functionality of online technical evaluation by TEC experts. 

131 
 
Monitoring: 
(i) Monitoring dashboard on various metrices of all the components of HRD 
scheme. 
(ii) Reviewed and addressed more than 1500 consisting of technical, general, 
administrative and financial queries received on the ePMS portal. 
 
The HRD Scheme Reports have been strengthened with the implementation of the 
auto-fetch master sheet data feature. This measure ensures systematic retrieval and timely 
updating of data, thereby enhancing efficiency and reducing manual intervention. 
 
5.3.14 Further, DHR submitted before the Committee the major challenges in AI and Digital 
Health Integration which are as follows:  
 
(i) Data Quality, Privacy, and Security: AI models require vast amounts of high-
quality, diverse, and representative data for training and validation.  
(ii) Regulatory and Ethical Concerns: The rapidly evolving nature of AI technology 
outpaces traditional regulatory frameworks for medical devices.  
(iii) Interoperability and Integration: A lack of standardised data protocols hinders 
effective data exchange between different systems and providers. 
(iv) Clinical Validation and Reliability: Rigorous, real-world validation through 
prospective studies and randomised controlled trials (RCTs) is needed to 
demonstrate a tool's clinical and economic utility and ensure it is safe and reliable 
for diverse populations. 
(v) Workforce Skills and Acceptance: There is a significant shortage of healthcare 
professionals with expertise in AI and data science.  
(vi) DHR, often in collaboration with the National Health Authority (NHA) and ICMR, 
is addressing these challenges through initiatives:  
(vii) Establishing Regulatory and Ethical Frameworks: DHR adheres to existing national 
digital and data protection frameworks, including the ICMR's ethical guidance for 
biomedical AI, the IT Act 2000, and the Digital Personal Data Protection Act.  
(viii) Developing an Interoperable Digital Ecosystem: Through the Ayushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM), the government is building a national digital health 
infrastructure that uses a unique Ayushman Bharat Health Account (ABHA) ID for 
citizens to link and access their digital health records across various facilities. 
 
5.3.15 The Committee is seized of the importance of Artificial Intelligence in health 
research and notes that DHR has plans to integrate emerging technologies like AI, 
genomics, and big data into its research framework. AI is being integrated into various 
diagnostic processes, particularly in medical imaging (radiology, pathology), to enhance 
speed, accuracy, and efficiency. Government initiatives like the Ayushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM) and the National Health Mission are creating the digital 
infrastructure and regulatory frameworks to support the integration of AI. ICMR has 
also released ethical guidelines for AI use in healthcare.  
 
5.3.16 The Committee understand that AI-enabled healthcare technologies such as 
clinical decision-support systems, digital diagnostics, digital screening and surveillance 
tools, disease prediction models, population surveillance analytics and real-world 

132 
 
evidence platforms are increasingly essential for improving efficiency of national health 
programmes and early disease detection and optimisation of healthcare expenditure. 
 
5.3.17 A few of the remarkable steps by DHR that the Committee takes note of are (i) 
creation of a national-grade data centre with AI/ML and cybersecurity infrastructure 
across ICMR institutes through PM-ABHIM, (ii) establishment of ICMR–National 
Institute for Research in Digital Health and Data Science (ICMR-NIRDHDS) mandated 
with research in development, evaluation and implementation of high impact and 
affordable digital and AI based health solutions and (iii) ICMR’s NAKSHATRA, high-
performance computing cluster for virological research is intended to enable faster 
analysis of complex genomic and bioinformatics data and support more efficient 
outbreak response and preparedness. The Committee is also aware of the various 
hurdles to be surmounted by DHR to safely amalgamate AI into a sensitive domain like 
health research. One of the hurdles being privacy of digital date and safe use of digital 
platforms/technologies/tools.  
 
5.3.18 The Committee notes that DHR understandably expects that the Ministry of 
Finance may support a structured expansion of budgetary allocations specifically 
earmarked for digital and AI-enabled health research. The Committee considers the 
importance of integrating AI into the field of health research and related areas and the 
humongous benefits it can bring to the vast population of our country. The Committee 
in this regard urges the Ministry of Finance to accords high priority in allocating 
adequate funds to DHR keeping into consideration proper outcome parameter that 
generally DHR hinges upon in the field of health research. The Committee would also 
like to recommend a certain amount of budget should be kept aside solely for safety and 
protecting of health data keeping into consideration the vast amount of data available 
in the health sector. 
 
 
************* 
 
  

133 
 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE- 
AT A GLANCE 
 
Vision and Mission of DHR 
 
1. The Committee understands that DHR has nodal/administrative role regarding 
various Rules/Regulations/Guidelines, including the Acts like Assisted Reproductive 
Technology (Regulatory) Act 2021 and Surrogacy (Regulatory) Act 2021.The 
Committee also notes that DHR has not undertaken any post-legislative impact 
assessment of the Acts/Rules/Regulations/Guidelines, as on date. But, the Department, 
taking into consideration the large no. of cases in the country, a holistic view is being 
taken for amending the Acts. The Committee lauds the view of the Department and 
recommends that necessary steps may be taken towards specific post-legislative impact 
assessment and bring in appropriate amendments to the 
Acts/Rules/Regulations/Guidelines keeping in mind the requirements of the changing 
time on account of scientific, technological  and medical advancements. 
(Para 1.2.5) 
 
Opportunities, Strength and Challenges of DHR as an organization 
2. The Committee finds that DHR considers Ad‑hoc /Project-Based Staffing to be 
one of its biggest challenges. According to the Department this results in high turnover, 
which disrupts research continuity, weakens institutional memory, and increases costs 
due to repeated recruitment and retraining. The Committee endorses the view of DHR 
that the contractual staff, either project-based or as part of Secretariat weakens 
institutional memory due to frequent change and loss of trained personnel causing  
disruption in ongoing research, besides having  higher administrative training and skill 
development overhead. It is of the considered opinion of the Committee that permanent 
work force is an asset to any organization, especially, in offices like DHR and ICMR 
where institutional memory and research continuity is a requirement for a long term 
result oriented research. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Department 
shall continue its efforts to acquire permanent workforce both for DHR Secretariat as 
well as scientific research activities.  
(Para 1.3.3) 
Human Resource Management of DHR  
3. Based on the submission of the Department, the Committee feels that 
contractual (or project-based) appointments are the new norm. The Committee is not in 
agreement with such ad-hoc or contractual arrangement. The Committee reckons the 
practical point of view that for time-bound specific scheme contractual appointments 
may contain operational merits and should have cost effectiveness.  However, the 
Committee expresses concern to observe that the contractual scientists are now 
outnumbering the permanent/deputation staff. The Committee is in favour of 
permanent staff in DHR secretariat and permanent scientist for continuity in research 
activities.   
(Para 1.4.7) 
4. In the view of the Committee, a permanent scientific workforce in research 
offers significant merits for an entity like DHR. Wherein institutional knowledge, 
sustained progress in mission-oriented, complex, long-term research are of critical 
importance in general. Further, job security encourages researchers to pursue novel 

134 
 
and high-impact ideas which should curtail migration of brighter minds to abroad due 
to lack of stability and career progression. The Committee acknowledges that 
permanent positions involve upfront costs and less flexibility for the Government but in 
research, where breakthroughs often require patience and accumulated wisdom, the 
long-term benefits of sustained scientific advancement cannot be overlooked. Hence, the 
Committee recommends a sustainable approach on the issue like hybrid model of 
appointments including permanent and deputation with less dependency on contractual 
scientists. The service conditions for contractual appointees can be based on the New 
Labour Codes which may include fixed term contracts, social security network and 
performance-based conversion to permanent posting after a fixed period on the lines of 
CSIR model. 
(Para 1.4.8) 
 
5. The Committee appreciates the efforts taken by the Department to fill up the 
various post of scientist but is disappointed to perceive the reasons for the posts to 
remain vacant like difficulty in attracting eligible and qualified candidates, lack of 
interest on the part selected candidate to join, etc. Meanwhile, the Committee 
recognises the proactive approach adopted by the Department in addressing the issue 
and revisiting and reviewing the Recruitment Rules of HTAIn to include direct 
recruitment as well. Accordingly, the Committee supports and recommends the 
revision of recruitment rules of HTAIn Attached Office to include direct recruitment 
keeping in account the importance of assessment of AI technologies/tools by HTAIn. 
 
(Para 1.4.9) 
 
6.   Considering the relevance of Ethics Committee in the era of emerging AI 
technologies and digital health platforms in the public health system, the Committee 
feels that while formulating the Recruitment Rules for the post of Scientists in the 
Ethics Committee, the Department should opt for direct recruitment on the lines of 
HTAIn Attached Office. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the 
Recruitment Rules for the post of Scientists in the Ethics Committee needs to be 
formulated including direct recruitment, at the earliest,  and these posts are to be 
populated as soon as possible.The Committee also recommends that time to time 
updation of the Recruitment Rules should be a conscious effort to contemporise the 
conditions of service for development of a permanent scientific workforce in Health 
Research sector of the country. 
(Para 1.4.10) 
7. The Committee is appreciative of the working ecosystem of DHR. Reassignment 
of roles, problem description to solution orientation, centralized science to distributed 
capacity are effective management tactics. Further, the Committee finds that operation 
mechanism for these tactics like mission-mode research, patent facilitation, Health 
Technology Assessment, human capital pipeline, data as infrastructure are bringing in 
expected benefits at ground level. The Committee also notes that the Department 
operates through the concept of Management by Objectives (MBO). This the 
management approach is based on setting specific and measurable goals and, according 
to the Committee, is an effective requirement in healthcare administration. 
(Para 1.4.12) 
Health Research Challenges In India       
 
8. The Committee recognises the work done by DHR through HTAIn and MRHRU 
towards systematic collection of data and analysis of evidence thereby addressing the 

135 
 
current ‘policy to practice’ gap. Having said that, the Committee feels more can be 
done to track whether research findings are actually incorporated into 
national/state/district health policy/plans. The Committee observes the need and 
existence of programmes addressing malnutrition, infectious diseases, and maternal 
mortality point to this. Taking this into account, the Committee recommends that DHR 
should create an “Evidence-to-Action Dashboard” that maps research outputs against 
national health priorities. Further, every ministry/department involved in the 
implementation of a particular programme/scheme should submit an action-taken 
report on relevant ICMR/DHR studies within six months of its publication, with public 
disclosure and Parliamentary review. The Committee is of the considered view that 
such measures would bridge the critical translation gap and ensure that India’s robust 
health research ecosystem directly strengthens evidence-based community interventions 
and delivers measurable public health gains. The Committee is of the firm conviction 
that research must ultimately translate into affordable medicines and accessible 
therapies for citizens. 
(Para 1.5.5) 
 
9. The Committee is pleased to note the work in progress towards the development 
of India-specific AI-based solutions for strengthening the healthcare systemin the 
country. Having said that, the Committee feels that emerging technologies such as AI 
diagnostic tools and digital health platforms in India remain in their early stages due to 
issues like poor data management, algorithmic bias, ethical challenges, and limited 
internet access in semi-urban, rural, hilly and tribal areas. Alongwith these factors, the 
Committee also hold the view that callous electronic health records maintenance and 
cybersecurity vulnerabilities compromise trust and safety towards AI tools & digital 
health platforms. Accordingly, the Committee opine that there should be data driven 
healthcare planning with strict privacy safeguards. For the success of these data driven 
healthcare policy, the country also needs technology empowered scientist, doctors, 
support staff and patients consolidating Protective Research Equilibrium (PRE). 
 
(Para 1.5.14) 
 
10. As steps to make AI tools & digital health platforms more reliable the 
Committee recommends that DHR should make pre and post-market HTA mandatory 
for all AI diagnostics seeking to integrate with public health system. DHR must update 
and enforce Ethics Guidelines for Application of Artificial Intelligence in Biomedical 
Research and Healthcare, 2023 by ICMR with compulsory bias audits, diverse dataset 
requirements, and ‘Human-in-the-Loop’ features for every government funded or 
publicly integrated AI tool. Health data/record being a sensitive subject, the Committee 
recommends for more region-diseases specific studies by DHR/ICMR on health data 
bias, maintenance and usage. 
(Para 1.5.15) 
 
11. The Committee notices that inspite of Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission 
(ABDM), internet in rural/tribal areas is still unreliable. Another concern in rural area 
is digital literacy gaps and inadequate training for frontline workers like Accredited 
Social Health Activist (ASHAs) and Auxiliary Nurse Midwife (ANM).  This results in 
urban-centric development and continued exclusion of millions in the rural belts from 
benefits of tools like AI-powered chest X-ray analysis or clinical decision support 
systems like eSanjeevani. To address these, the Committee recommends that DHR 
should develop offline-capable indigenous AI solutions and launch a nationwide AI 
training programme designed for last-mile integration beginning with the aspirational 
districts for frontline workers (ASHAs, ANMs) through Model Rural Health Research 
Units. 

136 
 
 
(Para 1.5.16) 
 
12. The Committee realises that AI is revolutionizing health sector as in every other 
field. However, like any technology, it comes with both advantages and challenges. In 
the opinion of the Committee, besides the considerable efforts on the part of DHR and 
its institutions, strengthening oversight and the capacity of Ethics Committees is one of 
the measures needed to ensure consistent ethical review standards of emerging Al 
technology. Foremost, the Committee recommends thorough monitoring by DHR of 
registration/ re-registration of Ethics Committees across India with the National Ethics 
Committee Registry for Biomedical and Health Research (NECRBHR) and enforce 
penalties for non-compliant institutions. The Committee is of the opinion that the 
country lacks appropriate number of accredited and functional Ethics Committees in 
comparison to the volume of regulatory and academic trials, resulting in delayed 
approvals and increased risk of ethical lapses. 
(Para 1.5.21) 
 
13. Since there is an involvement of various Ministries/Bodies like DHR, MeitY, 
ICMR, National Health Authority (NHA), Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission (ABDM), 
Anusandhan National Research Foundation (ANRF), etc, the Committee recommends a 
dedicated AI Surveillance & Ethics Monitoring Cell with inter-ministerial 
representation and mandated annual Action Taken Report submission to an 
Appropriate Authority for real time performance tracking and periodic ethical audits 
and to create safety benchmarks & roadmaps for AI integration for health sector as a 
whole.  
(Para 1.5.22) 
Funding pattern of DHR 
 
14. The Committee observes that since Government is spending only 0.02% of GDP 
for Health research for the last 5 years, a large proportion of existing schemes would 
naturally suffer from implementation gaps, poor last-mile delivery, and suboptimal 
progress in semi-urban, rural, hilly and tribal areas. Despite the funds being hard to 
come, DHR has always ensured best utilization of the allocated funds and the 
Committee is appreciative of the efforts of DHR. Having said that, the Committee 
believes that the shortfall in Health Research Funding is not only a budgetary issue but 
reflects weak evidence on the returns of existing schemes/programmes as well, both 
physical and financial. DHR should be able to persuade the Ministry of Finance in pre-
budget consultations, that every additional fund allocated, yields promotion of 
indigenous affordable health technologies and digital health solutions which is 
translated into practical health policies and actions, all of these ultimately translating to 
saving of public funds. Accordingly, the Committee recommends a dedicated cell in 
DHR to bring forth periodic reports on both the physical and financial aspects of public 
health spending on bio-medical research. The reports should give emphasis on the 
outcome of schemes/programmes and utility of innovations and technology emanating 
from bio medical research for practical use on the ground level.  
(Para 2.7) 
 
15. To improve the fund utilization of DHR, the Committee recommends measures 
like teaming of seasoned scientists with early-career researchers in 
programmes/schemes to bridge knowledge gaps and variant insights in the funding 
landscaping, real-time tracking of projects outcomes involved in assets creation via 
GIS-enabled portal, developing scorecards and linking the same to annual performance 
appraisals of institutes/individuals concerned, introduction of automatic forfeiture and 
reallocation of unutilised capital grants after a certain period as well as blacklisting of 

137 
 
defaulting institutes for future projects. For the Committee, the success of any research 
endeavour is inextricably linked to adequate funding. By addressing the challenges 
inherent in the funding process through these recommended methods, DHR should be 
able to strengthen their position which may catalyse the Ministry of Finance to dedicate 
more funds for the Department for the stated strategic purpose based on actual 
outcome intending to make the healthcare more accessible and affordable. 
 
(Para 2.8) 
 
16. The Committee understands that currently DHR schemes are not benefitting 
from by the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Nidhi (PMSSN). However, the 
Committee is of the firm opinion that the DHR schemes are in no way less important in 
the endeavours to attain inter- alia the targets of National Health Policy, 2017, which is 
one of the salient features of PMSSN. Hence, the Committee recommends that DHR 
should submit a formal proposal to the Ministry of Finance for ring-fencing an 
appropriate percentage of the health component of cess from PMSSN for its schemes/ 
programmes. The Committee feels that such a proposal would hold weightage since 
PMSSN is the primarily a non-lapsable reserve fund which has been conceived to cover 
broader aims like National Health Policy 2017, Emergency & Disaster preparedness 
and response during health emergencies, etc. These aims of PMSSN directly impinge 
implementation of evidence-based interventions in urban/rural/tribal areas and 
translation of research outcomes into policy and practice. 
(Para 2.10) 
 
 17. The Committee considers the submission of the Department that as far as 
research programmes/schemes are concerned, even though institutions are generally 
located in Tier I, II and III cities, the population coverage of research activities and 
field components includes both rural and underserved populations. Also, the 
submission made by the Department of 60% and 40% fund allocation to MRUs and 
MRHRUs respectively, under Development of Infrastructure for Health Research 
during the 15th Finance Commission Period suggests appropriately earmarked funding 
pattern for both urban and rural belts. Still the Committee feels that lack of proper 
monitoring mechanism when research is conducted in rural areas leads to 
infrastructure and human resource gaps at the time of its implementation. To address 
this, DHR can launch a real-time portal linked to all MRHRUs and Ayushman Bharat 
Health & Wellness Centres (AB-HWCs) and accessible to the Centre and States. 
Moreover, to bring in more transparency in fund allocation, the Committee 
recommends mapping of funding patterns and to make available in public domain, 
institute-wise, State-wise, and rural/urban break-up data of sanctioned funds. Further, 
an independent annual equity audit by CAG should be institutionalised, with findings 
presented to the Parliament. 
(Para 2.13) 
 
18. The Committee is of the considered view that when research funding remains 
concentrated in urban-based institutions and research centres it may result in health 
challenges innate to rural and tribal areas such as vector-borne diseases, malnutrition, 
maternal mortality and sickle cell anaemia, not getting its needed share of funding. 
More research institutions in the tribal and aspirational districts will ensure the area 
specific research gets needed attention and no region is left behind in healthcare 
preparedness. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that DHR should notify a 
policy mandating that at least 40% of all extramural research grants be awarded to 
projects led by or implemented in rural/tribal institutions. These projects can also be 
partnered by State Medical Colleges and Model Rural Health Research Units 
(MRHRUs).  

138 
 
(Para 2.14) 
 
19. As perceived by the Committee, another downside for rural and tribal areas is 
that their medical colleges and research units lack trained manpower, equipment, and 
administrative support, making them less competitive for DHR grants thereby 
reinforcing the urban funding dominance. In this regard, the Committee recommends 
that DHR should provide extra weightage in grant evaluation for proposals from these 
institutions and special incentives like hardship allowance, fast-track promotion for 
scientists opting for rural postings to aggressively handhold them. 
(Para 2.15) 
 
Projected Demands and Actual Allocations of DHR 
 
20. Based on the figures of Projected Demand, BE, RE and Actual Expenditure 
pertaining to DHR for the financial years, 2024-25, 2025-26 and 2026-27, the 
Committee observes that the gap between the funds projected by DHR and the budget 
allocated by the Ministry of Finance has narrowed from Rs 1069.34 crore in 2024-25 to 
Rs. 702.67 during 2026-27. The Committee views such trend as a positive move and 
hopes that the funds get translated into measurable outcomes like affordable treatment, 
strong disease surveillance and world class research infrastructure. The Committee also 
observes that the utilisation of funds by Department which remained below par owing 
to various emergent and unprecedented constraints initially, has picked up in the post-
pandemic period.  
(Para 2.2.6) 
 
21. The Committee observes that the enhanced BE for 2026-27 of Rs 4,821.21 crore 
(increase of 42.2 % over the Actuals of 2024-25 and 23% over RE of 2025-26) is 
overwhelmingly research-driven (Rs. 850 crore to ICMR, including Rs. 475 crore 
extramural, Rs 150 crore Intramural and Rs. 200 crore GIA-Capital funds), with 
targeted scaling of new initiatives, MedTechMitra, First-in-the-World, Centre for 
Advanced Research (CAR), National Health Research Priority Projects (NHRP), 
NIRMAN, National Virus Resource Facility (NVRF), etc.) and HRD fellowships. The 
Committee expects DHR to devise proactive steps to efficiently utilise the increased 
funding received for the ensuing year. Ensuring the assessment of capacity to utilise 
including institutional infrastructure, financial compliance, project progress roadmap, 
etc, this should be ensured before funds are released. Such measures would be 
especially pivotal in case of new Schemes like MedTechMitra, Patent Mitra, etc. The 
key concerns areas of utilisation leading to under absorption of resources that needs to 
be identified and sorted out. The Committee also strongly favours and recommends the 
creation of an institutional mechanism to monitor progress of GIA and PM-ABHIM 
projects, which have consistently witnessed project delays in the past. Realtime 
Outcome Dashboards would be a great tool for the purpose of monitoring the schemes. 
This step may be in addition to the time proven, periodic utilisation review mechanisms 
that may be in place for expected results. A dedicated monitoring mechanism would 
help to convert the ₹200+ crore GIA-Capital spike into tangible assets within the 
financial year, avoiding RE-stage surrenders and supporting long-term research 
capacity. 
(Para 2.2.7) 
 
22 The Committee further observes that to make optimal utilisation of the funds 
allocated, the Department may explore channelising money from schemes making 
sluggish progress, despite best efforts, to schemes making better headway. The 
Committee recommends such move can be made when required with the due 
consultation and approval of the Ministry of Finance, wherever required. 

139 
 
(Para 2.2.8) 
 
23. The Committee takes into account the submission of DHR about the proposed 
new research infrastructure activities under PM-ABHIM Scheme for the 16th Finance 
Commission period viz. NIRMAN, NVRF, National Grade Data Centre and Disaster 
Recovery Infrastructure and establishment of Infectious disease Research & Diagnostic 
Labs. All are critical initiatives that can act as stepping stones in the nation’s progress 
towards self-reliance in the health sector in combating its challenges.  
(Para 2.2.9) 
 
24. With respect to the matter of capital investment in health research, the 
Committee reckon from the submission of DHR that while DHR is primarily a policy, 
coordination and funding entity and do not maintain independent laboratory 
infrastructure. Physical research infrastructure is undertaken through ICMR and 
national health research missions which reflect the bulk of capital formation. PM-
ABHIM also involves significant infrastructure developments. Hence, the present 
budgetary pattern reflects a division of responsibilities between DHR and ICMR on 
these lines. The Committee makes the observation that DHR and ICMR shoulder a very 
significant component of national advancement that would contribute towards the 
Viksit Bharat vision of the Government and should take all measures to bring out the 
expected outcomes. 
(Para 2.2.14) 
 
15th Finance Commission period vs 16th Finance Commission in respect of DHR. 
25. The Committee notes that DHR, apart from its existing schemes/programmes, 
has inter alia submitted proposed new schemes, like, Integrating Evidence-based 
Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme⁠ and Policy and Strategic 
Alliance for Nurturing Medical Innovations and Translational Research Acceleration 
(SANMITRA) for the 16th Finance Commission period (2026-27 to 2030-31). The 
financial statistics of 15th Finance Commission period in respect of Department of 
Health Research shows that all through the period, the Department has never been 
allocated their Projected Demand. The Committee’s inference on the aspect is that the 
components of the schemes are getting compromised. The Committee, therefore, would 
expect that the Department is more realistic in their physical & financial proposals for 
the 16th Finance Commission period. The Committee recommends that the Department, 
on their part should adopt broad measures like concentrating on high-impact areas and 
aligning them with the national priorities, linking resources to measurable components, 
resorting to modern public financial management, turning to performance-linked 
incentives to retain skilled scientist/researches, etc. Thereby, the Department must 
enhance the performance of their schemes/programmes and be more assertive of their 
financial requirement. 
(Para 2.3.6) 
 
A Comparative study of research budget of DHR with other major research 
Ministries/Organizations. 
 
26.  The Committee takes into account DHR's budgetary allocations for research 
activities vis-a-vis other key ministries and departments involved in research, such as 
the Department of Science and Technology (DST), Department of Biotechnology 
(DBT), Department of Space (DoS), Department of Atomic Energy (DAE), Ministry of 
Education and Ministry of AYUSH in the Union Budget 2026-27, is the lowest to DHR. 
Comparison of the similarly structured Ministry of Health & Family Welfare and 

140 
 
Ministry of Education is more thought provoking, as both cater to infrastructural 
development as well as research. Even, Ministry of Education is spending 9% of its 
total allocation for research activities while Ministry of Health & Family Welfare 
shares only 4.8% with DHR. Having said that, the Committee observes that out of the 
total Government of India R&D outlay (all scientific ministries combined) for the FY 
2026–27 at ₹63,381 crore, DHR is allocated to the tune of Rs. 4,821.21 crore which is 
approximately 7.6%. When compared to 2017-18, the BE for the DHR/ICMR has more 
than tripled in 2026-27. However, the allocation over the years has been chequered. 
Whereas the growth in BE for CSIR/DSIR for the corresponding period has been 1.5 
times but their increase has been linear. 
(Para 2.4.11) 
 
27. Overall, the Committee feels that the DHR's share of budgetary allocations for 
research activities can be considered only as modest compared to other key ministries 
and departments involved in the field. Funding for the sake of enhanced budget 
allocation is not proper without relevant outcome. To improve/enhance its total 
allocation DHR has adopted many policy directions like recognition of health research 
as a strategic national priority comparable to sectors such as space, atomic energy and 
industrial research,  strengthening of allocation mechanism through outcome-linked 
budgeting, institutionalisation of enhanced inter-ministerial convergence, 
conceptualisation of a dedicated capital funding window for national health research 
infrastructure complemented by public-private partnerships, viewing of schemes like 
HTAIn as a prerequisite for efficient rollout of AB-PMJAY, National Health Mission, 
and national health programmes and not as a discretionary research scheme, etc. Also, 
DHR/ICMR should consider raising its own resources by tying up with major private 
players in the industry. The Committee is of the considered view that these policy 
directions of DHR can accelerate the pace of activities in the field of medical research in 
the country that would ultimately lead to global standard of healthcare. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that DHR should strive for Medical Innovations 
Development Acceleration Council (MIDAC) for steering translational research 
partnerships and technology development and equity for ICMR.  
(Para 2.4.12) 
 
Secretariat 
 
28. The Committee takes cognizance of the reasons submitted by the Department on 
the increase of its allocation under the Head- Secretariat under various stages of 
allocation during 2025-26 and BE 2026-27. During the previous FY, DHR had funds 
under the Head kept aside for filling up vacant posts in the Department and acquiring 
an office premise of its own. The Department clarified that very little progress has been 
made on both the issues. The Committee has recommended for the filling up of the 
vacant posts and acquiring office premise of its own for the Department during the 
examination Demands for Grants, 2025-26, as well. It is discouraging to notice that the 
one of the premier Departments in a field as vital as Health Research of the nation is 
deprived of two basic infrastructural requirements, i.e., physical infrastructure and 
human resources. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Department puts 
forth a better plea based on practical solutions to rectify its HR issues and get itself a 
permanent official premise and avoid recurring expenditure on rent. 
(Para 2.5.6) 
 
Health Technology Assessment (HTA)  
 
29. The Committee takes into account the explanation furnished by the Department 
regarding reflection of HTA under two different heads in their Demand. HTA began as 

141 
 
a pilot programme in April 2017 for evaluation of appropriateness and cost 
effectiveness of available and new health technologies in the country and was later 
approved as an Attached Office of DHR in 2023. A separate budget line was given in 
2024. Now, given its distinctive mandate and requirement of sufficient technical and 
financial support, concept note of a separate scheme ‘Integrating Evidence-based 
Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme⁠ and Policy’ has been 
submitted to Department of Expenditure. With a proposed outlay of Rs. 379 Cr (2026-
27 to 2030-31), the scheme will be implemented by HTA Attached office. The 
Committee is in agreement with DHR and perceives HTA as a very important aspect of 
curating an effective ecosystem for ensuring quality and cost effectiveness of medical 
technology available the country through evidence-based mechanisms and recommends 
that DHR should effectively pursue approvals for the new scheme from the Ministry of 
Finance during the 16th FC Cycle. The Committee hopes that the accounting matter of 
reflecting HTA under two heads in the Demand No 47 of DHR will also be taken care of 
as HTA is now removed from the HRD scheme and the new scheme, namely,  
Integrating Evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in 
Programme⁠ and Policyto the implemented by the Attached Office is approved.   
(Para 2.6.3) 
 
30. The Committee observes that as of February 2026, no new separate HTA 
Scheme has been publicly notified. As on date, the new HTA Scheme titled ‘Integrating 
Evidence-based Guidelines and Health Technology Assessment in Programme and 
Policy’ – 2026-27 to 2030-31 is awaiting final approval of Department of Expenditure. 
Whereas the old scheme HTAIn is continuing only as a sub-scheme under the umbrella 
Human Resource & Capacity Development programme of the 15thFinance Commission 
cycle. In this scenario, the Committee is apprehensive that this change over and absence 
of a dedicated, adequately funded HTA scheme risks funding gaps, stalled HTA studies, 
etc. The Committee, over the years, has repeatedly recommended for time-bound 
planning and better coordination while seeking approval to avoid administrative delays. 
In tune with this, keeping in mind the relevance of having HTA, the Committee 
recommends that the Department should approach the Department of Expenditure to 
expedite its approval and finalise a standalone National Health Technology Assessment 
Scheme considering the growing need for evidence-based prioritisation under national 
programmes like, Ayushman Bharat PM-JAY, AB-HWCs, etc. 
(Para 2.6.11) 
 
31. The Committee is of the firm opinion that the Department should revisit the 
proposed outlay of Rs. 379 Cr, if a standalone National HTA scheme is to be considered 
in place of a mere expansion of the existing HTAIn sub-scheme for 2026–31. Taking 
into consideration the trend of allocation, the Committee notices that the Department 
hardly gets its Projected Demand for a scheme because even for the FY 2026-27, the 
proposed demand of Rs. 56.63 Cr was downsized to Rs. 25.00 Cr. Percentage of 
Variation of BE 2026-27 as compared to Projected Demand was 55.85%. The 
Committee, therefore recommends the Department to be more realistic and reasonable 
while making projections in consonance with requirement and in convincing the 
Department of Expenditure of their fund requirement with due justification. 
(Para 2.6.12) 
 
32. The Committee appreciates that the targets set under Health Technology 
Assessment for the FY 2025-26 have exceeded the mark except for the fact that no of 
policy briefs have been utilized by user agencies. The Annual Report -2025-26 of DHR 
shows that HTAIn has completed 136 studies. But the need of the hour is also that these 
studies are consumed by user agencies in the form of guidelines, uptake of technology in 
program and for procurement of devices/kits/technologies. Only then India’s goal of 

142 
 
Atmanirbhar Bharat in health technology can be achieved taking the nation towards 
efficient, equitable and sustainable healthcare. To ensure that the new HTA scheme 
provides affordability, access and cost saving and  maximum value for every rupee, the 
Committee, therefore, recommends mandatory provisions of i) time-bound integration 
of every HTA recommendation into appropriate national health programmes and 
essential medicine/device lists within 6 months, (ii) periodic capacity-building for 
officials and procurement officers (iii)  introduction of a performance-linked funding 
mechanism tied to number of HTA studies translated into policy within 12 months. 
According to the Committee, scientific evaluation of drugs, devices and treatment 
protocols should be the guide for health policy decisions to ensure optimal utilization of 
public resources. 
(Para 2.6.13) 
 
33. The Committee desires that more Research Hubs & Centres be established 
under the new HTA Scheme, particularly in unserved /underserved regions such as 
Jammu & Kashmir, Ladakh, the North-Eastern Region (NER) and in the Islands. 
These States/UTs have region-specific health challenges like, high-altitude diseases, 
tribal health issues, difficult terrain, which are poorly represented in current HTA 
studies. At present, HTAIn operates with only 8 Regional Hubs and 20 Resource 
Centres, the majority concentrated in southern states. Now, the proposal is to establish 
another 50 centres/hubs.The Committee observes that establishment of physical hubs is 
just an initial step. Without appropriate infrastructure and trained manpower, 
evidence generation for underserved areas is not possible. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that each proposed new centres/hubs should have a core team of HTA 
professionals including epidemiologists, health economists, and clinicians. Also, these 
remote hubs will need sustained annual recurring expenditure for manpower, training, 
data systems, etc. Hence, the Committee strongly recommends that these facts are 
appropriately taken care of in the new schema of HTA. 
(Para 2.6.14) 
 
Overview of Budget Allocation for DHR Schemes 
 
 
34. The Committee, as part of the examination of Demands of Grants 2026-27, has 
appraised the Committee on the importance of the schemes of DHR and the fast 
progress of the schemes. The Committee feels that the meagre increase in the allocation 
for the schemes, apart from ICMR, is not enough considering the projects lined up 
under these schemes by the Department. Further, while analysing the progress of the 
schemes under DHR, the Committee notes that certain schemes have been merged like 
Development of Tools/support to Prevent Outbreaks of Epidemics have been merged 
with VRDLs scheme and for other schemes like MRUs & MRHRUs draft EFC Notes 
seeking extension of the scheme for 16th FC period is awaiting approvals. According to 
DHR, the Department have re-sent higher amounts based on requirement of work of 
the schemes would be considered after the EFCs are done for the DHR schemes. 
Considering all these the Committee wants the DHR to fervently pursue the approval 
for their schemes and secure adequate funding so as to realise all the original objectives 
of the schemes to the best extent possible. 
(Para 3.1.3) 
 
Establishment of Network of Laboratories for Managing Epidemics and National 
Calamities- Viral Research & Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDLs) 
 
35. The Committee has been given to understand that the 22.3% increase of BE 
2026-2027 at ₹70.00 crore over 2025-2026 BE of the scheme has been earmarked taking 

143 
 
into consideration that the scheme of “Development of Tools / Support to Prevent 
Outbreaks & Epidemics,” is proposed to be discontinued from the 16thExpenditure 
Finance Cycle and its core components are to be integrated into the VRDL Scheme. 
Also, certain numbers of VRDLs are to be upgraded to IRDLs by including new 
components such as enhanced surveillance activities, support to IRDLs, NABL 
accreditation and External Quality Assessment (EQA), and contingency support for 
outbreak management. The funding pattern of the scheme suggests that the allocation 
over the past three years have been stagnant, but the utilization has been 100%. There 
has been a moderate increase of BE 2026-2027 at ₹70.00 crore against the projected 
demand of ₹180.00 crore thereby the percentage of variation of BE 2026-27 as 
compared to projected demand is 61.11 %. The Committee is of the opinion that it is 
disappointing, especially considering the significance of the scheme and some vital 
components are being merged with the scheme. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that with added responsibilities and structure of the scheme, the 
Department should make efforts to secure appropriate fund allocation under the 16th 
Finance Commission (2026-31) emphasizing the importance of investment in virology 
labs, genome surveillance and diagnostics to strengthen preparedness against future 
epidemics. 
(Para 3.2.8) 
 
36. The Committee, further, observes the fact that capital expenditure is ‘nil’ under 
the scheme because capex is proposed and released under the Scheme only when new 
VRDLs are sanctioned. During the 15th Finance Commission period, VRDLs that have 
completed five years of recurring support under the DHR Scheme and for the 16th 
Finance Commission period, it is proposed in the Expenditure Finance Committee that 
the recurring expenditure of IRDLs established under PM-ABHIM will be supported 
through the VRDL Scheme to ensure continuity and optimal utilization of the national 
laboratory network. Accordingly, DHR apprised the Committee that no proposals are 
there in the pipeline for new labs under the scheme. The Committee observes that the 
map of VRDL labs shows gaps in the geographical spread of State level and regional 
level labs. India is a country with more than a billion population, extreme topographic 
and climatic diversity accompanied by rising zoonotic and climate-amplified threats 
like, Nipah, Dengue surges, Avian influenza, etc which require that the gaps of regional 
and State level labs across the country should be abridged. Accordingly, the Committee
