recommends that VRDLs should be upgraded to IRDLs and these factors may be given 
due consideration and even upgradation across the country should be undertaken. 
(Para 3.2.9) 
 
37. The Committee takes note of the achievements of VRDL scheme like Integrating 
of VRDLN portal with Integrated Health Information Platform (IHIP) which has 
enhanced the public health surveillance and the launching of the Quarterly VRDL 
Bulletin at the National VRDL Conclave 2025 has provided a transparent platform for 
real-time data on viral infection trends. The Committee is of the view that the viral data 
base of VRDLs must serve as systematic sentinel surveillance platform in India for 
early detection of emerging/ re-emerging infectious diseases in the ensuing year, 
keeping into consideration the veiled threat of high risk pathogens viz., MPoX, Nipah, 
Zika, CCHF, Avian influenza etc.The Committee observes that such steps would ensure 
early-warning and containment of outbreaks. 
(Para 3.2.10) 
 
Development of Infrastructure for Promotion of Health Research 
 
38. The Committee is pleased to note that the Development of Infrastructure for 
Promotion of Health Research is one umbrella scheme which has been granted the 

144 
 
Projected Demand for the FY 2026-27 of Rs 171.35 crore as BE 2026-27. Even though 
the BE 2026-27 of Rs 171.35 crore is lesser but the RE 2025-26, it is still a good start. In 
the opinion of the Committee, accurate assessment of gaps in financial resource with 
justified projections is the reason for the Department securing the Projected Demands. 
Hence, it feels that the Department has earned the Projected Demands for the scheme 
as the utilization over the years has been close to 100%. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends the Department to continue with the judicious utilisation of enhanced 
funds and build confidence in its capacity to deploy and absorb resources effectively for 
nationwide research infrastructure. 
(Para 3.3.6) 
 
39. The Committee, further observes a thrust towards the funding of MRUs at Rs 
100.27 crores in comparison to that of MRHRUs at Rs 71.08 crores for the FY 2026-
27.The Committee over the time emphasised on adequate attention towards both rural 
health research and multi-disciplinary research in medical colleges. But, when more 
thrust is being given to MRUs, it by default leads to rural areas being left out as these 
areas lack in government medical colleges compared to urban areas. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends more or at least equal thrust for both areas and to ensure apt 
push is given under MRUs to government medical colleges in rural areas. This would be 
in alignment with National Health Policy goals for more research in underserved areas. 
(Para 3.3.7) 
 
40. The Committee, keeping in mind the increasing life span of the Indian 
population, has highlighted the arising need for attention towards the problems related 
to ageing and geriatrics. In this regard, it is curious to know about the 
recommendations of the Performance Evaluation Committee (PEC) constituted vide 
order dated 19th December, 2022 by DHR on the establishment of a new institute- 
National Institute of Ageing and Healthy Longevity. National Centre for Ageing, 
AIIMS, is one of the government institutions which are currently dedicated for the 
cause. The Committee wants to be appraised on the progress made on this 
recommendation of the PEC. The Committee recommends that proactive steps shall be 
taken for the establishment of the National Institute of Ageing and Healthy Longevity 
with approved traditional and integrative medical practices. Also, DHR should 
collaborate with AIIMS to create protocol of treatment to address the problems related 
to ageing and geriatrics. That is so because AIIMS has elaborate infrastructure and 
treatment experience for ageing and geriatrics. 
(Para 3.3.8) 
Multi-Disciplinary Research Units (MRUs) 
 
41.  The Committee notes that the MRU scheme has achieved the annual targets 
throughout the 15th Financial Commission period. The projected demand of Rs 100.27 
crores for the FY 2026-27 has been allocated with one of the targets being establishment 
of new MRUs. In this regard, the Committee observes that MRUs should be more 
evenly distributed across the country. The Committee finds from the map submitted by 
DHR on MRUs across India that there are gaps in areas like western and central India. 
The Committee recommends that the Department should consider these areas for the 
establishment of new MRUs. Further, as a significant portion of funds are allocated to 
extramural projects and MRUs, proper and timely feedback on their utilization and 
outcomes are to be ensured through a well-defined Utilization Report System. 
(Para 3.3.14) 
 
42.  One of the ways to improve the performance of MRUs according to a Third-
Party Evaluation is going beyond uniform allocations and shifting to 

145 
 
performance/requirement-based funding in deserving cases. The Committee feels that 
the contention may contain merit but, in its opinion, to ensure even ground, the varying 
constraints under which different beneficiary institutions operate has to be 
appropriately factored in while deciding upon the parameters to assess the 
performance. Accordingly, the Committee recommends that the Department create a 
dedicated MRU monitoring cell under DHR with real-time tracking for both physical 
and financial progress for evaluating performance/requirement-based funding beyond 
uniform allocations. 
(Para 3.3.15) 
 
Model Rural Health Research Units (MRHRUs) 
43.  The Committee has been given to understand that increase in cost of 
construction of MRHRUs buildings under Civil Work is one of the components under 
the MRHRUs scheme for which allocation of Rs 71.08 crores for the FY 2026-27 has 
been made. Further, the Committee observes that as per report of a Third-Party 
Evaluation on the performance of MRHRUs, one of the areas of concern is ensuring 
continued functionality and optimal utilization of infrastructure, financial support for 
civil repairs/maintenance of equipment in MRHRUs. The Committee observes that the 
ICMR responsible institution is assigned a MRHRU project to coordinate with the State 
Government concerned and that institution oversees civil works and infrastructure 
development, procurement and installation of equipment, etc, of the project. In this 
regard, the Committee recommends that the ICMR be responsible to undertake a 
comprehensive annual audit of the MRHRUs project assigned to them, including actual 
expenditure versus sanctioned grants and submit the same to be made part of DHR’s 
annual report. 
(Para 3.3.21) 
 
44.  Another area of concern is the human resource structure for MRHRUs. The 
prerequisite of MRHRUs are adequate staff strength with posts including clinicians, 
epidemiologists, bio-statisticians, technicians, etc., as lack of human resources would 
directly affect research outputs of MRHRUs. The Committee has been recommending 
hardship allowance and fast-track promotion for rural postings to attract more human 
resource. The Committee is reiterating its earlier recommendation for hardship 
allowance, fast-track promotion and other appropriate measures for rural postings. 
Adequate human resource is essential to strengthen research institutions in tribal and 
aspirational districts. This would ensure balanced regional growth and take care that 
will no region left behind in healthcare preparedness. 
(Para3.3.22) 
 
Human Resource Development (HRD) for Health Research 
 
45. The Committee observes that DHR along with ICMR has done much in the post 
pandemic era under the Human Resource Development Scheme for Health Research to 
encourage health research among mid - career Scientists, medical students, etc. The 
hand-holding to applicants through grant writing workshops and webinars on a regular 
basis is quite appreciable. Further, the communication channels of ICMR, including 
ICMR radio and institutional networks have actively leveraged to disseminate 
information about the HRD Scheme among the researchers. The Committee, however, 
wants to be apprised about the parameters associated with First in the World Challenge 
scheme in R&D. 
(Para 3.5.10) 
 

146 
 
46. The Committee notes that the Output-Outcome Monitoring Framework 
document of Ministry of Health & Family Welfare, shows Outputs 2026-27 for the 
number of fellowships awarded as 3100 and the number of fellowships awarded for 
women scientist as 30 under the scheme for the FY 2026-27. The difference in the 
number of fellowships awarded for women scientists against the total fellowships 
awarded is disappointing. The Committee recommends that special provisions for 
women, young NRI/PIO/OCI scientists/researchers and also scientists/researchers from 
underrepresented regions like the North-East and the Islands should be given more 
thrust. Further, DHR can also look into the possibility of collaboration with reputed 
private entities for co-funding fellowships, workshops, grants, etc, to reduce 
dependency on government budgets. Capacity building is another area where support 
of private entities can be factored in. The Committee also supports and recommends the 
steps taken towards reviewing the medical education curriculum to incorporate 
research training aspects among the future clinicians. 
(Para 3.5.11) 
Grants-in-aid Scheme for Inter-Sectoral Convergence and Coordination for Promotion 
& Guidance on Health Research (GIA) 
 
47. The Committee reckons that the Grant-in-aid Scheme for Inter-Sectoral 
Convergence for Health Research has been reviewed and revised by the Department 
and is hopeful that it will lead towards effective implementation of the Scheme. As per 
the revision funding for joint initiatives is given thrust. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends following collaborative research areas that need to be prioritised:- 
(i) Environmental and Occupational Health Impacts on Public Health in 
coordination with the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change. 
 
(ii) One Health Approaches for Zoonotic and Infectious Diseases in partnership 
with the Department of Animal Husbandry and Dairying &MoEFCC. 
 
(iii) Nutrition, Maternal, and Child Health Interventions in collaboration with 
the Ministry of Women and Child Development & Ministry of Agriculture 
and Farmers Welfare. 
 
(iv) Geriatric care in coordination with the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare's NCD programs, Department of Science and Technology. 
 
(v) Tribal and Rural Health Research with Focus on Traditional Medicine in 
collaboration with the Ministry of AYUSH & Ministry of Tribal Affairs. 
(Para 3.5.16) 
 
48.  In Committee’s considered opinion such inter domain studies would address 
neglected areas like workplace hazards, effects of pollution, prevention, and control of 
zoonoses, congenital malformations, herbal remedies, etc, therefore, DHR should chalk 
out Action Plan based on the suggested recommendation. 
(Para 3.5.17) 
 
DHR-ICMR Advanced Molecular Oncology Diagnostic Services and Research 
(DIAMOnDSR). 
 
49. The Committee takes cognisance of the work being undertaken by DHR/ICMR 
through the DIAMOnDSR scheme. According to the Department, the DIAMOnDS 
scheme focuses have been on lung and breast cancers. The Committee recommends that 
due focus maybe directed to other types of cancers as well as biomarkers.  

147 
 
(Para 3.5.25) 
 
50. On the Uniform Cancer Treatment Protocol, the Committee acquiesce with the 
submission of ICMR, being the field experts, that it does not recommend “uniform 
cancer treatment protocol”. Having said that the Committee observes that the 
treatment procedures and cost involved in a particular type of cancer in the country 
have wide gaps between a government institution and a private institution. While the 
Committee acknowledges that DHR does not have any say in the cost involved in 
treatment procedures, the Committee recommends the ICMR’s National Cancer 
Registry Programme (NCRP) along with the D/o H&FW should undertake a study to 
identify a way to address the issue of wide gaps in treatment procedures for a particular 
type of cancer between a government institution and a private institution. The 
Committee, however, is of the considered view that as a beginning, DHR should 
formulate treatment protocol for common cancers like oral, cervical and breast cancers 
and come up with a broad based for its treatment procedures and guidelines. Like the 
submission of ICMR, since National Institute of Cancer Prevention & research 
(NICPR)‑ICMR updates are directly linked to national clinical guidelines, the 
Committee perceives such a study would help to lessen the gaps. 
 
(Para 3.5.26) 
 
51. Regarding anti-cancer vaccine, the Committee has been impressed upon that 
efforts and scientific progress is being made in this field globally. The availability of 
personalized mRNA-based vaccines for specific cancers and its clinical use depends on 
the completion of ongoing trials and subsequent regulatory framework. The only anti-
cancer vaccination as of now available, both globally as well as in India, is the HPV 
vaccine. The Committee hopes that India, through ICMR, enters this field as the rising 
incidence of cancer among the population, especially among the youth, in recent times is 
alarmingly high. Accordingly, the Committee recommends ICMR should make efforts 
to make itself a part of on-going anti- cancer vaccination research projects globally in 
coordination with international entities like WHO and come forward for regulatory 
approval for clinical use. 
(Para 3.5.27) 
 
Prime Minister's Ayushman Bharat Health Infrastructure Mission (PM-ABHIM) -Bio-
Security 
 
52. The Committee takes into account the submission of the Department that the 
fund allocated under the PM ABIM scheme for 2025–26 of ₹350.49 crore and ₹351.06 
crore 2026-27 is broadly unchanged as the scheme is presently in a consolidation and 
transition phase. The objectives under the 15th FC plan period have largely been 
completed. The Committee also notes the submission of the DHR that regarding the 
adequacy of the fund of ₹351.06 crore of 2026–27 being is sufficient for sustaining 
ongoing projects and initiating incremental platform strengthening. Funding will be 
reviewed at RE stage in line with the pace of platform roll-out and readiness of 
implementing entities. The Committee, keeping into consideration the interdisciplinary 
and cross-sectoral nature of One Health and bio-security, full-scale national 
platforming and rapid expansion may require additional, phased investments and 
convergence with allied sectoral programs, recommends that DHR should persuade the 
Ministry of Finance to allocate funds as per  needs at RE stage.  
(Para 3.6.15) 
53.  Having acknowledged the statements of the Department, the Committee wants to 
point out that last year the Department had to review and revise timeline for each of the 
pending projects. The reasons being the delay in identification of specialised BSL-3/4 

148 
 
agencies, revisions in drawings due to consultant recommendations, budget escalation 
arising from GST increase and electrical scope changes, change of land use approvals 
are some of them. The Committee feels that these technical/administrative delays will 
eventually culminate in budget escalation. In this regard, the Committee had 
recommended to streamline tendering/procurement and to fix responsibilities by 
imposing penalties. In view of the unchanged situation prevailing, the Committee wants 
to reiterate the same recommendation. Further, the Committee recommends the use of 
digital platforms for tendering/procurement, use Al technology for real-time 
surveillance of constructions of BSL -3/4 labs, VRDLs, etc. 
(Para 3.6.16) 
54. While appreciating the number of BSL-3 spread around the country as on date, 
the Committee feels that there are still areas which are underrepresented. Hence, the 
Committee recommends more BSL 3 labs in the scenario of rising zoonotic risks, 
climate-induced diseases, etc. The Committee also notes that there is only one BSL-4 lab 
in the country, in Dibrugarh. The Committee recommends that the Department should 
make efforts to construct at least three more BSL-4 labs in the 16th Plan period 
especially keeping in mind that the M/o H&FW has sought for Rs. 69,458.44 crores for 
2026-31for PM-ABHIM Scheme. 
(Para 3.6.17) 
National One Health Mission 
 
55. The Committee observes that National One Health Mission has the involvement 
of around 13 Departments, and more may be added to the list in the future, also, the 
mandate of the Mission pans the whole of India. The Committee is of the firm belief that 
DHR, ably guided by the Principal Scientific Advisor to Government of India, is well 
endowed to be the nodal department for leading the mission to success. Nevertheless, the 
Committee recommends for measures like digitized procurement, expansion of 
VRDLs/IRDLs, unified platforms for real-time data accessible to all, domain specific 
research hubs, local community involvement at grassroot levels, etc., to avoid 
coordination delays and ensuring inter ministerial integration.  
(Para 3.7.5) 
 
North-Eastern Areas 
 
56. The Committee acknowledges the work done by the Department under its 
various schemes in NER, especially setting up of VRDLs (Viral Research & Diagnostic 
Labs), activities under the HRD scheme, etc. Regarding the fund requirement, the 
Committee notes the submission of DHR that while the allocation to NER is 
proportionate to the prescribed formula, in case of additional requirements based on 
project proposals, research priorities, or State‑specific demands, the Department is 
open to consider enhanced fund support beyond the mandatory allocation, subject to 
availability of funds and due procedure. 
(Para 3.8.8) 
57. The Committee is aware that NER is prone to vector-borne disease burden due 
to its humid climate, dense forests and border proximity to high-risk areas. Considering 
this, the Committee recommends that ICMR should initiate investigator-initiated 
research projects on vector-borne disease which can yield tailored vaccines, diagnostics, 
and prevention strategies, reducing mortality rates in NER. This would be a step 
toward the national campaign of Malaria Elimination Program.  
(Para 3.8.9) 
58. Another field in NER where DHR along with Ministry of AYUSH should invest 
is the cataloguing and testing of medicinal plants involving local universities and tribal 

149 
 
communities. Such activities would be a boost to Traditional and Integrative Medicine 
practices. Research hubs for AI-driven telemedicine, mobile health apps, and remote 
diagnostics for chronic diseases are another area which can benefit the NER 
considering its terrain. NER is highly vulnerable to climate change, with frequent 
disasters exposing the people living there to conditions like malnutrition, hypertension, 
etc. Studies co-relating climate events and health outcomes, including mental health and 
nutritional security should be considered to understand and help people who are 
repeatedly exposed to natural disasters. Accordingly, the Committee recommends these 
initiatives from the part of DHR/ ICMR for NER.   
(Para 3.8.10) 
 
Funding; Extramural vs Intramural 
 
59. The Committee understands that over recent past, ICMR has shifted focus on 
two core areas – from descriptive to developmental research and gravitating towards 
extramural research instead of intramural. Such change in approach is appreciated and 
is viewed as a measure towards future readiness/preparedness to combat health 
challenges. The Committee is of the considered opinion that ICMR should continue 
funding more developmental and extramural projects so that larger numbers of 
research entities are encouraged to participate in the nation’s progress in health 
research.   
(Para 4.6) 
 
Budgetary allocation of ICMR 
 
60. The Committee notes the fact that out of the total allocation of DHR for the FY 
2026-27 of Rs. 4,821 crores, an amount of Rs. 4,000 crores is the share of ICMR. 
Further, it is also understood that amount does not include any fund from revenue or 
from any other source from within the ICMR but it is the budgetary allocation from the 
Government to ICMR. The submission of the Secretary, DHR that drew the attention 
of the Committee was that the ICMR pensions are not paid out of the salaries Head, but 
out of the Grants-in-Aid General which is basically the amount of money reflected to be 
spent on research i.e. research projects and research institutions. The Committee finds 
that the objective of Grants-in-Aid Scheme is to provide fund for research projects. 
While Grants-in-Aid General Head cover funding for other items alongwith research 
project. The Committee seeks the Department to submit a clarification in the matter.  
(Para 4.7.5) 
 
61. The Committee is appreciative about the effective utilisation of funds by ICMR. 
Over the years ICMR has always made efforts for maximum utilization of allocated 
funds. This year the allocation is a remarkable hike from previous years, which is an 
addition of Rs 850.00 Cr. Even though the projected demand for 2026-27 by ICMR was 
Rs. 4550crores, it got an allocation of Rs 4000 crores. Considering that ICMR has 
demanded for more funds, the Committee reckons that it has more projects planned for 
the year. Nevertheless, going by the trend of last two years, ICMR has always been 
granted higher allocation at the RE stage. Of course, such increase at RE stage is not at 
par with the projected demand. Addition at RE stage suggests optimal utilization of 
allocated funds and project efficiency. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
ICMR keep their good track record of actual utilization of allocated fund and physical 
progress of its projects against measurable outcomes. 
(Para 4.7.6) 
 
62. On the Committee’s recommendation that ICMR is to be allowed to retain the 
revenues generated by them rather than depositing all of it to the Consolidated Fund of 

150 
 
India, DHR had submitted that the revenue so generated is being appropriately 
recorded by ICMR Accounts section and shall be reflected in the Demand No 47. 
However, the Committee doesn’t find the refection of the same in the Demand No 47- 
2026-27. Accordingly, the Committee reiterates its recommendation on the issue. 
(Para 4.7.7) 
 
63. The Committee notes that Performance Evaluation Committees of ICMR are 
formed every 5 years and the last committee was constituted in 2022. Its report reflects 
0.01% of GDP as Government’s spending on Health Research in India, But, on the 
examination of Demand for Grants of DHR during 2026-27, it was informed that the 
Government spends 0.02% of GDP for Health Research vis-a vis U.S. National 
Institutes of Health receives about $47.77 billion annually (0.17% of GDP), the UK 
Medical Research Council $12.33 billion (0.36% of GDP), and Australia’s National 
Health and Medical Research Council $1.34 billion (0.07% of GDP). The percentage of 
the Government’s spending on Health Research in India has been stagnant for the last 
5 years. Nevertheless, the statistics is disappointing. The Government is doing a lot of 
things in its attempt to make India a developed country. Viskit Bharat@47, which is a 
part of this attempt, is a revolutionary thought and to achieve that the nation need a 
young population as well as a healthy population. The Committee, therefore 
recommends for higher budgetary support to DHR as percentage of GDP as 
comparable to leading Asian economies such as China, South Korea, Japan, etc. Efforts 
to be made to choke out a road map to bridge the research and investment gap by 
extracting measurable outcome from intramural and extramural project, especially in 
terms of publications, patents and clinical guidelines. The Committee further 
recommends for adequate budgetary support towards theoretic development advanced 
trails and indigenous innovations. Action plans should be chalked out for PPP based 
funding on mission mode to reduce long term dependency on expensive imported 
therapies.  
(Para 4.7.9) 
 
64. Every health scheme/program/research/study should be conceived as a step 
towards creating a healthy population who would directly/indirectly contribute to the 
GDP of the country. The Committee feels that at 0.02% of its GDP, India is at a very 
lower position compared to developed countries in the field of Health Research 
expenditure. As of now, India’s representation and contribution to the global health 
forums is not much remarkable. The Committee feels that India has no dearth of 
talents. Accordingly, the Committee recommends the Department to timely fulfil their 
commitments towards the on-going projects, which would in turn help them secure 
more financial assistance from the Union Government for completing their research 
projects in time. The Committee reiterates its recommendation that ICMR should be 
allowed to retain the revenues generated by ICMR rather than depositing all of it to the 
Consolidated Fund of India as well as collaborate with prominent private players in the 
field of medical research to enhance its funds. The Committee desires that ICMR 
should strive to raise extra budgetary resources upto 25% from other sources to fulfil 
its requirements for undertaking research projects. 
(Para 4.7.10) 
 
65. Further, the Committee endorses the suggestions of the Performance Evaluation 
Committee on having a peer review and third party monitoring system integrating 
constructive feedback as well as enhancement of India’s presence, visibility and 
influence in the WHO system through active participation in global health forums. 

151 
 
Accordingly, the Committee recommends necessary steps to be taken in achieving the 
suggestions of the Performance Evaluation Committee. 
(Para 4.7.11) 
Physical outcomes of ICMR projects 
 
66. The Committee takes note of the progress made by ICMR in the previous years 
on various aspects like core research, research papers published, patents filed and 
patents granted, etc. The Committee also acknowledges that ICMR has contributed to 
addressing the health issues affecting the masses of the country. Nevertheless, the 
Committee is concerned to note that a portable X-Ray machine is being cited as being 
made under Make in India despite its important components like HV tank and X ray 
tube not being made indigenously. The Committee recommends that ICMR should set 
up a standardised guideline to classify its products under Make in India so that it forms 
a bench mark for the private Sector to imbibe and inculcate. Mere assembly of 
imported components should not form the basis for classification under Make in India. 
The Committee appreciates that ICMR has developed its own AI platform for rapid 
diagnosis of Chest and other X-rays. Accordingly, the Committee strongly recommends 
that this AI should be provided free of cost to all CHCs/PHCs and Government 
Hospitals so that primary diagnosis can be made under the TB Muktbharat campaign. 
The Committee also recommends that the pricing of AI needs to be justified in a 
country like India where AI can be a game changer to provide affordable health care on 
a population scale. 
(Para 4.8.3) 
 
67. The Committee would like to recommend an impact assessment of the work 
carried out by ICMR, through a Third-Party, to establish a structured mechanism to 
monitor and regulate the outcomes and overall impact of these initiatives. In this 
regard, the Committee wishes to be appraised also on research projects, if any, of 
ICMR that had to be discontinued and the reasons therefore. 
(Para 4.8.4) 
 
Initiatives of ICMR- Medical Innovations Patent Mitra & MedTech Mitra 
 
68. The Committee recognizes the relevance of initiatives like MedTech Mitra and 
Patent Mitra. These are handholding measures for innovators for clinical evaluation, 
regulatory facilitation, execution of license agreements and uptake of new products. 
The Committee believes that the Department should make women, young and rural 
innovators primary beneficiaries in their handholding measures. In this regard, the 
Committee recommends phased funding at different levels of product readiness as 
innovators struggles to reach towards finished products/technology. There should be 
regional hubs related to these handholding initiatives to cater to local innovators which 
will also mitigate urban bias. The Department should seek to address the issue of low 
market penetration of new products/technology under these initiatives. This is mainly 
due to the mismatch between the demand and the final product. Last, but not the least, 
the Committee recommends effective capacity building programmes for nascent 
innovators about the various technical and procedural complexities involved in  market 
entry with new products/technology. According to the Committee, start-ups and 
innovators must be supported enough so India becomes a global hub for affordable 
health solutions. 
(Para 4.9.5) 
 
 
 
 

152 
 
Medical Innovations Development Acceleration Council (MIDAC) 
 
69. The Committee recalls that Medical Innovations Development Advancement 
Company (MIDAC) was presented before it as a transformative initiative of DHR last 
year during the examination of DFG of DHR. MIDAC, envisioned as a Section-8 
company under the Companies Act, 2013, aims to serve as a catalyst for translational 
research and technology development. Within five years, it intends to support the 
development of 25 regulation-compliant products, including formulations, vaccines, and 
medical devices, and 50 regulation-compliant diagnostics. However, the Committee 
finds that the much-anticipated company is still entangled in procedural matters 
connected to approvals. The Committee is of the considered view that DHR should not 
linger the formation of MIDAC, a Section-8 company under the Companies Act, 2013. 
It is apparent to the Committee that DHR has taken shelter behind the caveat of every 
procedural requirement being followed. In view of this, the Committee strongly 
recommends that DHR shall vigorously pursue the final approvals for MIDAC and 
ensure that the delay should not extend beyond a month with the Registrar of 
Companies and the Company must see the light of the day during the ensuing financial 
year. 
(Para 4.9.8) 
Indian Clinical Trial and Education Network (INTENT):  
70. The Committee appreciates the Budget announcement 2026-27 regarding 1000 
clinical trial sites under BioPharma SHAKTI Mission with an outlay Rs 10,000 crores 
over five years indented to mark  a strategic shift towards strengthening India’s 
biopharma ecosystem. The Committee has given to understand that creation of a 
national network of 1000 clinical trial sites is one of the components of Biopharma 
SHAKTI which will be led by the ICMR. The Committee firmly believes Biopharma 
SHAKTI Mission, a core strategic intervention aimed at strengthening India’s 
biopharmaceutical ecosystem will definitely serve as an essential pillar required to 
meet the growing demands of the Indian pharma industry and to position India as a 
competitive global hub for biopharma innovation. The Committee in this regard, 
recommends ICMR to leverage its prior experience and strength by creating and 
strengthening 1000 clinical trial sites, and adopting a ‘Hub and Spoke model’ to meet 
the needs of the Indian Pharma Industry.  
(Para 4.9.16) 
 
71. The Committee reckons that to regulate clinical trials, DHR registers Ethics 
Committees (EC) to review Biomedical and Health Research which also involves human 
participants through the NAITIK portal. Further, the Committee also acknowledges 
the reforms undertaken by DHR to facilitate public access to necessary trial 
information, like introduction of multilingual interface, mobile friendly platforms, 
mandatory registration tied to funding/publication, awareness campaigns for public, 
researchers & institutions, etc. In this regard, the Committee holds the view that 
regulatory reforms and ease of doing research, while maintaining ethical standards, 
needs to be encouraged. Maintaining ethical standards itself is a challenge viz. hurdles 
include uneven Ethics Committees quality, delays, and vulnerability in multicentric 
trials, etc. Hence, the Committee recommends further strengthening of the role of DHR 
as a regulatory entity through frequent trainings for Ethics Committees members and 
participants, introduction of digital tools for monitoring and timely review of 
guidelines. ICMR should ensure that participation in clinical trials is based on genuine 
informed consent and all measures to ensure this shall be taken by DHR. 
(Para 4.9.17) 

153 
 
 
 
Contemporary and Critical Areas of Research in India 
72 Almost all areas of research taken up ICMR are those which can bring in 
transformative impact in accordance with the National Health Policy outcomes. The 
Committee extends all its support to the ICMR Centre for Advanced Research on 
Addictive Behaviours (CAR-AB) being established at AIIMS Delhi, with a focus 
initiative for delivering evidence-based interventions targeting excessive and 
problematic use of technology and associated stress, depression, anxiety, and 
addiction. The Committee is of the considered view that mental health is an area where 
integration of Indian Knowledge Sytsem through methodology like yoga meditation can 
bring in lot of positive changes in behaviour science. Accordingly, the Committee 
recommends that both DHR/ICMR and Ministry of Ayush adopt steps to develop a 
treatment protocol wherein safe integration of traditional/ alternative treatment along 
with allopathic treatment can be effectively used in the area of mental health. 
(Para 4.10.2) 
73 The Committee recommends that ICMR should undertake a study to look into the 
possibility of developing indigenous Sickle Cell Anaemia from Sugarcane derivatives as 
has been developed in countries like USA/Japan.  Regarding hypertension, ICMR had 
conducted a study on “Genetic Evaluation of Hypertension in the North East 
population during the period of 2015 to 2018’, the Committee recommends that ICMR 
should also undertake a study on the impact of hypertension and diabetic among the 
people of India in the post pandemic period. Further, ICMR has informed that there 
are many studies from various groups in India on genetic factors related to diabetes. 
Study of genetics of monogenic diabetes is already in routine clinical practice. The 
Committee understands that millets help in the management of body weight, which is of 
utmost importance in diabetic patient. In this regard, the Committee desires that ICMR 
should undertake a study on the merits of the millet diet as part of promotion of its 
usage especially among children. Such studies should help including millet in the 
nutritional status of children in POSHAN Abhiyaan.   
 (Para 4.10.3) 
74 The Committee has been given to understand that ICMR has published a White 
paper on venomous snakebite in India in 2020* which was acknowledged in the 
“National Action Plan for Prevention and Control of Snakebite Envenoming 
(NAPSE)”, launched by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare in March 2024, 
with the aim to halve snakebite-related deaths and disabilities by 2030 in India. The 
Committee in this regard recommends that ICMR should identify the specify area of 
more snake bite and undertake research on various types of snakes bites especially by 
King Cobra, Russell’s viper, Karaita, etc which are highly venomous snake species and 
adequate arrangement be made for anti-venom doses in district hospitals and rural 
block hospitals.  Anti-venom doses of specific species of snakes may be stored in 
hospitals of the area where such species of snakes are found in plenty. 
(Para 4.10.4) 
75 The Committee, taking note of the fact that ICMR–DHR has done significant 
research and public‑health interventions in Uttar Pradesh and Bihar to reduce 
mortality from Japanese Encephalitis (JE)/Acute Encephalitis Syndrome (AES), 
however, the Committee observes that Japanese Encephalitis are much more prevalent 
in North India, ie, from Uttar Pradesh to North Bihar (from Muzafarpur to 

154 
 
Gorakhpur and Gaya) and there is toll of death of children who are vulnerable to the 
disease. The Committee therefore recommends ICMR and DHR to undertake in-depth 
research in JE and desires that the government should give more grants to support the 
research projects on the subject. 
(Para 4.10.5) 
76 The Committee views that DHR should promote R&D under PPP model in 
order to extract maximum outcome for preventing the prevalence of various diseases in 
specific part of the country. The Committee desires that multinational companies or 
companies manufacturing drugs on large scale and earning high profit should be 
guided to make expenditure of certain percentage of profit on R&D with the purpose of 
reducing the cost of drugs and treatment of disease at an affordable cost on common 
diseases like JE, kidney failure, fatty liver, etc. The Committee is of the opinion that 
each regional AIIMS may be entrusted with health research on specific disease 
prevalent in that area with enhanced budgetary support to undertake R&D projects. 
The Committee notes that ICMR has informed that under the National Health 
Research Priority (NHRP) on stillbirth, being implemented across seven states, the 
focus is on prevention of stillbirth through improving the uptake and coverage of 
evidence-based antenatal and intrapartum interventions. One of the strategies under 
this project is early detection of fetal anomalies by including early pregnancy 
registration and attendance at Pradhan Mantri Surakshit MatritvaAbhiyan (PMSMA).  
The Committee in this regard recommends that the ICMR should expand the NHRP on 
stillbirth across the country instead of limiting it in just seven states. The Committee 
feels that the role of frontline workers likes Accredited Social Health Activist (ASHAs) 
and Auxiliary Nurse Midwife (ANM) is cardinal in early pregnancy registration and 
attendance at PMSMA, especially in rural/tribal areas accordingly adequate training 
should be imparted for ASHAs and ANM in this area.  
(Para 4.10.6) 
 
Non-Communicable Diseases (NCDs) 
 
77. The Committee takes into account the submission of DHR that ICMR has 
adopted a dual approach that advances NCD research while maintaining a strong focus 
on infectious disease preparedness. The Committee is given to understand that while for 
communicable diseases, the National One Health Mission has been launched while 
Multidisciplinary Research Units (MRUs) in Medical Colleges/Research Institutes 
undertake research primarily in Non- Communicable Diseases. Considering that India 
is experiencing a shift in disease patterns, with NCDs, causing nearly 60% of all deaths, 
the Committee recommends DHR to revisit the funding pattern and strategy by giving 
thrust to NCD research at the same time sustaining the intensity on infectious disease 
projects. The Committee recommends that preventive healthcare with focused research 
on early detection of lifestyle diseases and its affordable treatment should be 
prioritized. 
(Para 4.10.10) 
 
Anti-Microbial Resistance 
 
78. Antimicrobial Resistance (AMR) identified as a major future threat is already 
impinging treatment protocols. DHR through ICMR operates an AMR Surveillance 
Network and contributes evidence on key resistant strains of pathogens found in 
clinical settings. Also, the National Action Plan (NAP)-AMR 2.0 (2025-2029) emphasizes 
One Health surveillance and thereby integrates environmental data into human health 

155 
 
research. In this backdrop, the Committee recommends that DHR/ICMR should 
establish AMR-specific hubs and promote community One health training, focusing 
inter alia on animal-human interface. The Committee also holds the opinion that the 
Department should also launch a national risk mapping app for zoonoses and integrate 
its findings into antimicrobial stewardship and protocols nationwide. 
(Para 4.10.15) 
 
79. The Committee is given to understand that the use of technology/products like 
BIOFIRE is reported as a major step towards the antimicrobial fight. Patients who 
earlier had to remain in the ICU for around 10 days, with the help of BIOFIRE, can 
now receive antibiotic sensitivity reports within just 2 to 4 hours, leading to faster and 
more targeted treatment. This has substantially reduced the overall cost and improved 
clinical effectiveness following its introduction. However, the Committee is told that the 
technology/products like BIOFIRE are currently imported and are expensive per se. 
Accordingly, the Committee recommends DHR to look into the possibility of developing 
such technology/products indigenously so that it can be made accessible more 
commonly and to smaller healthcare set ups also as part of affordable health care 
initiatives. 
(Para 4.10.16) 
 
HPV Vaccine 
 
80. The Committee reckons the importance of national HPV vaccination programme. 
The Committee understands that ICMR is engaged in the "Antibody Response 
following One Dose of Cervavac® as compared to One Dose of Gardasil Vaccine in 
Healthy Girls Aged 9-14 Years: A Non-inferiority, Multicentre, Double-blind, 
Randomized Controlled Trial" to determine whether the antibody response elicited by 
one dose of Cervavac® (study product) is non-inferior to one dose of Gardasil®. The 
result of the trial would to aid in adoption of WHO’s single dose HPV vaccination 
strategy as a public health initiative by India. Further, the Home-grown single dose 
vaccines, if proven to be non-inferior, would save import costs and duties, thus 
rendering the vaccination more affordable as well as effective. The Committee feels that 
to make the trial result oriented, a transparent narration of the facts related to the 
merits of the vaccination connecting it to cervical cancer prevention to build public 
trust is important. It is also pertinent to make the public aware that the impact of 
vaccination programme can be observed one and half decades after the vaccination, 
when the recipients have grown into the age group at risk. The Committee firmly 
believes that HPV vaccination is a corrective and a way forward step. The delay in the 
roll out of the Universal Immunization Programme (UIP) maybe due to lack of 
information among the public. Hence, the Committee strongly recommends publishing 
of a detailed fact sheet on the ICMR/DHR website outlining the primary objectives and 
scope of HPV. The Committee is of the view that HPV vaccination if implemented with 
transparency and community involvement, the programme would emerge as a 
consequential public health achievement in protecting next generation of women from 
preventable malignancies, and thus reclaiming preponderance of prevention of cancer 
in national health policy. 
(Para 4.10.24) 
 
81.  The Committee further recognises the fact that if the trial could demonstrate 
Cervavac®’s single-dose non-inferiority, it would be a "major fillip" by enabling faster 
rollout in the Universal Immunization Programme (UIP). Positive culmination of the 
trial would be an unequivocal boost to indigenous production and lower cost offers 
economic benefits like forex savings, job creation in biotech, etc. Single dosage can 
make UIP more successful as human lapses and follow up of multiple doses can be 

156 
 
avoided. Accordingly, the Committee wants to render all its support to and pin its 
expectations on to the Department to act on the recommendations of the Committee.  
(Para 4.10.25) 
Oral Health 
82.  Anant Muskaan, ICMR's National Health Research Priority Program, is aimed at 
promoting awareness of the importance of oral health in school children. The project 
focus on primary school-based tooth brushing and oral health education to prevent 
costly oral diseases and reduce out-of-pocket expenditure. It is a cost-effective 
preventive intervention. The Committee perceives that the extension of the Anant 
Muskaan Project beyond the current seven districts would be considered upon 
successful completion of the two-year implementation phase in February 2027. The 
Committee recommends that the project be expanded nationally which would address 
the issue of oral disease burden and recommends the project to be integrated with the 
School Health & Wellness Programme (SHWP), under the Ayushman Bharat. This 
would be an ideal move towards affordability over treatment.  
(Para 4.10.29) 
 
Food safety 
 
83. The Committee is impressed with the number of programmes/studies undertaken by 
ICMR in the country in the field of nutrition science and policy along with food safety. 
The Committee notes that majority of the studies undertaken by ICMR are hinged on 
the biomedical aspect. The Committee feels ICMR would be the ideal forum to initiate 
studies on India's diverse food ecosystem. For this, ICMR should include experts from 
agriculture (ICAR), environment (MoEFCC), and FSSAI while identifying the subjects 
of study to address real-world problems. The Committee strongly recommends a Multi-
Disciplinary Food Safety Research Panel for this. Another recommendation of the 
Committee is that due attention shall be given to changing food habits of the population 
across the societal strata keeping in mind aspects like easier access to fast food with the 
advent of fast food delivery at door steps primarily through platform-based services. At 
the same time, there is also an emerging cross section of population who is very health 
conscious. The Committee strongly feels that if the gist of findings in this field can be 
communicated in lay man’s language, it can reach the public in highly appreciated and 
acceptable manner. Further, considering the recent reports of rampant Vitamin D 
deficiency, especially among the urban population, the Committee recommends that 
ICMR shall consider commissioning a study on the deficiencies of Vitamin D and 
countermeasures. The Committee also favours a study on the impact of packaged food, 
including on the mechanisms that are in place to assess and regulate the quantity of 
preservatives used by commercial manufacturers. 
(Para 4.10.31) 
 
Treatment Procedure, Vaccine and Medical Devices Developed By ICMR 
 
84. On the information regarding details of vaccines at various stages of 
development/approval by ICMR, the Committee feels that the information is scattered 
across press releases and annual reports, leading to difficulty in keeping track. 
Accordingly, the Committee recommends a dedicated page on the ICMR official 
website, clearly indicating the vaccine name, target disease, development stage, and 
expected timeline for each stage. 
(Para 4.11.8) 
 
85.  The Committee understands that ICMR has no regulatory role in the approval of 
medical devices. MedTech MITRA, an ICMR-CDSCO initiative with guidance from 

157 
 
Niti Aayog, supports MedTech innovators including those of medical devices. Further, 
ICMR’s Indian Clinical Trial and Education Network (INTENT) and Centre for 
Advanced research facilitate regulation compliant of medical devices. Also, ICMR 
facilitates the clinical validation of such devices already approved by Atomic Energy 
Regulatory Board (AERB) and Central Drugs Standard Control 
Organisation (CDSCO), resulting in regulatory overlap creating an uneven protocol for 
development of medical devices. The Committee observes that the current medical 
device registration process involves multiple organizations and ICMR has no role after 
AERB approval. Hence, the Committee strongly recommends a more simplified, single-
window system which would reduce timelines and improve transparency. 
 
(Para 4.11.9) 
 
86 The Committee observes that ICMR, in collaboration with Institute of Plasma 
Research (IPR), has developed and validated an indigenous AI-based tool (DeepCXR) 
for automated interpretation of chest X-rays.The Committee recommends that ICMR 
make such indigenous technology available throughout the country free of cost which 
would be a step towards accessible and affordable healthcare. Even while making 
progress towards developing indigenous technology, the Committee opines that our 
country still relies heavily on imported medical technologies and equipment such as 
MRI, CT scans, PET-CT, and LINAC systems, which are procured from international 
companies like Philips and Siemens. ICMR has acquiesces that certain component such 
as the X-ray tube (a part of the X-ray source) and the detector, which are integral to the 
handheld X-ray system developed by them were imported substantiates this fact. If the 
core component like, X-ray tube, HV tank, etc is being imported ICMR should refrain 
from claiming it as their own technology. The better way for ICMR would be to engage 
itself in depth research for indigenous production, design, and development of medical 
devices. The medical devices developed viz the portable x-ray should be completely 
indigenous including X-ray tube, HV tank. Medical innovation and products should not 
be an assembly of components to be cited as ‘Made in India’. In this context, the 
Committee recommends that ICMR should set up a benchmark for ‘Made in India’ 
initiatives and derive a protocol wherein ‘Atmanirbhar Bharat’ is encompassed on a 
true scale. In case ICMR has to go for compilation of medical devices, the protocol 
should be clearly highlighted on the dashboard. Having said that the Committee also 
recommends that ICMR should make maximum efforts to develop some mechanism to 
manufacture these machines in our medical device parks with 100 % percentage of 
indigenous value addition which is essential for regaining public trust in the field of AI. 
 
 (Para 4.11.10) 
ICMR and Young Researchers 
 
87. The Committee notes with appreciation the measures adopted by ICMR to 
promote health research among the youth of the country like targeted support for 
Young Researchers for early-career biomedical researchers across medical colleges and 
research institutions, Fellowships & International Exposure to support young scientists 
and researchers for short-term training abroad and participation in international 
conferences. The Committee has been appraised that ICMR’s Encouraging Research 
Culture Among Students focusing on undergraduate, postgraduate, and doctoral 
students are three initiatives the Committee has been appraised of. The Committee 
observes that under the HRD Scheme, the DHR places special focus on the youth 
pursuing undergraduate/postgraduate medicine and Ph.D. programmes, as well as 
young faculty and women scientists with career breaks to build a strong pipeline of 
biomedical researchers comparable to global standards. The Committee appreciates 
that a new ICMR–AcSIR PhD Programme has been launched to strengthen India’s 

158 
 
clinical research ecosystem and foster indigenous innovation in medical devices and 
therapeutics. Efforts are also on to address gaps in structured mentorship. The 
Committee recommends that in its action taken note should furnish the details of 
outcome of efforts made so far by ICMR. 
(Para 4.12.6) 
 
88. To attract and engage the new generation to research is a critical initiative to 
ensure a continuum in Health Research. The Committee feels that it is important to 
promote students and researchers at all levels - from popularising bio sciences at higher 
secondary levels to handholding young individual and well as institutional researchers. 
Mentoring, especially at higher levels, is a key factor in producing skilled researchers 
and has to be given high priority by ICMR. The Committee wants DHR to devise 
appropriate initiatives to invite senior researchers and clinicians from both public as 
well as private spheres to mentoring and aim at forming a national mentorship grid. 
Targeted scholarships and small grants for researchers could be considered to earmark 
funds for the facilitation programme. The Committee also recommends internships at 
ICMR Institutes for students and young researchers can offer an opportunity to them 
to be part of real projects, while the Institutes also benefit.  
(Para 4.12.7) 
 
 89. The Committee also holds the firm view that assessment of outcomes based on 
the Publication-Equivalent(PE)Metric, allowing beyond short-term training 
opportunities abroad supported by fellowships, targeted outreach campaigns for 
underserved regions and institutions alongwith seed funding for medical colleges in 
underserved areas as well as effective feedback loop for review of the promotion 
programmes can be of immense help in this direction. 
(Para 4.12.8) 
World Trends in Health Research and ICMR 
90. The Committee takes into cognisance the latest trends in health research across 
the globe, as submitted by DHR, and how DHR and ICMR are pitching in with their 
initiatives in these cutting-edge areas. It is observed that ICMR is validating AI-based 
tools for TB chest X-ray interpretation, diabetic retinopathy screening, and digital 
pathology, the Committee recommends that ICMR must make such indigenous 
technology available throughout the country which would ensure certainly a step 
towards accessible and affordable healthcare. The Committee also notes the major on-
going research projects DHR is engaged with international entities like WHO or private 
sector partners for collaborative research. In this regard, the Committee is pleased to 
note these ongoing research projects that are implemented under formal international 
cooperation agreements in priority areas of public health and biomedical research. 
 (Para 4.13.4) 
91. The Committee feels that one of the areas DHR should prioritise is building 
capacity in AI ethics and related guidelines. International best practices may be 
factored in while doing this. One of the possible ultimate outcomes of this would be 
garnering trust of the world in our health innovations. Further, the Committee also 
favours climate-health research initiatives in collaboration with global partners on One 
Health, zoonosis, etc. Such steps would help us to be better prepared for outbreaks and 
position DHR as a torchbearer of resilient health research.  
(Para 4.13.5) 
 
92. The Committee appreciates the importance to involve and encourage to get 
young health researchers to foray into global trends of frontier fields through 

159 
 
fellowships and other appropriate supporting mechanisms. The Committee believes 
that cultivating efficient public-private partnerships by incentivising the industry for 
pandemic preparedness technologies, such as co-developing affordable diagnostics and 
vaccines would be a prudent idea to equip the nation with the ability to deploy rapid 
countermeasures.  
(Para 4.13.6) 
Strategic Plan of DHR (2024–29) 
 
93. The Committee notes that DHR has formulated a comprehensive Strategic Plan 
2024-29 aligned with the vision of Viksit Bharat @ 2047 under which the following 6 
focus areas have been identified – 
 
(vi) Promotion of Indigenous, Affordable and Globally Competitive Health 
Technologies; 
(vii) Accelerated Solutions for Difficult and Resistant Health Challenges; 
(viii) Advancing Digital Health research and Infrastructure; 
(ix) Translating Research Evidence into actionable Public Health Interventions; 
(v) Strengthened Surveillance Systems and Accelerated Medical 
Countermeasures; and 
(vi) Enhancing Global Standing of Indian Medical Research. 
(Para 5.1.14) 
 
94. The Committee finds theses identified core areas to be potential drivers of 
progress in Health sector that are aimed at self-reliance, solutions for health problems 
of future, accelerating innovation, improving deliver and positioning India as a global 
leader. It is going to be a herculean task that warrants meticulous planning and 
execution through different vehicles. Establishment of MIDAC, Med-Tech Mitra, CoEs, 
setting up of National Gold Standard Data Repository, expansion of HTA, 
augmentation of MRUs and MRHRUs, National Institute of Digital Health Research, 
Establishment of a National Institute of One Health, formation of Integrated Research 
& Diagnostic Laboratories, introduction of Patent Mitra inter alia are defining these 
drivers. The Committee strongly recommends in this direction the formulation of a 
well-defined and updated National Health Research Policy (NHRP) as guiding factor to 
embark upon this ambitious task.  The Committee also is of the firm opinion that 
distinguishing health research as one of the National priorities is another unavoidable 
step to embellish the pursuit.  
(Para 5.1.15) 
 
95. As emphasised by DHR, it is imperative to establish closer integration and 
coordination with programmes of the Department of Health & Family Welfare 
(DoH&FW) and other scientific departments to ensure that research findings are 
translated into deliverable products. Nevertheless, the prime driver of all the initiatives 
shall remain ICMR. The Committee would like to appreciate the effort to create a new 
Faculty of Medical Research (FMR) through AcSIR-ICMR collaboration. Another 
matter of high interest to the Committee is the role of DHR in India's goal of achieving 
Universal Health Coverage (UHC) through research-driven policies, which will be 
pivoted by ICMR and supported by HTAIn. 
(Para 5.1.16) 
96. The Committee has been made aware of the main hurdles in the path towards 
attainment of the Viksit Bharat Goals. As per DHR, there is a critical need to bridge the 
gap between biomedical research and clinical practice to enable effective integration of 
research insights into patient care. In this direction, DHR has started Medical PhD 
program focusing on developing clinician-scientists. The Committee notice that ICMR 

160 
 
has developed the Impact of Research and Innovation Scale (ICMR-IRIS), which 
provides a standardized framework for multidimensional impact assessment. This 
approach converts diverse research outputs into a single metric termed Publication-
Equivalent (PE). The Committee also note that to promote health research in the 
country, particularly among the youth, DHR has undertaken measures like Targeted 
Support for Young Researchers, Fellowships and International Exposure to young 
scientists, Encouraging Research Culture among Students by focusing on 
undergraduate, postgraduate, and doctoral students, etc. Further, the Committee is 
appreciative of the unique ‘First-in-the-World Challenge’, inspired by Chandrayan-3 
landing, making India the first country to do so. This initiative holds the promise of 
creating technologies, products, or solutions that are truly ‘first in the world.’ 
Accordingly, the Committee are extending its firm support to these endeavours and 
expects that DHR would be able to back up these initiatives with the requisite financial 
and infrastructural support. With regard to ‘First-in-the-World Challenge’, the 
Committee recommend that there should be prescribed criteria/parameter for selecting 
projects and allocating funds under the said category of research. 
 (Para 5.1.17) 
Research on Traditional and Integrative Medicine Practices  
97.  The Committee believes that millions rely on traditional systems like Ayurveda, 
herbal remedies, for primary care, especially for NCDs. The WHO Global Traditional 
Medicine Strategy 2025–2034, adopted by the Seventy-eighth World Health Assembly, 
aims to ensure universal access to safe and effective traditional, complementary, and 
integrative medicine (TCIM). The Committee finds the merits of traditional medicines 
include primarily cost-effectiveness, limited hospitalizations, reducing over-reliance on 
pharmaceuticals and the fact that traditional practices represent centuries of 
accumulated wisdom. However, a host of demerits overshadow the benefits. The 
concerns of the Committee related to Traditional and Integrative Medicine practices are 
that these practices are largely individualized, generates unreliable or insufficient data, 
research inadvertently leads to pseudoscientific claims, presence of heavy metals and 
pesticides in herbal products, inconsistent quality, methodological hurdles lowering 
investment returns, etc. All these factors combined with lack of training, governance, 
and patient awareness can erode trust in traditional healthcare systems. The Committee 
appreciates DHR initiative with Ministry of AYUSH in establishing Five AYUSH-ICMR 
Advanced Centres for Integrative Health Research (AI-ACHIR) at four AIIMS. The 
Committee recommends that both DHR and Ministry of Ayush adopt propitious steps to 
address the concerns expressed by the Committee and take pro-active steps for safe 
integration of traditional and alternative medicines into mainstream healthcare systems. 
(Para 5.2.6.) 
AI in Health 
98. The Committee is seized of the importance of Artificial Intelligence in health 
research and notes that DHR has plans to integrate emerging technologies like AI, 
genomics, and big data into its research framework. AI is being integrated into various 
diagnostic processes, particularly in medical imaging (radiology, pathology), to enhance 
speed, accuracy, and efficiency. Government initiatives like the Ayushman Bharat 
Digital Mission (ABDM) and the National Health Mission are creating the digital 
infrastructure and regulatory frameworks to support the integration of AI. ICMR has 
also released ethical guidelines for AI use in healthcare.  
(Para 5.3.15) 

161 
 
 
99. The Committee understand that AI-enabled healthcare technologies such as 
clinical decision-support systems, digital diagnostics, digital screening and surveillance 
tools, disease prediction models, population surveillance analytics and real-world 
evidence platforms are increasingly essential for improving efficiency of national health 
programmes and early disease detection and optimisation of healthcare expenditure. 
(Para 5.3.16) 
 
100. The Committee takes note of the remarkable steps by DHR that of are (i) 
creation of a national-grade data centre with AI/ML and cybersecurity infrastructure 
across ICMR institutes through PM-ABHIM, (ii) establishment of ICMR–National 
Institute for Research in Digital Health and Data Science (ICMR-NIRDHDS) mandated 
with research in development, evaluation and implementation of high impact and 
affordable digital and AI based health solutions and (iii) ICMR’s NAKSHATRA, high-
performance computing cluster for virological research is intended to enable faster 
analysis of complex genomic and bioinformatics data and support more efficient 
outbreak response and preparedness. The Committee recommends DHR to choke out 
strategy to safely amalgamate AI into a sensitive domain like health research and to 
overcome the hurdles of privacy of digital date and safe use of digital 
platforms/technologies/tools.  
(Para 5.3.17) 
 
101. The Committee notes that DHR understandably expects that the Ministry of 
Finance may support a structured expansion of budgetary allocations specifically 
earmarked for digital and AI-enabled health research. The Committee considers the 
importance of integrating AI into the field of health research and related areas and the 
humongous benefits it can bring to the vast population of our country. The Committee 
in this regard urges the Ministry of Finance to accords high priority in allocating 
adequate funds to DHR keeping into consideration proper outcome parameter that 
generally DHR hinges upon in the field of health research. The Committee would also 
like to recommend a certain amount of budget should be kept aside solely for safety and 
protecting of health data keeping into consideration the vast amount of data available 
in the health sector. 
 (Para 5.3.18)
